## I At the Army School / I 在陆军学校

### I / 一

===Original===
I have said that Spring arrived late in 1916, and that up in the trenches opposite Mametz it seemed as though Winter would last forever. I also stated that *as for me, I had more or less made up my mind to die* because *in the circumstances there didn’t seem anything else to be done*. Well, we came back to Morlancourt after Easter, and on the same evening a message from the Orderly Room instructed me to proceed to the Fourth Army School next morning for a month’s refresher-course. Perhaps Colonel Kinjack had heard that I’d been looking for trouble. Anyhow, my personal grievance against the Germans was interrupted for at least four weeks, and a motorbus carried me away from all possibility of dying a murky death in the mine-craters.
Barton saw me off at the crossroads in the middle of the village. It was a fine day and he had recovered his good spirits. “Lucky Kangaroo﻿—to be hopping away for a holiday!” he exclaimed, as I climbed into the elderly bus. My servant Flook hoisted up my bulging valise, wiped his red face with his sleeve, and followed me to the roof. “Mind and keep Mr. Sherston well polished up and punctual on parade, Flook!” said Barton. Flook grinned; and away we went. Looking back, I saw Barton’s good-natured face, with the early sun shining on his glasses.
There were several of us on board (each Battalion in our Brigade was sending two officers) and we must have stopped at the next village to pick up a few more. But memory tries to misinform me that Flook and I were alone on that omnibus, with a fresh breeze in our faces and our minds “making a separate peace” with the late April landscape. With sober satisfaction I watched a train moving out of a station with rumble and clank of wheels while we waited at the crossing gates. Children in a village street surprised me: I saw a little one fall, to be gathered, dusted, cuffed and cherished by its mother. Up in the line one somehow lost touch with such humanities.
The War was abundantly visible in supply-convoys, artillery horse-lines, in the dirty white tents of a Red Cross camp, or in troops going placidly to their billets. But everyone seemed to be off duty; spring had arrived and the fruit trees were in blossom; breezes ruffled the reedy pools and creeks along the Somme, and here and there a peaceful fisherman forgot that he was a soldier on active service. I had been in close contact with trench warfare, and here was a demonstration of its contrast with cosy civilian comfort. One has to find things out as one goes along, I thought; and I was wholeheartedly grateful for the green grass and a miller’s wagon with four horses, and the spire of Amiens Cathedral rising above the congregated roofs of an undamaged city.

===Chinese===
我说过，1916 年的春天来得很迟；而在马梅斯对面的战壕里，仿佛冬天要一直延续到永远。我也说过，*至于我，已多少打定了死的主意*，因为*在那种境况下，似乎再没有别的办法可想了*。嗯，复活节后我们回到莫朗库尔，当天傍晚营部勤务室便传来一道命令，令我次日前往第四集团军学校，参加为期一个月的复训班。说不定金杰克上校已听说我一直在自找麻烦。无论如何，我对德国人那一桩私怨至少被打断了四个星期，一辆长途汽车把我载走，使我彻底告别了在弹坑里稀里糊涂送命的可能。
巴顿在村子中央的十字路口为我送行。那是个好天，他的好兴致也恢复了过来。「好福气啊，袋鼠——一蹦一蹦地奔着度假去喽！」我正爬上那辆老客车，他这样嚷道。我的勤务兵弗卢克把我那鼓鼓囊囊的旅行袋扛了上去，用袖子抹了抹涨红的脸，跟着我爬上了车顶。「弗卢克，把舍尔斯顿先生伺候得精神点，操练要准点！」巴顿叮嘱道。弗卢克咧嘴一笑；车便开了。我回头望去，看见巴顿那张和善的脸，朝阳正照在他的眼镜上。
车上还有几个人（我们旅每个营都派两名军官），到下一个村子时想必又接了几个。可记忆却偏要骗我说，那辆公共汽车上只我和弗卢克两人，迎面是清新的微风，我们的心正与四月下旬的乡野风景「单独媾和」。车停在道口栅栏前等候时，我怀着一种清醒的满足，看着一列火车隆隆铿铿地驶出车站。村里街上的孩子们让我看了新奇：我瞧见一个小的摔倒在地，被母亲一把抱起，掸掸土，轻轻拍打两下，又疼爱起来。在前线上，人不知不觉就和这种人情味断了来往。
这场战争处处可见——辎重车队、炮兵马列、红十字会营地那些脏兮兮的白帐篷，或是安然走向驻地的队伍。可人人都仿佛下了班；春天已到，果树正在开花；微风拂过索姆河沿岸芦苇丛生的池塘与溪汊，这儿那儿有个安闲的垂钓者，竟忘了自己是个现役军人。我此前一直与堑壕战贴身相伴，眼前这一幕正演示着它与市民家居那份温馨适意的反差。我想，凡事都得一边走一边慢慢明白；而我由衷地感激那青青的草地、一辆四匹马拉的磨坊大车，以及亚眠大教堂的尖顶——它从未受损的城市一片攒聚的屋顶之上冉冉升起。

===Original===
The Fourth Army School was at Flixécourt, a clean little town exactly halfway between Amiens and Abbeville. Between Flixécourt and the War (which for my locally experienced mind meant the Fricourt trenches) there were more than thirty English miles. Mentally, the distance became immeasurable during my first days at the School. Parades and lectures were all in the day’s work, but they failed to convince me of their affinity with our long days and nights in the Front Line. For instance, although I was closely acquainted with the mine-craters in the Fricourt sector, I would have welcomed a few practical hints on how to patrol those Godforsaken cavities. But the Army School instructors were all in favour of Open Warfare, which was sure to come soon, they said. They had learnt all about it in peacetime; it was essential that we should be taught to “think in terms of mobility.” So we solved tactical schemes in which the enemy was reported to have occupied some village several miles away, and with pencil and paper made arrangements for unflurried defence or blank-cartridged skirmishing in a land of field-day make-believe.
Sometimes a renowned big-game hunter gave us demonstrations of the art of sniping. He was genial and enthusiastic; but I was no good at rifle-shooting, and as far as I was concerned he would have been more profitably employed in reducing the numerical strength of the enemy. He was an expert on loopholes and telescopic-sights; but telescopic-sights were a luxury seldom enjoyed by an infantry battalion in the trenches.
The Commandant of the School was a tremendous worker and everyone liked him. His motto was “always do your utmost,” but I dare say that if he had been asked his private opinion he would have admitted that the School was in reality only a holiday for officers and N.C.O.s who needed a rest. It certainly seemed so to me when I awoke on the first morning and became conscious of my clean little room with its tiled floor and shuttered windows. I knew that the morning was fine; voices passed outside; sparrows chirped and starlings whistled; the bell in the church tower tolled and a clock struck the quarters. Flook entered with my Sam Brown belt and a jug of hot water. He remarked that we’d come to the right place, for once, and regretted that we weren’t there for the duration. Wiping my face after a satisfactory shave, I stared out of the window; on the other side of the street a blossoming apple-tree leant over an old garden wall, and I could see the friendly red roof of a dovecot. It was a luxury to be alone, with plenty of space for my portable property. There was a small table on which I could arrange my few books. Hardy’s *Far from the Madding Crowd* was one of them. Also Lamb’s *Essays* and *Mr. Sponge’s Sporting Tour*. Books about England were all that I wanted. I decided to do plenty of solid reading at the Army School.
Near by was the Mess Room where fourteen of us had our meals. A jolly-faced Captain from the Ulster Division had undertaken the office of Mess President and everyone was talkative and friendly. With half an hour to spare after breakfast, I strolled up the hill and smoked my pipe under a quick-set hedge. Loosening my belt, I looked at a chestnut tree in full leaf and listened to the perfect performance of a nightingale. Such things seemed miraculous after the desolation of the trenches. Never before had I been so intensely aware of what it meant to be young and healthy in fine weather at the outset of summer. The untroubled notes of the nightingale made the Army School seem like some fortunate colony which was, for the sake of appearances, pretending to assist the struggle from afar. It feels as if it’s a place where I might get a chance to call my soul my own, I thought, as I went down the hill to my first parade. If only they don’t chivvy us about too much, I added.﻿ ﻿… It was not unlike the first day of a public school term, and my form-master (we were divided into classes of twenty-eight) was a youngish Major in the Oxford and Bucks Light Infantry. He was an even tempered man, pleasant to obey and specially likeable through a certain shyness of manner. I cannot remember that any of us caused him any annoyance, though he more than once asked me to try and be less absentminded. Later in the year he was commanding a battalion, and I don’t doubt that he did it excellently.

===Chinese===
第四集团军学校设在弗利克斯库尔，亚眠与阿布维尔正中间一座干净的小城。从弗利克斯库尔到这场战争（在我这深谙此地的人心里，它意味着弗里库尔的战壕），中间足有三十多英里。可在学校头几天，这段距离在心理上却变得无法丈量。出操、听课都属日常例行，可它们总叫我相信不了这跟前线的漫长日夜有何亲缘。比方说，弗里库尔防区那些弹坑我已了如指掌，倒真希望能讨几条怎样巡逻那些被上帝遗弃的窟窿的实用窍门。可陆军学校的教官们满口都是「运动战」，说这很快就要来临。这些他们在和平年代就学了个通透；至关重要的是，我们要受教「以机动性来思考」。于是我们做种种战术想定——据报敌军已占据数英里外的某村——而后用纸笔从容部署防御，或在演习的假想天地里来一场空包弹的 skirmish。
有时一位赫赫有名的大型猎物猎手来给我们示范狙击术。他为人热忱、兴致勃勃；可惜我对步枪射击一窍不通，就我而言，他若去削减敌军兵力倒更有用些。他是射击孔与瞄准镜的行家；可在战壕里的步兵营中，瞄准镜是少有的奢侈品。
学校的校长是个极其勤勉的人，人人都喜欢他。他的座右铭是「永远竭尽全力」，不过我敢说，若有人私下问他，他大概会承认这学校说到底不过是给需要休整的军官与军士们放个假。头一个早晨醒来，我觉察到自己那间干干净净的小屋——铺砖的地面、带百叶窗的窗户——情形确也如此。我知道外头是个好天；人声从窗外经过；麻雀啁啾，椋鸟鸣啭；教堂塔楼的钟悠悠地敲响，时钟报着刻数。弗卢克捧着我的萨姆·布朗武装带和一壶热水进来。他说这回我们可算来对了地方，又遗憾不能在这儿待到战争结束。一次舒坦的刮脸之后，我抹着脸，凝神望向窗外；街对面一棵开花的苹果树从一堵旧花园墙上探过身来，我还能望见一座鸽舍那和气的红屋顶。独处一室是种奢侈，宽敞得足够安置我那点随身家当。屋里有张小桌，可供我把所剩无几的几本书摆开。哈代的《远离尘嚣》是其中之一。还有兰姆的《伊利亚随笔》和《斯庞杰先生的游猎之行》。写英格兰的书，正是我想要的全部。我决计在陆军学校里好好做些扎实的阅读。
附近就是食堂，我们十四人在那里用饭。一位来自阿尔斯特师、生了一副快活面孔的上尉担任了食堂长，人人都健谈而友善。早饭后天还剩半个钟头，我信步爬上山岗，在一道绿篱荫下抽着烟斗。我松开腰带，望着枝叶满冠的栗子树，听一只夜莺唱得无懈可击。战壕的荒凉之后，这一切恍若奇迹。在此之前，我从未如此深切地体会到：在初夏的好天气里年轻而健康，意味着什么。夜莺那无忧无虑的啼啭，使陆军学校像极了某个幸运的殖民地，不过为了做做样子，才远远地装作在助这场战争一臂之力。这地方倒像能让我有机会把灵魂收归己有，我下山去赶头一次操练时这样想。只要他们别太拿我们折腾就好，我又添一句。……这一切倒很像是公学新学期开学头一天；我的级任老师（我们被分成二十八人一班）是牛津与白金汉轻步兵团一位年轻少校。他性情平和，让人乐意服从，又因带几分腼腆而格外讨人喜欢。我不记得我们当中有谁惹他生过气，尽管他不止一次请我努力少走点神。同年晚些时候，他便指挥一个营了，我毫不怀疑他干得极为出色。

===Original===
Every afternoon at half-past five the School assembled to listen to a lecture. Eyeing an audience of about 300 officers and N.C.O.s, I improved my knowledge of regimental badges, which seemed somehow to affect the personality of the wearer. A lion, a lamb, a dragon or an antelope, a crown, a harp, a tiger or a sphinx, these devices differentiated men in more ways than one. But the regimental names were probably the potent factor, and my meditations while waiting for the lecturer would lead me along pleasant associative lanes connected with the English counties﻿—the difference between Durham and Devon for instance. There was food for thought also in the fact of sitting between a Connaught Ranger and a Seaforth Highlander, though both were likely to have been born in Middlesex. Queer, too, was the whole scene in that schoolroom, containing as it did a splendid sample of the Fourth Army which began the Somme Battle a couple of months afterwards. It was one of those peaceful war-pictures which have vanished forever and are rarely recovered even in imaginative retrospect.
My woolgatherings were cut short when the lecturer cleared his throat; the human significance of the audience was obliterated then, and its outlook on life became restricted to destruction and defence. A gas expert from G.H.Q. would inform us that “gas was still in its infancy.” (Most of us were either dead or disabled before gas had had time to grow up.) An urbane Artillery General assured us that high explosive would be our best friend in future battles, and his ingratiating voice made us unmindful, for the moment, that explosives often arrived from the wrong direction. But the star turn in the schoolroom was a massive sandy-haired Highland Major whose subject was “The Spirit of the Bayonet.” Though at that time undecorated, he was afterwards awarded the D.S.O. for lecturing. He took as his text a few leading points from the *Manual of Bayonet Training*.

> To attack with the bayonet effectively requires Good Direction, Strength and Quickness, during a state of wild excitement and probably physical exhaustion. The bayonet is essentially an offensive weapon. In a bayonet assault all ranks go forward to kill or be killed, and only those who have developed skill and strength by constant training will be able to kill. The spirit of the bayonet must be inculcated into all ranks, so that they go forward with that aggressive determination and confidence of superiority born of continual practice, without which a bayonet assault will not be effective.

He spoke with homicidal eloquence, keeping the game alive with genial and well-judged jokes. He had a Sergeant to assist him. The Sergeant, a tall sinewy machine, had been trained to such a pitch of frightfulness that at a moment’s warning he could divest himself of all semblance of humanity. With rifle and bayonet he illustrated the Major’s ferocious aphorisms, including facial expression. When told to “put on the killing face,” he did so, combining it with an ultra-vindictive attitude. “To instil fear into the opponent” was one of the Major’s main maxims. Man, it seemed, had been created to jab the life out of Germans. To hear the Major talk, one might have thought that he did it himself every day before breakfast. His final words were: “Remember that every Boche you fellows kill is a point scored to our side; every Boche you kill brings victory one minute nearer and shortens the war by one minute. Kill them! Kill them! There’s only one good Boche, and that’s a dead one!”
Afterwards I went up the hill to my favourite sanctuary, a wood of hazels and beeches. The evening air smelt of wet mould and wet leaves; the trees were misty-green; the church bell was tolling in the town, and smoke rose from the roofs. Peace was there in the twilight of that prophetic foreign spring. But the lecturer’s voice still battered on my brain. “The bullet and the bayonet are brother and sister.” “If you don’t kill him, he’ll kill you.” “Stick him between the eyes, in the throat, in the chest.” “Don’t waste good steel. Six inches are enough. What’s the use of a foot of steel sticking out at the back of a man’s neck? Three inches will do for him; when he coughs, go and look for another.”

===Chinese===
每天下午五点半，全校集合听讲。我打量着约三百名军官与军士的听众，借机增进了对各团徽章的认识——这些徽章似乎在某种意义上影响着佩戴者的性情。一头狮子、一只羔羊、一条龙或一头羚羊、一顶王冠、一架竖琴、一只老虎或一尊斯芬克斯，这些图样以不止一种方式将人区别开来。但更具威力的，恐怕还是各团的名字；等待讲师开讲时，我的胡思乱想便顺着愉快的联想小径，溜到与英格兰各郡相关的话题上去——比如达勒姆与德文郡之间的差异。坐在一名康诺特 ranger 与一名海福德高地兵之间，也颇可玩味，虽说这两人八成都生在米德尔塞克斯。同样古怪的，是那间教室里的整个场面——其中正容纳着第四集团军一支精采的样本，两个月后，正是这支集团军发动了索姆河战役。这是那些已永远消逝的「宁静战争画面」之一，即便在最富想象力的回望里，也极少能再度拾起。
讲师清了清嗓子，我的白日梦便被打断；听众那作为人的意义旋即被抹去，其人生观也被压缩到只剩摧毁与防御。一位来自总司令部的毒气专家会告诉我们「毒气尚在襁褓之中」。（我们大多数人，还没等毒气长大成人，便已非死即残。）一位温文尔雅的炮兵将军向我们保证，在未来战役里高爆炸药将是我们最好的朋友，他讨好的语调使我们一时间忘了：炸药常常是从相反的方向飞来的。可教室里真正的明星，是一位身材魁伟、沙色头发的高地少校，他讲的题目是「刺刀之魂」。虽说当时他尚无勋章，后来却因讲课被授予优异服务勋章。他取材自《刺刀训练教范》中的若干要点，奉为讲义：

> 有效地以刺刀发起攻击，需要在高度亢奋、多半体力衰竭的状态下，具备良好的方向感、力量与速度。刺刀本质上是一种进攻性武器。在刺刀冲锋中，各阶官兵奋勇向前，非杀人即被杀；唯有经不断训练练出技艺与力量者，方能杀敌。刺刀之魂必须灌输给所有官兵，使其凭着不断练习所生的那股进取的决心与优越的自信而奋勇向前——若无此二者，刺刀冲锋便不会奏效。

他讲得杀气腾腾、雄辩滔滔，又用得体而恰如其分的笑话把场面撑得热络。他有一名中士从旁协助。那中士是个高大精壮的机器，被操练到一种骇人的境地，只消一声令下，便能卸下一切人性的伪装。他端着上了刺刀的步枪，把少校那些凶悍的格言一一演示出来，连面部表情也照做不误。一叫「换上杀人的脸」，他立马照办，并配上一副格外狠毒的姿态。「使敌人心生恐惧」是少校的主要箴言之一。看起来，人之被造，就是为了把德国人的命戳出来。听少校那口气，你会以为他每天早饭前都要亲手干上一回。他最后说道：「记住，弟兄们，你们每杀一个德国佬，就是给我方添上一分；每杀一个德国佬，就让胜利提早一分钟到来，让这场战争缩短一分钟。杀了他们！杀了他们！好德国佬只有一个，那就是死了的德国佬！」
过后我爬上山岗，到我钟爱的避难所——一片榛树与山毛榉的小树林——里去。傍晚的空气里有湿霉与湿叶的气味；树木是一种朦胧的绿；镇上的教堂钟声悠悠地敲响，屋顶上升起炊烟。在那预言般的外国春日的薄暮里，那里确有安宁。可讲师的声音仍在我脑中乱撞。「子弹与刺刀是兄妹。」「你不杀他，他就杀你。」「戳他两眼之间、喉咙、胸口。」「别糟蹋好钢。六英寸就够。一英尺长的钢条从人后脖颈戳出来有什么用？三英寸就够他受的了；他一咳嗽，你就去找下一个。」

### II / 二

===Original===
Whatever my private feelings may have been after the Major’s lecture, the next morning saw me practising bayonet-fighting. It was all in the day’s work; short points, long points, parries, jabs, plus the always to be remembered importance of “a quick withdrawal.” Capering over the obstacles of the assault course and prodding sacks of straw was healthy exercise; the admirable sergeant-instructor was polite and unformidable, and as I didn’t want him to think me a dud officer, I did my best to become proficient. Obviously it would have been both futile and inexpedient to moralize about bayonet-fighting at an Army School.
There is a sense of recovered happiness in the glimpse I catch of myself coming out of my cottage door with a rifle slung on my shoulder. There was nothing wrong with life on those fine mornings when the air smelt so fresh and my body was young and vigorous, and I hurried down the white road, along the empty street, and up the hill to our training ground. I was like a boy going to early school, except that no bell was ringing, and instead of Thucydides or Virgil, I carried a gun. Forgetting, for the moment, that I was at the Front to be shot at, I could almost congratulate myself on having a holiday in France without paying for it.
I also remember how I went one afternoon to have a hot bath in the Jute Mill. The water was poured into a dyeing vat. Remembering that I had a bath may not be of much interest to anyone, but it was a good bath, and it is my own story that I am trying to tell, and as such it must be received; those who expect a universalization of the Great War must look for it elsewhere. Here they will only find an attempt to show its effect on a somewhat solitary-minded young man.
At that time I was comfortably aware that the British Expeditionary Force in France was a prosperous concern. I have already remarked that the officers and N.C.O.s at the School epitomized a resolute mass of undamaged material; equally impressive was the equine abundance which I observed one afternoon when we were on our way to a “demonstration” at the Army Bombing School. Hundreds of light and heavy draft horses were drawn up along a road for an inspection by the Commander-in-Chief (a bodily presence which the infantry mind could not easily imagine). The horses, attached to their appropriate vehicles and shining in their summer coats, looked a picture of sleekness and strength. They were of all sorts and sizes but their power and compactness was uniform. The horsehood of England was there with every buckle of its harness brightened. There weren’t many mules among them, for mules were mostly with the Artillery, and this was a slap-up Army Service Corps parade, obviously the climax of several weeks’ preparation. I wished that I could have spent the afternoon inspecting them; but I was only a second-lieutenant, and the bus carried me on to study explosions and smoke-clouds, and to hear a lecture about the tactical employment of the Mills Bomb.

===Chinese===
少校那场讲演之后，我心里究竟是何种滋味且不去说，第二天一早，我已在练刺刀了。那都是分内之事；短突、长突、格挡、猛刺，外加那条永远要牢记的「迅速拔出」。在突击训练场的障碍物间蹦跳腾挪，拿草袋当靶子戳，是项有益健康的运动；那位令人敬佩的教官中士客气而不可怕，我既不想被他当成无能的军官，便竭力把动作练得纯熟些。要在陆军学校里对刺刀术发一通道德说教，显然既无谓又不合时宜。
我瞥见自己肩挎步枪走出小屋门的那一幕，心里竟涌起一种失而复得的幸福感。在那些空气清新、身体年轻有力的好早晨，生活原也没甚么不好；我匆匆沿着白色的公路、穿过空荡的街市、爬上山岗奔向训练场。我像个赶早学的学童，只是没有钟声催唤，我手里拎着的不是修昔底德或维吉尔，而是一杆枪。一时间把「我上前线是来挨枪子儿的」这事忘了个干净，我几乎要庆贺自己不花一文钱便在法国度起了假。
我还记得某个下午我到黄麻厂去洗热水澡。水是灌进一只染缸里的。记得自己洗过一回澡，对旁人或许无甚趣味，可那确是一回好澡；而我要讲的，毕竟是我自己的故事，只能照此领受；指望在此看到对这场大战作一番宏大升华的人，得到别处去找。在这里，他们只会看到一种尝试——试着展示这场战争对一个心思略显孤僻的年轻人所生的影响。
那时我心安理得地意识到，在法国的英国远征军是一桩兴旺的事业。我已说过，学校里的军官与军士们把一大团完好无损的坚毅人马凝缩成了缩影；同样令人难忘的，是我在某个下午目睹的良马之盛——当时我们正前往陆军轰炸学校看一场「示范」。数百匹轻重挽马沿一条道路列队，等候总司令检阅（这种肉身存在，是步兵的头脑所难以想象的）。那些马各就各车，夏毛油亮，看上去便是一幅光滑与力量的图画。它们高矮各异、品种不一，但其壮健与紧凑却整齐划一。英格兰的马之族类俱在，每一根挽具上的扣环都擦得锃亮。其中骡子不多，因骡子多在炮兵里；这是一场阔绰的陆军勤务队检阅，显然是数周准备的高潮。我倒巴不得整个下午都能在那儿端详它们；可我不过是个少尉，汽车把我载去研究爆炸与烟云，又听了一堂关于米尔斯手榴弹战术运用的课。

===Original===
News of the Battalion came from the Quartermaster, to whom I had sent an account of my “cushy” existence. Dottrell wrote that things had been quiet up in the Line, but advised me to make the most of my rest-cure, adding that he’d always noticed that the further you got from the front-line the further you got from the War. In accordance with my instructions he was making good progress with the box of kippers (which Aunt Evelyn sent me twice a month); ditto the Devonshire cream, though some of it hadn’t stood the journey well. His letter put me in the right frame of mind for returning to tours of trenches, though I should be sorry to say goodbye to young Allgood, with whom I was spending most of my spare time.
Allgood was quiet, thoughtful, and fond of watching birds. We had been to the same public school, though there were nearly ten years between us. He told me that he hoped to be a historian, and I listened respectfully while he talked about the Romans in Early Britain, which was his favourite subject. It was easy to imagine him as an undergraduate at Cambridge; travelling in Germany during the Long Vacation and taking a good degree. But his degree had been postponed indefinitely. He said he’d always wanted to go to Germany, and there seemed nothing incongruous in the remark; for the moment I forgot that every German we killed was a point scored to our side. Allgood never grumbled about the war, for he was a gentle soul, willing to take his share in it, though obviously unsuited to homicide. But there was an expression of veiled melancholy on his face, as if he were inwardly warned that he would never see his home in Wiltshire again. A couple of months afterwards I saw his name in one of the long lists of killed, and it seemed to me that I had expected it.

===Chinese===
营里的消息是从军需官那里传来的，我曾给他寄去一封描述我「安逸」日子的信。多特雷尔回信说，前线一向平静，却又劝我把这疗养假用足，并添了一句：他一向发觉，离前线越远，离这场战争也就越远。他遵照我的嘱托，正稳步消灭那盒熏鲱鱼（伊芙琳姨母每月寄两次给我）；德文郡奶油亦然，尽管有些在路上走了样。他这封信，把我调教回了即将重返战壕轮值的合适心境，尽管要与年轻的奥尔古德道别，我是会难过的——我的业余时间大半都与他一道度过。
奥尔古德沉静、爱思考、喜欢看鸟。我们上过同一所公学，尽管两人相差近十岁。他说他希望日后做个历史学家，我便恭恭敬敬地听他谈早期不列颠的罗马人——那是他钟爱的话题。要想象他在剑桥做本科生、长假里去德国游历、拿个好学位，原是轻而易举的事。可他的学位已被无限期搁置。他说他一直想去德国，这话听来毫无不谐之处；一时间我竟忘了：我们每杀一个德国人，就是给我方添上一分。奥尔古德从不抱怨这场战争，他是个温顺的人，甘愿分担自己的一份，尽管显然不适宜杀人。可他脸上总带着一种隐约的忧郁，仿佛内心早有预感：自己再也回不了威尔特郡的家了。两个月后，我在一长串阵亡名单里看见了他的名字；而我竟觉得，自己原是料到了的。

===Original===
Our last day at the School was hot and cloudless. In the morning English and French Generals rolled up in their cars; there must have been about a hundred of them; it was not unlike an army of uniformed Uncles on Prize-giving Day. There were no prizes, naturally. But we did our best to show them how efficient we were, by running round the assault course in teams, stabbing the straw sacks. We also competed in putting up screw-pickets and barbed wire with rapidity and precision. Our exertions ended with a march past the Army Commander, and then we fell out to witness the explosion of two small mines. Earth and chalk heaved up at the blue sky, the ground vibrated, and there was a noise like a mad rainstorm, caused by the whizzing descent of clods and stones and the hiss of smaller particles. Finally, a fountain of dingy smoke arose and drifted away from the debris, and the Generals retired to have luncheon in the white château; and there, let us hope, they let their belts out a hole or two and allowed themselves a little relaxation from intellectual effort. Allgood said that he thought the French Generals looked much brainier than the British ones; but I told him that they must be cleverer than they looked, and anyhow they’d all got plenty of medal-ribbons.

===Chinese===
我们在学校的最后一天，天热而无云。上午，英法两军的将军们乘着小车鱼贯而至；足有一百来位；倒像是一支身着制服的叔叔大军，前来出席发奖日。自然，并无奖项可发。我们便竭力展示自己的高效——编成小组绕着突击场飞奔，猛戳那些草袋。我们还比赛快速而精准地架设螺旋桩与铁丝网。这一番劳累以在集团军司令面前分列式走过告终，随后我们解散，去观看两枚小型地雷的爆炸。泥土与白垩向蓝天涌起，大地颤抖，发出一阵如疯狂暴雨般的声响——那是土块与石子呼啸着落下、细碎颗粒嘶嘶飘落所致。最后，一股污浊的烟柱腾起，从废墟之旁飘散开去；将军们退入那座白色城堡用午餐；在那里，但愿他们把腰带放松了一两个扣眼，让自己从脑力劳顿中稍得喘息。奥尔古德说他觉得法国将军们看上去比英国的聪明得多；我却告诉他，他们一定比看上去更聪明，再说无论如何，他们胸前都挂满了一排排的勋章绶带。

## II The Raid / II 突袭

### I / 一

===Original===
I came back from the Army School at the end of a hot Saturday afternoon. The bus turned off the bumpy main road from Corbie and began to crawl down a steep winding lane. I looked, and there was Morlancourt in the hollow. On the whole I considered myself lucky to be returning to a place where I knew my way about. It was no use regretting the little room at Flixécourt where I had been able to sit alone every night, reading a good book and calling my soul my own.﻿ ﻿… Distant hills and hazy valleys were dazzled with sun-rays, and the glaring beams made a fiery mist in the foreground. It was jolly fine country, I thought. I had become quite fond of it, and the end-of-the-world along the horizon had some obscure hold over my mind which drew my eyes to it almost eagerly, for I could still think of trench warfare as an adventure. The horizon was quiet just now, as if the dragons which lived there were dozing.
The Battalion was out of the line, and I felt almost glad to be back as I walked up to our old Company Mess with Flook carrying my valise on his back. Flook and I were very good friends, and his vigilance for my personal comfort was such that I could more easily imagine him using his rifle in defence of my valise than against the Germans.
Nobody was in when I got to our billets, but the place had improved since I last saw it; the horsechestnut in front of the house was in flower and there were a few peonies and pink roses in the neglected little garden at the back.

===Chinese===
一个炎热的星期六下午将尽时，我从陆军学校回来了。汽车从科尔比那条颠簸的公路上拐下来，开始沿着一条陡峭蜿蜒的小径慢慢往下爬。我望去，莫朗库尔就在那片凹地里。总的算来，我倒庆幸自己能回到一个熟门熟路的地方。再去想弗利克斯库尔那间小屋——每夜独坐、读一本好书、把灵魂收归己有——已然无益。……远山与朦胧的山谷被阳光照得炫目，那灼灼的光束在前方化作一片炽热的雾霭。这乡野好不壮美，我想。我已对它生出相当的眷恋；而地平线上那片世界尽头，对我心头有种说不清的攫取之力，几乎引得我热切地望过去——毕竟，我仍能把堑壕战当作一场冒险。眼下地平线是安静的，仿佛住在那里的群龙正在打盹。
营已撤下火线休整，我朝我们连队那间旧食堂走去，弗卢克背着我的旅行袋跟在身后，心里竟有些欢喜。弗卢克和我交情甚笃，他对我个人安逸的护持之周到，使我更容易想象他端起步枪是为了捍卫我的旅行袋，而不是去打德国人。
我回到驻地时屋里没人，但这地方自上次见过之后已大有起色；屋前那棵马栗树开了花，屋后那荒疏的小园子里也长出了几株芍药和粉红的玫瑰。

===Original===
Dusk had fallen when I returned from a stroll in the fields; the candles were lit, there was a smell of cooking, and the servants were clattering tin plates in the sizzling kitchen. Durley, Birdie Mansfield, and young Ormand were sitting round the table, with a new officer who was meekly reading the newspaper which served as tablecloth. They all looked glum but my advent caused some pumped up cheeriness, and I was introduced to the newcomer whose name was Fewnings. (He wore spectacles and in private life had been a schoolmaster.) Not much was said until the end of the steak and onions; by then Mansfield had lowered the level of the whisky bottle by a couple of inches, while the rest of us drank lime-juice. Tinned peaches appeared, and I inquired where Barton was﻿—with an uneasy feeling that something might have happened to him. Ormand replied that the old man was dining at Battalion Headquarters. “And skiting to Kinjack about the Raid, I’ll bet,” added Mansfield, tipping some more whisky into his mug. “The Raid!” I exclaimed, suddenly excited, “I haven’t heard a word about it.” “Well, you’re the only human being in this Brigade who hasn’t heard about it.” (Mansfield’s remarks were emphasized by the usual epithets.) “But what about it? Was it a success?” “Holy Christ! Was it a success? The Kangaroo wants to know if it was a success!” He puffed out his plump cheeks and gazed at the others. “This goddamned Raid’s been a funny story for the last fortnight, and we’ve done everything except send word over to the Fritzes to say what time we’re coming; and now it’s fixed up for next Thursday, and Barton’s hoping to get a D.S.O. out of it for his executive ability. I wish he’d arrange to go and fetch his (something) D.S.O. for himself!” From this I deduced that poor Birdie was to be in charge of the Raiding Party, and I soon knew all there was to be known. Ormand, who had obviously heard more than enough lately, took himself off, vocally announcing that he was “Gilbert the filbert, the Nut with a *K*, the pride of Piccadilly, the blasé roué.”

===Chinese===
我从田野里闲逛回来时，暮色已沉；屋里点起了蜡烛，飘着饭菜香，勤务兵在嗞嗞作响的厨房里把洋铁盘子弄得叮当作响。德利、伯迪·曼斯菲尔德，还有小奥曼德围桌而坐，另有一位新来的军官正恭顺地读着那张权充桌布的旧报纸。众人都一脸阴郁，可我一露面，便引出几分勉强挤出来的热闹；我被引见给那位新来的，名叫弗宁斯。（他戴着眼镜，平民身份时是位教书先生。）牛排洋葱吃完之前，话都不多；彼时曼斯菲尔德已把威士忌瓶里的液面喝下去两三英寸，我们其余的人喝的则是青柠汁。端上糖水桃子时，我问巴顿哪里去了——心里隐隐不安，生怕他出了什么事。奥曼德答说老头子在营部用晚饭。「还在跟金杰克念叨突袭的事呢，我敢打赌。」曼斯菲尔德往自己的搪瓷缸里又倒了些威士忌，补上一句。「突袭！」我嚷起来，一下子来了劲，「我连一个字都没听说过。」「嗬，你是全旅唯一一个没听说过的人了。」（曼斯菲尔德的话里照例夹着些粗口作点缀。）「可到底怎么样？成功了没？」「我的老天爷！成功了没？袋鼠居然想知道成功了没！」他鼓起那张胖乎乎的腮帮子，朝众人翻翻眼。「这场该死的突袭，前两个礼拜一直是个笑话；我们什么都做了，就差没给德国佬捎个信儿告诉他们我们几点到；如今定在下周四，巴顿还指望着凭他的调度才能捞一枚优异服务勋章呢。我倒盼着他亲自去把自己那枚（脏字）勋章领回来！」由此我推断，可怜的伯迪要负责带队突袭，不久我便把该知道的全知道了。奥曼德显然最近听这些听得太多，一溜烟走了，嘴里唱着「我就是吉尔伯特那颗榛子，以 *K* 开头的那个坚果，皮卡迪利大街的骄傲，玩世不恭的花花公子」。

===Original===
Barton was still up at Headquarters when I went across the road to my billet. Flook had spread my “fleabag” on the tiled floor, and I had soon slipped into it and blown out my candle. Durley, on the other side of the room, was asleep in a few minutes, for he’d been out late on a working party the night before. I was now full of information about the Raid, and I could think of nothing else. My month at Flixécourt was already obliterated. While I was away I had almost forgotten about the Raid; but it seemed now that I’d always regarded it as my private property, for when it had begun to be a probability in April, Barton had said that I should be sure to take charge of it. My feeling was much the same as it would have been if I had owned a horse and then been told that someone else was to ride it in a race.
Six years before I had been ambitious of winning races because that had seemed a significant way of demonstrating my equality with my contemporaries. And now I wanted to make the World War serve a similar purpose, for if only I could get a Military Cross I should feel comparatively safe and confident. (At that time the Doctor was the only man in the Battalion who’d got one.) Trench warfare was mostly monotonous drudgery, and I preferred the exciting idea of crossing the mine-craters and getting into the German front-line. In my simple-minded way I had identified myself with that strip of No Man’s Land opposite Bois Français; and the mine-craters had always fascinated me, though I’d often feared that they’d be the death of me.
Mansfield had gloomily remarked that he’d something﻿—well go on the razzle if he got through Thursday night with his procreative powers unimpaired. Wondering why he had been selected for the job, I wished I could take his place. I knew that he had more commonsense ability than I had, but he was podgily built and had never been an expert at crawling among shell-holes in the dark. He and Ormand and Corporal O’Brien had done two patrols last week, but the bright moonlight had prevented them from properly inspecting the German wire. Birdie’s language about moonlight and snipers was a masterpiece, but he hadn’t a ghost of an idea whether we could get through the Boche wire. Nevertheless I felt that if I’d been there the patrolling would have been profitable, moon or no moon. I wouldn’t mind going up there and doing it now, I thought, for I was wide-awake and full of energy after my easy life at the Army School.﻿ ﻿… *Doing it now?* The line was quiet tonight. Now and again the tapping of a machine-gun. But the demented nightlife was going on all the time and the unsleeping strangeness of it struck my mind silent for a moment, as I visualized a wiring-party standing stock-still while a flare quivered and sank, silvering the bleached sandbags of the redoubt.
Warm and secure, I listened to the gentle whisper of the aspens outside the window, and the fear of death and the horror of mutilation took hold of my heart. Durley was muttering in his sleep, something rapid and incoherent, and then telling someone to get a move on; the war didn’t allow people many pleasant dreams. It was difficult to imagine old Julian killing a German, even with an anonymous bullet. I didn’t want to kill any Germans myself, but one had to kill people in self-defence. Revolver shooting wasn’t so bad, and as for bombs, you just chucked them and hoped for the best. Anyhow, I meant to ask Kinjack to let me go on the Raid. Supposing he *ordered* me to go on it? How should I feel about it then? No good thinking any more about it now. With some such ponderings as these I sighed and fell asleep.

===Chinese===
我穿过马路回自己的住处时，巴顿还在营部没回。弗卢克已把我的「跳蚤袋」铺在铺砖地面，我很快钻进去，吹熄了蜡烛。屋子另一头的德利几分钟便睡着了，他昨夜干劳役队干到很晚。我如今满脑子都是突袭的事，别的什么也想不了。在弗利克斯库尔那一个月已被抹得干干净净。离开的这段日子，我几乎把突袭忘到了脑后；可此刻却仿佛，我一向把它当作自己的私产——四月里这事刚有了眉目时，巴顿就说我一定能带队去。我的心情，与那种「自己有匹马，却被告知要让别人骑着去比赛」差不多。
六年前我曾渴望赢比赛，因为那似乎是证明我与同辈平起平坐的一条要紧路子。如今我想让这场世界大战也起到同样的作用，因为只要我能弄到一枚军功十字勋章，便会感到相对的安稳与自信。（那时全营只有医生有一枚。）堑壕战大多是枯燥乏味的苦差，我倒更倾心于穿越弹坑、闯入德军前沿那种刺激的念头。我以自己那点单纯的心思，把自己与布瓦弗朗赛对面那一狭长带无人区等同了起来；那些弹坑一直令我着迷，尽管我也常怕它们会是我的葬身之地。
曼斯菲尔德曾阴郁地说过，要是他熬过星期四夜里还能保住自己的生育能力，他就要出去痛痛快快胡闹一番。我纳闷为何挑中了他干这差事，巴不得能替了他。我知道他比我更有常情常识，可他身材矮胖，在弹坑间黑暗中匍匐从来不是行家。上周他与奥曼德和奥布赖恩下士出去巡逻过两次，可明亮的月光使他们无法好好查看德军的铁丝网。伯迪对月光和狙击手的那通咒骂堪称杰作，可他对我们能否穿过德国人的铁丝网，心里一点谱也没有。尽管如此，我仍觉得若换作我在场，无论有没有月光，巡逻定会有所斩获。眼下我倒愿意上去干一遭，我想，毕竟陆军学校那段安逸日子过后，我精神十足、精力充沛。……*此刻就去干？* 今晚战线安静。时不时一挺机枪哒哒几声。可那疯狂的夜生活无时无刻不在进行，它那不眠的诡异使我一时默然——我眼前浮现一支布线小分队，一动不动地站着，任照明弹颤动着升空又落下，把据点那些漂白的沙袋映得发银。
温暖而安全，我听着窗外白杨轻柔的低语，死亡的恐惧与伤残的战栗攫住了我的心。德利在梦中喃喃，含糊急促地不知说些什么，接着又催谁快些；这场战争不让人做几个好梦。要想象老朱利安去杀一个德国人，即便用一颗无主的子弹，也难。我自己并不想杀德国人，可人为自卫总得杀人。打手枪倒还好些，至于手榴弹，扔出去再听天由命就是。无论如何，我打算求金杰克让我参加这次突袭。倘若他*下令* 让我去呢？那时我又会作何感想？现在多想也无益。我就在诸如此类的盘算中叹了口气，沉沉睡去。

### II / 二

===Original===
Next morning I went to the other end of the village to have a chat with my friend the Quartermaster. Leaning against a bit of broken wall outside his billet, we exchanged a few observations about the larger aspects of the war and the possibilities of peace. Joe was pessimistic as ever, airing his customary criticisms of profiteers, politicians, and those whose military duties compelled them to remain at the Base and in other back areas. He said that the permanent staff at Fourth Army Headquarters now numbered anything up to four thousand. With a ribald metaphor he speculated on what they did with themselves all day. I said that some of them were busy at the Army School. Joe supposed there was no likelihood of their opening a rest-cure for Quartermasters.
When I asked his opinion about the Raid he looked serious, for he liked Mansfield and knew his value as an officer. “From all I hear, Kangar,” he said, “it’s a baddish place for a show of that kind, but you know the ground better than I do. My own opinion is that the Boches would have come across themselves before now if they’d thought it worth trying. But Brigade have got the idea of a raid hot and strong, and they’ve nothing to lose by it one way or the other, except a few of our men.” I asked if these raids weren’t a more or less new notion, and he told me that our Battalion had done several small ones up in Flanders during the first winter; Winchell, our late Colonel, had led one when he was still a company commander. The idea had been revived early this year, when some Canadian toughs had pulled off a fine effort, and since then such entertainments had become popular with the Staff. Our Second Battalion had done one, about a month ago, up at Cuinchy; their Quartermaster had sent Joe the details; five officers and sixty men went across, but casualties were numerous and no prisoners were brought back. He sighed and lit a cigarette. “It’s always the good lads who volunteer for these shows. One of the Transport men wanted to send his name in for this one; but I told him to think of his poor unfortunate wife, and we’re pushing him off on a transport-course to learn cold-shoeing.”

===Chinese===
第二天上午，我到村子另一头去找军需官朋友聊天。我们靠在他住处外头一截断墙上，就这场战争的大局与议和的可能交换了几句看法。乔伊一如既往地悲观，照例把那套对奸商、政客，以及那些因军务所系不得不留在后方基地和其他后方的家伙的批评端了出来。他说第四集团军司令部的常设参谋如今已多至四千之数。他用一句粗俗的比方揣度这些人成天都在干些什么。我说他们有些人正忙着办陆军学校呢。乔伊猜他们大概不会为军需官们也办个疗养班。
我问起他对突袭的看法，他神色凝重起来，因为他喜欢曼斯菲尔德，也深知这位军官的价值。「据我所闻，袋鼠啊，」他说，「这种事挑那种地段是个不妙的差事，不过那块地你比我熟。依我看，德国佬要是觉得值得一试，早自己扑过来了。可旅部对突袭这主意兴致正浓，他们横竖没什么损失——除掉我们几条人命。」我问这类突袭是不是多少算个新花样，他告诉我，第一个冬天我们营在弗兰德斯干过几次小规模的；我们已故的团长温切尔还是连长时就带队干过一回。今年早些时候这主意又时兴起来，当时几个加拿大的硬汉搞了漂亮的一票，从那以后这种「娱乐」便在参谋们中间流行开来。我们第二营大约一个月前在坎西干过一次；他们的军需官把详情寄给了乔伊：五名军官、六十名士兵过了去，可伤亡惨重，一个俘虏也没带回来。他叹了口气，点上一支烟。「去报名干这种事的，总是那些好后生。运输队有个弟兄想把自己名字报上去；可我叫他想想他那苦命的媳妇，我们正把他支去上一期运输培训班，学冷钉马蹄铁。」

===Original===
Prodding the ground with my stick, I stared at the Transport lines below us﻿—a few dirty white bell-tents and the limbers and wagons and picketed horses. I could see the horses’ tails switching and the men stooping to groom their legs. Bees hummed in the neglected little garden; red and grey roofs clustered round the square church tower; everything looked Sunday-like and contented with the fine weather. When I divulged my idea of asking Kinjack to let me go on the Raid, Joe remarked that he’d guessed as much, and advised me to keep quiet about it as there was still a chance that it might be washed out. Kinjack wasn’t keen about it and had talked pretty straight to the Brigade Major; he was never afraid of giving the brass-hats a bit of his mind. So I promised to say nothing till the last moment, and old Joe ended by reminding me that we’d all be over the top in a month or two. But I thought, as I walked away, how silly it would be if I got laid out by a stray bullet, or a rifle-grenade, or one of those clumsy “canisters” that came over in the evening dusk with a little trail of sparks behind them.

===Chinese===
我用手杖戳着地，望着我们下方的运输队队列——几顶脏兮兮的白色钟形帐篷，还有弹药前车、大车和拴着的马匹。我看得见马尾巴甩动，看得见弟兄们弯腰给马腿刷洗。荒疏的小园子里蜜蜂嗡嗡作响；红的灰的屋顶簇拥在方形的教堂钟楼四周；一切都透着星期日般的安闲，对这好天气心满意足。当我吐露想求金杰克让我参加突袭的念头时，乔伊说他早料到了，劝我对此守口如瓶，因为这差事说不定还会黄掉。金杰克对此并不热心，已跟旅参谋长把话挑得相当直白；他从不怕给那些「大檐帽」一点颜色看。我便答应到最后关头再开口，老乔伊临了又提醒我，一两个月后我们终归都要跃出掩体冲锋。可我走开时心想：要是我被一颗流弹、一发步枪榴弹，或是黄昏时分拖着一线火星摇摇晃晃飞来的那种笨拙「圆筒」给撂倒，那才叫冤呢。

===Original===
We went into the line again on Tuesday. For the first three days Barton’s Company was in reserve at 71. North, which was an assortment of dugouts and earth-covered shelters about a thousand yards behind the front-line. I never heard anyone ask the origin of its name, which for most of us had meant shivering boredom at regular intervals since January. Some map-making expert had christened it coldly, and it had unexpectedly failed to get itself called the Elephant and Castle or Hampton Court. Anyhow it was a safe and busy suburb of the front-line, for the dugouts were hidden by sloping ground and nicely tucked away under a steep bank. Shells dropped short or went well over; and as the days of aeroplane aggressiveness had not yet arrived, we could move about by daylight with moderate freedom. A little way down the road the Quartermaster-sergeant ruled the ration-dump, and every evening Dottrell arrived with the ration-limbers. There, too, was the dressing-station where Dick Tiltwood had died a couple of months ago; it seemed longer than that, I thought, as I passed it with my platoon and received a cheery greeting from our Medical Officer, who could always make one feel that Harley Street was still within reach.
The road which passed 71. North, had once led to Fricourt; now it skulked along to the British Front Line, wandered evilly across No Man’s Land, and then gave itself up to the Germans. In spite of this, the road had for me a queer daylight magic, especially in summer. Though grass patched and derelict, something of its humanity remained. I imagined every day rural life going along it in prewar weather, until this businesslike open air inferno made it an impossibility for a French farmer to jog into Fricourt in his hooded cart.
There was a single line railway on the other side of the road, but the only idea which it suggested to Barton was that if the war lasted a few more years we should be coming to the trenches every day by train like city men going to the office. He was due for leave next week and his mind was already half in England. The Raid wasn’t mentioned now, and there was little to be done about it except wait for Thursday night. Mansfield had become loquacious about his past life, as though he were making a general audit of his existence. I remember him talking about the hard times he’d had in Canada, and how he used to get a meal for twelve cents. In the meantime I made a few notes in my diary.

===Chinese===
星期二我们又上了火线。头三天，巴顿的连作为预备队驻在「71 号·北」——那是前沿身后约一千码的一群掩体与覆土棚屋。我从没听人问过这名字的来历，对我们大多数人而言，自一月起它便定期意味着冻得发抖的百无聊赖。某位制图专家冷冰冰地给它施了洗，它却莫名其妙地没能像别处那样被唤作「大象与城堡」或「汉普顿宫」。无论如何，它算是前沿一个安全而繁忙的郊区，因为那些掩体被斜坡遮着，舒舒服服地塞在一道陡岸之下。炮弹不是打短了，便是远远飞过头顶；而飞机逞凶的日子尚未到来，我们白天还能较为自由地走动。沿路下去一点便是军需军士坐镇的口粮堆，每到傍晚多特雷尔便押着口粮车赶到。包扎所也在那儿——两个月前迪克·蒂尔特伍德就死在那里；我带着排里弟兄走过时心想，这事好像远不止两个月那么久。我们的军医朝我愉快地招呼一声，他这人总能让人觉得哈雷街仍是指日可待的。
经过「71 号·北」的那条路，从前是通往弗里库尔的；如今它一路躲躲闪闪地蹭到英国火线，邪里邪气地穿过无人区，末了把自己交给了德国人。尽管如此，这条路在我眼里有种古怪的、白日里的魔力，尤其是在夏天。它虽已处处长草、破败不堪，却多少还留着点人情味。我想象战前的每个好天气里，都有乡间的日常生计沿它而行——直到这营生般露天进行的地狱，叫一个法国农夫再也不能赶着带篷的小车晃进弗里库尔。
路对面有一条单线铁路，可它在巴顿脑子里唤起的唯一念头是：要是战争再打上几年，我们以后每天都要坐火车来上战壕了，跟城里人上班一样。他下周就该休假了，心思已有一半回到了英格兰。突袭之事如今无人再提，除了干等星期四夜里，也没什么可做的。曼斯菲尔德变得絮絮叨叨地谈他的前半生，仿佛在对自己的人生作一番总清算。我记得他讲在加拿大那阵子怎么苦，怎样花十二分钱对付一顿饭。与此同时，我在日记里记下了几笔。

===Original===
“*Tuesday evening, 8:30. At Bécordel crossroads.* On a working party. A small bushy tree against a pale yellow sky; slate roofs gleaming in the half-light. A noise of carts coming along with rations. Occasional bang of our guns close to the village. The church tower, gloomy; only the front remains; more than half of it shot away and most of the church. In the foreground two broken barns with skeleton roofs. A quiet cool evening after a shower. Stars coming out. The R.E. stores are dumped around French soldier-cemetery. Voices of men in the dusk. Dull rattle of machine-guns on the left. Talking to a Northumberland Fusilier officer who drops aitches. Too dark to write.﻿ ﻿…
“*Wednesday, 6:15 p.m. On Crawley Ridge.* Ormand up here in the Redoubt with a few men. I relieve him while he goes down to get his dinner. Very still evening; sun rather hazy. Looking across to Fricourt; trench mortars bursting in the cemetery; dull white smoke slowly floats away over grey-green grass with buttercups and saffron weeds. Fricourt; a huddle of reddish roofs; skeleton village; church tower, almost demolished, a white patch against green of Fricourt wood (full of German batteries). North, up the hill, white seams and heapings of trenches dug in chalk. Sky full of lark songs. Sometimes you can count thirty slowly and hear no sound of a shot; then the muffled pop of a rifle or a slamming 5.9 or one of our 18 pounders. Then a burst of machine-gun fire. Westward the yellow sky with a web of filmy cloud half across the sun; the ridges with blurred outlines of trees. An aeroplane droning overhead. A thistle sprouting through the chalk on the parapet; a cockchafer sailing through the air. Down the hill, the Bray-Fricourt road, white and hard. A partridge flies away, calling. Lush grass and crops of nettles; a large black slug out for his evening walk (doing nearly a mile a month).”

===Chinese===
「*星期二晚，八点半。在贝科尔代尔十字路口。* 干劳役。一棵矮灌木映在淡黄色的天幕上；石板屋顶在半明半暗的光里闪光。运口粮的大车辚辚而来。村子附近时不时有我们火炮的轰响。教堂钟楼，阴沉沉的；只剩正面还留着；大半已被炮火削去，教堂本身亦然。前景里是两座塌了顶的破谷仓。阵雨过后一个清凉安静的傍晚。星星出来了。皇家工兵的器材堆在法国士兵墓地的四周。暮色里弟兄们说话的声音。左侧机枪闷闷地哒哒。与一位北umberland Fusilier 丢 h 的军官聊天——他把 h 音都吞了。天黑得没法写了。……
「*星期三，下午六点一刻。在克劳利山脊上。* 奥曼德带几个人在据点这儿。我替下他，让他下去吃晚饭。极静的傍晚；太阳略朦胧。眺望弗里库尔；战壕迫击炮在墓地里炸开；暗白的烟在灰绿色的草地上——开着毛茛和藏红花色的野草——上头慢慢飘散。弗里库尔；一堆发红的屋顶；一副骨架般的村庄；教堂钟楼几近摧毁，是弗里库尔树林（满布德军炮兵）绿色背景上的一块白斑。北边，上坡处，白垩里挖出的战壕的白色纹路与一堆堆白土。天空中满是云雀的歌。有时你能慢慢数到三十，听不见一声枪响；接着是一声步枪闷响，或一发 5.9 英寸重炮的轰击，或我们一发 18 磅炮。然后是一阵机枪扫射。西边，黄色的天空，一层薄云如蛛网半遮着太阳；山脊上树木的轮廓模模糊糊。一架飞机在头顶嗡嗡。胸墙上白垩里钻出一棵蓟草；一只金龟子在空中悠悠地飘。山下，布雷—弗里库尔公路，又白又硬。一只山鹑叫着飞走。丰茂的青草和大片荨麻；一条大黑蛞蝓出来散步过黄昏（一月爬不到一英里）。」

### III / 三

===Original===
At ten o’clock on Thursday night I was alone with Durley in the sackcloth smelling dugout at 71. North. Rain was falling steadily. Everything felt fateful and final. A solitary candle stood on the table in its own grease, and by its golden glimmer I had just written a farewell letter to Aunt Evelyn. I did not read it through, and I am glad I cannot do so now, for it was in the “happy warrior” style and my own fine feelings took precedence of hers. It was not humanly possible for me to wonder what Aunt Evelyn was doing while I wrote; to have done so would have cramped my style. But it is possible that she was calling her black Persian cat in from the dripping summer garden; when it scampered in from the darkness she would dry it carefully with a towel, whistling under her breath, while she did so, some indeterminate tune. Poor Aunt Evelyn was still comfortingly convinced that I was transport officer, though I had given up that job nearly three months ago. Having licked and fastened the flimsy envelope I handed it to Durley, with a premonition that it would be posted. Durley received it with appropriate gravity.
In the meantime Mansfield was making a final reconnaissance of the ground with Sergeant Miles and Corporal O’Brien, while Barton (unaware of my intentions) was administering a drop of whisky to the raiding party in the large dugout just along the road. It was time to be moving; so I took off my tunic, slipped my old raincoat on over my leather waistcoat, dumped my tin hat on my head, and picked up my nail-studded knobkerrie. Good old Durley wished me luck and economically blew out the candle. As we went along the road he remarked that it was lucky the night was dark and rainy.

===Chinese===
星期四夜里十点，我和德利单独待在「71 号·北」那间散发着粗麻布气味的掩体里。雨稳稳地下着。一切都透着一种命定与了结的味道。桌上孤零零一支蜡烛，立在自己化下的蜡油里；借着那点金黄的微光，我刚给伊芙琳姨母写完一封诀别信。我没有重读它，我也庆幸如今再不能重读，因为它通篇是「快乐的战士」那一调子，我自己的高雅情怀盖过了她的。我写信时，是不可能去想伊芙琳姨母此刻在做什么的；一想，便会坏了我的笔致。但她也许正在把她的黑波斯猫从滴水的夏日花园里唤进屋；猫从黑夜里一窜进来，她便会用毛巾仔仔细细把它擦干，一边轻声吹着口哨，吹一支含含糊糊的什么调子。可怜的伊芙琳姨母仍心安理得地相信我是运输官，尽管这差事我已卸下将近三个月了。我舔了舔那只薄信封，封上口，把它递给德利，心里有种它会被人寄出的预感。德利以相称的郑重收下了它。
与此同时，曼斯菲尔德正与迈尔斯中士和奥布赖恩下士对地形作最后的侦察；而巴顿（不知我心里打的是什么主意）正在沿路那间大掩体里，给突袭队员们分发几滴威士忌。是时候动身了；于是我脱下军上衣，在皮马甲外罩上那件旧雨衣，把钢盔往头上一扣，抄起那根钉了铁钉的圆头棍。好脾气的老德利祝我好运，又颇会过日子地吹熄了蜡烛。沿路走着时，他说今夜又黑又下雨，倒是桩幸事。

===Original===
Entering the other dugout I was slightly startled, for I had forgotten that the raiders were to have blacked faces (to avoid the danger of their mistaking one another for Germans). Exchanging boisterous jokes, they were putting the finishing touches to their makeup with bits of burnt cork. Showing the whites of their eyes and pretending not to recognize one another, those twenty-five shiny faced nigger minstrels might almost have been getting ready for a concert. Everyone seemed to expect the entertainment to be a roaring success. But there were no looking-glasses or banjos, and they were brandishing knobkerries, stuffing Mills bombs into their pockets and hatchets into their belts, and “Who’s for a Blighty one tonight?” was the stock joke (if such a well worn wish could be called a joke).
At 10:30 there was a sudden silence, and Barton told me to take the party up to Battalion Headquarters. It surprises me when I remember that I set off without having had a drink, but I have always disliked the flavour of whisky, and in those days the helpfulness of alcohol in human affairs was a fact which had not yet been brought home to me. The raiders had been given only a small quantity, but it was enough to hearten them as they sploshed up the communication trench. None of us could know how insignificant we were in the so-called “Great Adventure” which was sending up its uneasy flares along the Western Front. No doubt we thought ourselves something very special. But what we thought never mattered; nor does it matter what sort of an inflated fool I was when I blundered into Kinjack’s Headquarters at Maple Redoubt to report the presence of the raiders and ask whether I might go across with them. “Certainly not,” said the Colonel, “your job is to stop in our trench and count the men as they come back.” He spoke with emphasis and he was not a man who expected to have to say a thing twice. We stared at one another for a moment; some freak of my brain made me remember that in peacetime he had been an enthusiastic rose grower﻿—had won prizes with his roses, in fact; for he was a married man and had lived in a little house near the barracks.

===Chinese===
走进另一间掩体时我略吃了一惊，因为我忘了队员们要把脸涂黑（以免彼此误认作德国人）。他们正吵吵嚷嚷地开着玩笑，用烧焦的软木碎给彼此的妆做最后收尾。那二十五张油光发亮的黑脸「吟游歌手」翻着眼白、装作互不相识，简直像在筹备一场音乐会。人人似乎都盼着这场娱乐会大获成功。只是既无镜子也无班卓琴，他们挥舞着圆头棍，往口袋里塞米尔斯手榴弹，往腰带上别短斧，「今晚谁来一发能回国的伤？」是句现成的老笑话（倘若这么个说烂了的愿望也算得笑话的话）。
十点半，忽然一阵静下来，巴顿令我带队上营部。如今回想自己竟滴酒未沾便出发了，连我都觉诧异；可我一向不喜欢威士忌的味道，况且在那个年代，酒在人事中所能起的助力，于我还是尚未领教的一桩事实。给队员们的酒量不大，可也足够让他们哗啦哗啦蹚过交通壕时壮起胆来。我们都无从知道，自己在这场沿西线不安地升起一颗颗照明弹、号称「伟大冒险」的事业里，是何等微不足道。无疑我们把自己当成了颇为特别的角色。可我们怎么想从来无关紧要；也无妨我如今写下：当年那个冒冒失失闯进金杰克设在枫树据点的营部、去报告突袭队已到并请缨一同过线的，是怎样一个自命不凡的傻瓜。「绝对不行，」上校说，「你的差事就是待在我们的战壕里，数他们回来几个人。」他说得斩钉截铁，他不是个会想把话说第二遍的人。我们对视了片刻；我脑子里不知哪根筋作怪，竟想起他在和平年代是个热心的玫瑰栽培者——事实上还凭他的玫瑰拿过奖；因为他是有家室的人，住在营房附近一幢小房子里。

===Original===
My thought was nipped in the bud by his peremptory voice telling Major Robson, his second-in-command, to push off with the party. We were about 400 yards from the front-line, and Robson now led us across the open to a point in the support trench, from which a red electric torch winked to guide us. Then up a trench to the starting point, the men’s feet clumping and drumming on the duckboards. This noise, plus the clinking and drumming and creaking of weapons and equipment, suggested to my strained expectancy that the enemy would be well warned of our arrival. Mansfield and his two confederates now loomed squatly above us on the parapet; they had been laying a guiding line of lime across the craters. A gap had been cut in our wire, and it was believed that some sort of damage had been done to the German wire which had been strafed by trench mortars during the day.
The raiders were divided into four parties of five men; operation orders had optimistically assumed that the hostile trenches would be entered without difficulty; “A” party would go to the left, “B” party to the right, and so on and so forth. The object of the raid was to enter the enemy loop on the edge of the crater; to enter Kiel Trench at two points; to examine the portions of trench thus isolated, capture prisoners, bomb dugouts, and kill Germans. An “evacuating party” (seven men carrying two ten-foot ladders and a red flash lamp) followed the others. The ladders were considered important, as the German front trench was believed to be deep and therefore difficult to get out of in a hurry. There were two mine-craters a few yards from our parapet; these craters were about fifty yards in diameter and about fifty feet deep; their sides were steep and composed of thin soft soil; there was water at the bottom of them. Our men crossed by a narrow bridge of earth between the craters; the distance to the German wire was about sixty yards.

===Chinese===
我的念头被他那一声断喝掐断在萌芽里——他命令副手罗布森少校带队出发。我们距前沿约四百码，罗布森带我们走开阔地，前往支援壕中的一处，那里有一支红色电筒一明一灭地为我们指路。而后沿一条战壕上到出发点，弟兄们的脚在泥炭板上咚咚作响。这点动静，再加上武器装备的叮当、撞击与咯吱声，叫我那紧绷的期待不禁担心：敌人怕是要被我们的到来预警个十足。曼斯菲尔德和他那两个同伙此刻蹲伏在胸墙之上，黑乎乎一片；他们刚在弹坑间撒下一条石灰导引线。我们的铁丝网已剪开一个缺口，而据信德国人的铁丝网——白天受过战壕迫击炮的轰击——也多少受了些损毁。
突袭队员分成四组，每组五人；作战命令乐观地假定进入敌方战壕将畅通无阻；A 组往左，B 组往右，依此类推。突袭目标是：进入弹坑边缘的敌方环形工事；从两处进入「基尔壕」；搜查由此孤立的几段战壕，抓俘虏，炸掩体，杀德国人。一支「接应组」（七人，扛两架十英尺的梯子、一盏红色闪光灯）跟在后面。梯子被视为要紧之物，因为据信德军前沿战壕很深，临时难以爬出。离我们胸墙几码外有两个弹坑；这两个坑直径约五十码，深约五十英尺；坡面陡峭，由松散软土构成；坑底有水。我们的人从两坑之间一道狭窄的土堤上越过；至德军铁丝网的距离约六十码。

===Original===
It was now midnight. The five parties had vanished into the darkness on all fours. It was raining quietly and persistently. I sat on the parapet waiting for something to happen. Except for two men at a sentry post near by (they were now only spectators) there seemed to be no one about. “They’ll never keep that —﻿—﻿— inside the trench,” muttered the sentry to his mate and even at that tense moment I valued the compliment. Major Robson and the stretcher-bearers had been called away by a message. There must be some trouble further along, I thought, wondering what it could be, for I hadn’t heard a sound. Now and again I looked at my luminous watch. Five, ten, fifteen minutes passed in ominous silence. An occasional flare, never near our craters, revealed the streaming rain, blanched the tangles of wire that wound away into the gloom, and came to nothing, bringing down the night. Unable to remain inactive any longer, I crawled a little way out. As I went, a few shells began to drone across in their leisurely way. Our communication trench was being shelled. I joined the evacuating party; they were lying on the lip of the left-hand crater. A flare fizzed up, and I could see the rest of the men lying down, straight across the ridge, and was able to exchange a grimace with one of the black-faced ladder-carriers. Then some whizzbangs rushed over to our front trench; one or two fell on the craters; this made the obstinate silence of Kiel Trench more menacing. Soon afterwards one of the bayonet men came crawling rapidly back. I followed him to our trench where he whispered his message. “They can’t get through the second belt of wire; O’Brien says it’s a washout; they’re all going to throw a bomb and retire.”

===Chinese===
此刻已是午夜。五组人已四肢着地，消失在黑暗里。雨不声不响地下个不停。我坐在胸墙上，等着点什么发生。除掉附近一个哨位上的两个人（如今也只是看客），周围似乎空无一人。「他们才不会把那些（脏字）老老实实摁在战壕里呢。」哨兵对同伴咕哝；即便在那紧张时刻，我也颇为看重这句恭维。罗布森少校和担架兵已被一道传令叫走了。再过去一段准是出了什么岔子，我想，又纳闷能是什么，因为我一个声响也没听见。我不时看看自己那块夜光表。五分、十分、十五分，在不祥的静默里过去。偶尔升起一颗照明弹，从不在我们这些弹坑附近，照出如注的雨，把那一团团蜿蜒隐入幽暗的铁丝网映得惨白，旋即熄灭，把夜色重新拉下。再没法干等着了，我往外爬了一小段。爬着的时候，几发炮弹开始悠闲地嗡嗡飞过。我们的交通壕正在挨炮。我汇入接应组；他们趴在左侧弹坑的坑沿上。一颗照明弹嗤地升起，我看得见其余的人平趴着，沿山脊一线排开，还能与其中一个扛梯子的黑脸弟兄交换一个鬼脸。接着几发「嗖—砰」速射炮朝我们前沿飞来；一两发落在弹坑里；这使「基尔壕」那顽固的沉默愈发显得凶险。不久，一名带刺刀的弟兄迅速爬了回来。我跟着他回到我们的战壕，他低声传话。「第二道铁丝网穿不过去；奥布赖恩说全黄了；大伙打算一人扔一颗手榴弹就撤。」

===Original===
I suppose I ought to have tried to get the ladder-carriers in before the trouble started; but the idea didn’t strike me as I waited with bumping heart; and almost immediately the explosions began. A bomb burst in the water of the left-hand crater, sending up a phosphorescent spume. Then a concentration of angry flashes, thudding bangs, and cracking shots broke itself up in hubbub and scurry, groans and curses, and stampeding confusion. Stumbling figures loomed up from below, scrambling clumsily over the parapet; black faces and whites of eyes showed grotesque in the antagonistic shining of alarmed flares. Dodging to and fro, I counted fourteen men in; they all blundered away down the trench. I went out, found Mansfield badly hit, and left him with two others who soon got him in. Other wounded men were crawling back. Among them was a grey-haired lance-corporal, who had one of his feet almost blown off; I half carried him in and when he was sitting on the fire-step he said, “Thank God Almighty for this; I’ve been waiting eighteen months for it and now I can go home.” I told him we’d get him away on a stretcher soon, and then he muttered: “Mick O’Brien’s somewhere down in the craters.”
All this had been quick work and not at all what I’d expected. Things were slowing down now. The excitement was finished, and O’Brien was somewhere down in the craters. The bombing and rifle fire had slackened when I started out to look for him. I went mechanically, as though I were drowning myself in the darkness. This is no fun at all, was my only thought as I groped my way down the soft clogging side of the left-hand crater; no fun at all, for they were still chucking an occasional bomb and firing circumspectly. I could hear the reloading click of rifle bolts on the lip of the crater above me as I crawled along with mud clogged fingers, or crouched and held my breath painfully. Bullets hit the water and little showers of earth pattered down from the banks. I knew that nothing in my previous experience of patrolling had ever been so grim as this, and I lay quite still for a bit, miserably wondering whether my number was up; then I remembered that I was wearing my prewar raincoat; I could feel the pipe and tobacco-pouch in my pocket and somehow this made me less forlorn, though life seemed much further away than the low mumble of voices in our trench. A flare would have helped my searchings, but they had stopped sending them up; pawing the loose earth and dragging my legs after me, I worked my way round the crater. O’Brien wasn’t there, so I got across into the other one, which was even more precipitous and squashy. Down there I discovered him. Another man was crouching beside him, wounded in one arm and patiently waiting for help. O’Brien moaned when I touched him; he seemed to have been hit in several places. His companion whispered huskily, “Get a rope.” As I clambered heavily up the bank I noticed that it had stopped raining. Robson was peering out of the trench; he sent someone for a rope, urging him to be quick for already there was a faint beginning of daylight. With the rope, and a man to help, I got back to O’Brien, and we lifted him up the side of the crater.

===Chinese===
我想我本该在麻烦起来之前，先把扛梯子的弟兄收进来；可我心蹦蹦跳地等着，根本没想起这一层；紧接着爆炸就开始了。一颗手榴弹在左侧弹坑的水里炸开，激起一片磷光般的水沫。随即一片密集的怒闪、轰响与脆响，在喧嚣与奔突、呻吟与咒骂、惊奔的混乱里散开。跌跌撞撞的人影从底下冒出来，笨拙地翻过胸墙；黑脸与眼白在受惊照明弹敌意的光亮里显得怪诞。我东闪西躲，数进来了十四个人；他们统统踉踉跄跄沿战壕下去了。我出去，发现曼斯菲尔德受了重伤，便把他交给另外两个人，他们很快把他弄了进来。别的伤兵在往回爬。其中有个灰白头发的下士，一只脚几乎被炸飞了；我半搀半扛地把他弄进来，他往火力台阶上一坐，便说：「全能的上帝啊，谢天谢地；我等这一下等了十八个月，如今总算能回家了。」我说马上用担架把他抬走，他便喃喃道：「米克·奥布赖恩还在底下弹坑里哪。」
这一切都来得飞快，全然不是我想象的那般。事态如今慢了下来。那阵激动业已结束，而奥布赖恩还在底下某个弹坑里。手榴弹和步枪火力都已稀疏下来，我这才动身去找他。我机械地往前爬，仿佛是要把自己溺死在黑暗里。这一点也不好玩——这是我在左侧弹坑松软黏滞的坡壁上摸下去时唯一的念头；一点都不好玩，因为他们还不时扔颗手榴弹、谨慎地放几枪。我满是烂泥的手指匍匐前行，或痛苦地屏息蹲伏时，能听见头顶坑沿上步枪拉栓上膛的咔哒声。子弹打进水里，岸上簌簌落下小片小片的泥土。我明白，从前所有巡逻经历都不曾这般阴森；我静躺片刻，凄苦地想自己是不是劫数到了；接着我记起自己身上穿着战前那件雨衣；我摸得出口袋里的烟斗和烟荷包，这一点不知怎地使我少了些凄惶——尽管此刻生命仿佛比我们战壕里那低低的嘈杂人声还要遥远。一颗照明弹本可助我的搜寻一臂之力，可他们已不再打了；我用两手扒着松土、拖着两腿，绕弹坑一点点挪。奥布赖恩不在这儿，我便翻到另一个坑里去——那个坑更陡、更稀烂。我在底下找到了他。另一个人蹲在他身旁，一只胳膊受了伤，耐着性子等救援。我一碰奥布赖恩，他便呻吟一声；他似乎身中数处。他同伴哑着嗓子低声说：「找根绳子来。」我吃力地爬上岸去，留意到雨已停了。罗布森正从战壕里探身张望；他派了个人去找绳子，催他快点，因为天色已隐隐有了点光。带着绳子和一名帮手，我回到奥布赖恩身边，我们把他从弹坑壁上抬了上来。

===Original===
It was heavy work, for he was tall and powerfully built, and the soft earth gave way under our feet as we lugged and hoisted the limp shattered body. The Germans must have seen us in the half-light, but they had stopped firing; perhaps they felt sorry for us.
At last we lowered him over the parapet. A stretcher-bearer bent over him and then straightened himself, taking off his helmet with a gesture that vaguely surprised me by its reverent simplicity. O’Brien had been one of the best men in our Company. I looked down at him and then turned away; the face was grotesquely terrible, smeared with last night’s burnt cork, the forehead matted with a tangle of dark hair.
I had now accounted for everyone. Two killed and ten wounded was the only result of the raid. In the other Company sector the Germans had blown in one of our mine-galleries, and about thirty of the tunnelling company had been gassed or buried. Robson had been called there with the stretcher-bearers just as the raid began.
Nothing now remained for me to do except to see Kinjack on my way back. Entering his dugout I looked at him with less diffidence than I’d ever done before. He was sitting on his plank bed, wearing a brown woollen cap with a tuft on the top. His blonde face was haggard; the last few hours had been no fun for him either. This was a Kinjack I’d never met before, and it was the first time I had ever shared any human equality with him. He spoke kindly to me in his rough way, and in doing so made me very thankful that I had done what I could to tidy up the mess in No Man’s Land.

===Chinese===
这是桩沉重的活计，他身量高、骨架大，那松软的泥土在我们脚下直打滑，我们硬是把这具瘫软、支离的身子又拖又抬地弄了上来。半明半暗里德国人想必看见了，可他们已停止射击；也许是动了恻隐之心吧。
我们终于把他从胸墙上方放下去。一名担架兵俯身看了看，随即直起身，摘下钢盔——这一动作因其虔敬的质朴而令我隐隐诧异。奥布赖恩是我们连最出色的弟兄之一。我俯视了他一眼，便转过脸去；那张脸怪诞可怖，糊着昨夜涂的焦炭，前额上糊着一绺纠结的黑发。
到此刻我已把所有的人都算了回去。这次突袭的全部结果，是两人阵亡、十人负伤。在另一个连的防区，德国人炸塌了我们的一条坑道，约三十名坑道兵被毒气熏倒或活埋。突袭一开始，罗布森便被一道传令叫到那边带担架兵去了。
眼下我只剩一件事可做——回去时顺道去见金杰克。走进他的掩体，我看着他，比从前任何一次都不再怯场。他坐在木板床上，戴一顶棕色毛线帽，帽顶上有一撮毛。他那白皙的脸憔悴不堪；过去这几个钟头对他想来也并不好过。这是我从未见过的金杰克，也是我头一回与他共享了某种身为人的平等。他以他那粗犷的方式亲切地对我说了几句话，倒叫我深自庆幸：好歹我在无人区那片烂摊子里尽了点绵薄之力。

===Original===
Larks were shrilling in the drizzling sky as I went down to 71. North. I felt a wild exultation. Behind me were the horror and the darkness. Kinjack had thanked me. It was splendid to be still alive, I thought, as I strode down the hill, skirting shell-holes and jumping over communication trenches, for I wasn’t in a mood to bother about going along wet ditches. The landscape loomed around me, and the landscape was life, stretching away and away into freedom. Even the dreary little warren at 71. North seemed to await me with a welcome, and Flook was ready with some hot tea. Soon I was jabbering excitedly to Durley and old man Barton, who told me that the Doctor said Mansfield was a touch and go case, but already rejoicing at the prospect of getting across to Blighty, and cursing the bad wire-cutters which had been served out for the raid. I prided myself on having pulled off something rather heroic; but when all was said and done it was only the sort of thing which people often did during a fire or a railway accident.

===Chinese===
下到「71 号·北」时，云雀在蒙蒙细雨的天空里婉转尖叫。我心里涌起一阵狂喜。身后是恐怖与黑暗。金杰克谢了我。还活着真好啊，我想，大步流星地往山下走，绕过一个个弹坑、跨过一道道交通壕——我没那份心情去走那些湿漉漉的沟了。乡野在我四周朦胧地铺展，而乡野便是生命，一直、一直伸展向自由。连「71 号·北」那个阴郁的小窝窟，似乎也带着欢迎在等我，弗卢克已备好热茶。不久我便兴奋地对德利和巴顿老头叽里呱啦讲了起来；巴顿告诉我，医生说曼斯菲尔德生死未卜，可他已然在为能回英国养伤而暗暗欢喜，嘴里还咒骂着突袭时分发下来的那些不顶用的剪铁丝工具。我颇以自己干了件颇为英勇的事为傲；可说到底，那也不过是人们在火警或火车失事时常常会做的那种事罢了。

===Original===
Nothing important had happened on the British Front that night, so we were rewarded by a mention in the G.H.Q. communiqué. “At Mametz we raided hostile trenches. Our party entered without difficulty and maintained a spirited bombing fight, and finally withdrew at the end of twenty-five minutes.” This was their way of telling England. Aunt Evelyn probably read it automatically in her *Morning Post*, unaware that this minor event had almost caused her to receive a farewell letter from me. The next night our Company was in the front-line and I recovered three hatchets and a knobkerrie from No Man’s Land. Curiously enough, I hadn’t yet seen a German. I had seen dim figures on my dark patrols; but no human faces.

===Chinese===
那天夜里英国整条战线上都没发生什么要紧事，于是我们便得了个奖赏——在总司令部的战报里被提了一笔。「在马梅斯，我们对敌方战壕发动了突袭。我方人员顺利进入，进行了一场激烈的手榴弹战斗，最终于二十五分钟后撤回。」这便是他们向英格兰通报的方式。伊芙琳姨母多半在她的《晨邮报》里木然地读到了它，浑然不知这一桩小事险些叫她收到我的一封诀别信。第二天夜里我们连上了前沿，我从无人区找回三把短斧和一根圆头棍。说来奇怪，我至今尚未见过一个德国人。我在漆黑的巡逻里见过模糊的人影；却没见过一张人脸。

## III Before the Push / III 进攻之前

### I / 一

===Original===
One evening about a fortnight later I was down in that too familiar front-line dugout with Barton, who had just returned from leave and was unable to disguise his depression. I wasn’t feeling over bright myself after tramping to and fro in the gluey trenches all day. A little rain made a big difference to life up there, and the weather had been wet enough to make the duckboards wobble when one stepped on them. I’d got sore feet and a trench-mouth and food tasted filthy. And the Boche trench-mortars had been strafing us more than usual that evening. Probably I’ve been smoking too much lately, I thought, knocking my pipe out against one of the wooden props which held up the cramped little den, and staring irritably at my mud-encumbered boots, for I was always trying to keep squalor at bay and the discomfort of feeling dirty and tickly all over was almost as bad as a bombardment. It certainly wasn’t much of a place to be low-spirited in, so I tried reading the paper which the Company-Sergeant-Major had just delivered when he came down for the rum ration. The rum-jar lived under Barton’s bed; having been poured into some tin receptacle, the rum was carried cautiously upstairs to be tipped into the men’s tea-dixies.
“Fancy Kitchener being drowned in the North Sea!” I remarked, looking up from the *Daily Mail* which was making the most of that historic event. (It seemed a long time since I rode past his park wall in Kent when I was with the Yeomanry; it would be two years next September, though it wasn’t much use looking as far ahead as that, with all these preparations going on for the “Big Push.”) Barton was scribbling away with his indelible pencil﻿—filling in all that bosh which made Brigade think they were busy. “If you want my opinion,” he grumbled, “I believe those damned Irish had a hand in Kitchener being drowned. I’d like to see that fatuous island of theirs sunk under the sea.” Barton had an irrational dislike of the Irish, and he always blamed anything on them if he could. He wouldn’t even admit that Ireland was an agricultural country, and since the Easter Rebellion in Dublin it wasn’t safe to show him a bottle of Irish whisky. “I’ve never met an Irishman with any more sense than that mouse!” he exclaimed. A mouse was standing on its head in the sugar-basin, which was made of metal and contained soft sugar. He eyed the mouse morosely, as though accusing it of Irish ancestry. “This time three nights ago my wife and I were having dinner at the Café Royal. Upstairs at the Café Royal﻿—best food in London, and as good as ever even now. I tell you, Kangar, it’s too much of a bloody contrast, coming back to all this.” There was a muffled *Wump* and both candles went out. Something heavy had burst outside our door. Lighting the candles, I thought I’d just as soon be upstairs as down in this musty limbo. In about an hour I should be out with the wiring-party, dumping concertina wire in the shell-holes along the edge of the craters. I wondered if I should ever get a Blighty wound. One of our best officers had been hit last night while out with the wirers. This was Bill Eaves, who had been a Classical Scholar at Cambridge and had won medals there for writing Greek and Latin epigrams. Now he’d got a nice bullet wound in the shoulder, with the muscles damaged enough to keep him in England several months. And two nights ago Ormand and a Sandhurst boy named Harris had been hit while on a working party. Ormand’s was a “cushy” shell splinter; but Harris had got his knee smashed up, and the doctor said he would probably be out of the war for good. It was funny to think of young Harris being hit in the first twenty-four hours of his first tour of trenches.

===Chinese===
约莫两星期后的一个傍晚，我和巴顿一同待在那间熟得不能再熟的前沿掩体里，他刚休假回来，那股消沉掩都掩不住。我在黏糊糊的战壕里来回跋涉了一整天，自己也不大精神。在上头，一点小雨就能让日子大变样，近来雨水足，踩在泥炭板上都打晃。我脚走疼了，得了战壕口炎，吃什么都一股糟味。那天傍晚，德国人的战壕迫击炮也比平常更凶地轰了我们一通。大概是我近来烟抽得太凶了，我想着，把烟斗在手心里那根撑着这逼仄小窝的木柱上磕了磕，恼恼地瞪着自己那双糊满烂泥的靴子——我一向努力把肮脏挡在身外，浑身又脏又痒的不适，简直跟挨炮轰差不多。这么个地方，委实不是发愁的好去处，我便试着看连队军士长送来的报纸——他下来领朗姆酒配额时一并捎来的。朗姆酒坛子栖在巴顿床底下；倒进某只洋铁家什里之后，便有人小心翼翼地端上壕去，分到弟兄们的茶饭盒里。
「基钦纳竟在北海里淹死了，你想想！」我从《每日邮报》上抬起头来说——那报纸正把这件历史性事件渲染得绘声绘色。（自从我在肯特郡骑马经过他庄园的围墙——那时我还在义勇骑兵队——仿佛已过了很久；到下个九月就两年了，虽说有「大攻势」这许多筹备在进行，往那么远去想也没什么用。）巴顿正用他那支擦不掉字的铅笔刷刷地写——填那些让旅部以为他们忙碌无比的废话。「要问我的看法，」他嘟囔道，「我相信基钦纳淹死，是那帮该死的爱尔兰人插了一手。我真巴望他们那座蠢岛沉到海底下去。」巴顿对爱尔兰人有一股无来由的厌恶，但凡能赖到他们头上的事，他一概赖上去。他连爱尔兰是个农业国家都不肯认；自都柏林复活节起义以来，当着他的面亮出一瓶爱尔兰威士忌都不安全。「我见过的爱尔兰人，没一个比那只老鼠更有头脑！」他嚷道。一只老鼠正头朝下倒立在糖盆里——那盆是金属的，盛着红糖。他阴郁地瞪着那老鼠，仿佛指控它有爱尔兰血统。「三夜之前的这个时候，我跟我妻子还在皇家咖啡馆用晚餐呢。皇家咖啡馆楼上——伦敦最好的菜，至今依旧好。我告诉你，袋鼠，从那儿回到这摊子事里，对比也太他妈惨烈了。」闷闷的一声「嘭」，两支蜡烛都灭了。有样重东西在我们门外炸开了。点蜡烛时我想：与其待在这霉味十足的混沌里，我倒宁可上壕去。再过约莫一个钟头，我就要随布线队出去，往弹坑边沿那些炮弹坑里堆放蛇腹式铁丝网了。我不知道自己会不会受上一处能回国的伤。昨晚我们一名最好的军官在外出布线时中了招。那是比尔·伊夫斯，在剑桥是读古典的，还因希腊文拉丁文警句得过奖。如今他肩膀上挨了一颗漂亮的子弹，肌肉伤得不轻，够他在英国歇上几个月。两夜之前，奥曼德和一个桑赫斯特来的小伙子哈里斯在干劳役时也中了招。奥曼德挨的是一块「舒坦」的弹片；可哈里斯的膝盖被砸烂了，医生说他大概要彻底告别这场战争了。想到年轻的哈里斯头一趟蹲战壕的最初二十四小时里就挨了打，真是怪唏嘘的。

===Original===
Anyhow we were due for Divisional Rest, which would take us to the back area for three weeks, and the clogging monotony of life in the line would be cleaned out of our minds. And you never knew﻿—perhaps the war would end in those three weeks. The troops were beginning to need a rest badly, for most of them had been doing tours of trenches ever since the end of January, and even when we were at Morlancourt there was a working party every second night, which meant being out from seven o’clock till after midnight. And Miles, my platoon sergeant, hadn’t been quite his usual self since the raid; but he’d been in France nearly a year, which was longer than most men could stick such a life. The chances are, I thought, that if Sergeant Miles is still here a few months hence, and I’m not, some fresh young officer from England will be accusing him of being windy. Sooner or later I should get windy myself. It was only a question of time. But could this sort of thing be measured by ordinary time, I wondered (as I lay on a bunk wishing to God Barton would stop blowing on his spectacles, which surely didn’t need all that polishing). No; one couldn’t reckon the effect of the war on people by weeks and months. I’d noticed that boys under twenty stood it worst, especially when the weather was bad. Mud and boredom and discomfort seemed to take all the guts out of them. If an officer crumpled up Kinjack sent him home as useless, with a confidential report. Several such officers were usually drifting about at the Depot, and most of them ended up with safe jobs in England. But if a man became a dud in the ranks, he just remained where he was until he was killed or wounded. Delicate discrimination about private soldiers wasn’t possible. A “number nine pill” was all they could hope for if they went sick. Barton sometimes told me that I was too easygoing with the men when we were out of the Line, but it often seemed to me that I was asking them to do more than could be fairly expected of them. It’s queer, I thought, how little one really knows about the men. In the Line one finds out which are the duds, and one builds up a sort of comradeship with the tough and willing ones. But back in billets the gap widens and one can’t do much to cheer them up. I could never understand how they managed to keep as cheery as they did through such drudgery and discomfort, with nothing to look forward to but going over the top or being moved up to Flanders again.

===Chinese===
无论如何，我们该轮到师休整了，要把我们带到后方待三个礼拜，战壕里那种黏滞的枯燥便会被从心头洗去一空。再说了，你永远说不准——说不定这三个礼拜里战争就结束了呢。弟兄们开始迫切地需要休整了，他们大多数人自一月底起就一趟趟蹲战壕，即便在莫朗库尔时也是隔一晚就要出一次劳役队——从七点出去一直干到过了午夜。还有我那个排的军士迈尔斯，自那次突袭以来便有点不大像往常的他了；可他在法国已待了将近一年，比大多数人受得住这种日子的时间都长。多半，我想，要是迈尔斯中士几个月后还在这里、而我不在了，从英国新来的嫩军官就会指责他胆怯了。迟早有一天我自己也会怯起来。那只是时间问题。可这种事能拿寻常的时间来度量么，我纳闷（我躺在铺上，恨不得求老天叫巴顿别再吹他的眼镜片了——那镜片哪用得着这么个擦法）。不能；人是不能用几个星期几个月来算这场战争对他作用的。我留意过，二十岁不到的小伙子最吃不消，尤其天一坏。烂泥、无聊和不适，仿佛把他们一身的精气神都抽空。哪个军官要是垮了，金杰克便以一份密报把他当作废料送回国。这类军官通常有几个在补充兵营里飘着，多半最后都在英国得了份安稳差事。可若当兵的在队伍里成了废物，他只能就地待着，直到阵亡或负伤。对待列兵没法这么细致区别。他们若是去求医，能盼到的无非一颗「九号丸」。巴顿有时说我撤下火线后对弟兄们太和软，可我时常觉得，我是在让他们做超过公道所该期望的活。怪的是，我想，一个人对当兵的其实所知甚少。在火线上你能辨出谁是废物，跟那些硬气肯干的人则建立起一种战友之情。可一回到驻地，这道鸿沟便豁然张开，你想给他们打气也无能为力。我始终想不通，他们是怎能在这样的苦役和不适里照旧打起精神来的，除了等着跃出掩体冲锋或是再被调上弗兰德斯，再没什么盼头。

===Original===
Next evening, just before stand-to, I was watching a smouldering sunset and thinking that the sky was one of the redeeming features of the war. Behind the support line where I stood, the shell-pited ground sloped sombrely into the dusk; the distances were blue and solemn, with a few trees grouped on a ridge, dark against the deep-glowing embers of another day endured. I was looking westward, away from the war, and the evening star twinkled serenely. Guns were grumbling miles away. Cartwheels could be heard on the roads behind Fricourt; it still made me feel strange when I remembered that they were German cartwheels.
Moments like those are unreproduceable when I look back and try to recover their living texture. One’s mind eliminates boredom and physical discomfort, retaining an incomplete impression of a strange, intense, and unique experience. If there be such a thing as ghostly revisitation of this earth, and if ghosts can traverse time and choose their ground, I would return to the Bois Français sector as it was then. But since I always assume that spectral presences have lost their sense of smell (and I am equally uncertain about their auditory equipment) such hauntings might be as inadequate as those which now absorb my mental energy. For trench life was an existence saturated by the external senses; and although our actions were domineered over by military discipline, our animal instincts were always uppermost. While I stood there then, I had no desire to diagnose my environment. Freedom from its oppressiveness was what I longed for. Listening to the German cartwheels rumbling remotely, I thought of an old German governess I had known, and how she used to talk about “dear old Moltke and Bismarck” and her quiet home in Westphalia where her father had been a Protestant pastor. I wondered what sort of a place Westphalia was, and wished I’d seen more of the world before it became so busy with bloodshed. For until I came out to the war I had only the haziest notion of anything outside England.
Well, here I was, and my incomplete life might end any minute; for although the evening air was as quiet as a cathedral, a canister soon came over quite near enough to shatter my meditations with its unholy crash and cloud of black smoke. A rat scampered across the tin cans and burst sandbags, and trench atmosphere reasserted itself in a smell of chloride of lime. On my way to the dugout, to fetch my revolver and attend the twilight ceremony of stand-to and rifle inspection, I heard the voice of Flook; just round a bend of the support trench he was asking one of the company bombers if he’d seen his officer-bloke go along that way. Flook was in a hurry to tell me that I was to go on leave. I didn’t wait to inspect my platoon’s rifles, and not many minutes later I was on my way down the Old Kent Road trench. Maple Redoubt was getting its usual evening bombardment, and as a man had been killed by a whizzbang in the Old Kent Road a few minutes earlier, I was glad when I was riding back to Morlancourt with Dottrell; glad, too, to be driving to Mericourt station behind the sluggish pony next morning; to hear the mellow bells of Rouen on the evening air while the leave train stood still for half an hour before making up its mind to lumber on to Havre. And thus the gradations of thankfulness continued, until I found myself in a quiet house in Kensington where I was staying the night with an old friend of Aunt Evelyn’s.
To be there, on a fine Sunday evening in June, with the drawing-room windows open and someone playing the piano next door, was an experience which now seemed as queer as the unnatural conditions I had returned from. Books, pictures, furniture, all seemed kind and permanent and unrelated to the present time and its troubles. I felt detached from my surroundings﻿—rather as if I were in a doctor’s waiting-room, expecting to be informed that I had some incurable disease. The sound of the piano suggested that the specialist had a happy home life of his own, but it had no connection with my coming and going. A sense of gentle security pervaded the room; but I could no longer call my life my own. The pensive music had caught me off my guard; I was only an intruder from the Western Front. But the room contained one object which unexpectedly reminded me of the trenches﻿—a silent canary in a cage. I had seen canaries in cages being carried by the men of the tunnelling company when they emerged from their mine-galleries.

===Chinese===
第二天傍晚，正好在待命之前，我望着一片将熄的落日，心想这天穹要算这场战争里少有的救赎之物了。在我所站的支援壕身后，弹坑累累的地面阴沉地向暮色里倾斜下去；远处一片蓝，庄严寂静，几棵树聚在一道山脊上，在又熬过去的一天那深沉灼亮的余烬背景上现出黑色的剪影。我望着西方，背对着这场战争，长庚星在恬静地眨眼。数英里外大炮在低吼。弗里库尔那边的路上能听见车轮声；一想到那是德国人的大车，我至今仍觉怪异。
回头想去，那种时刻是无法复现的，怎样努力也找不回它活生生的肌理。人的记忆会滤去枯燥与身体的不适，留下一桩奇异、紧张、独一无二的经历的不完整印象。倘若世间真有鬼魂重游故地这回事，倘若鬼魂能穿越时间、自择去处，我愿回到彼时的布瓦弗朗赛防区。可我一向假定鬼影已失了嗅觉（对他们有没有听觉装置，我同样拿不准），那么这般缠绵或许也同此刻耗费我心神的那番回望一样力不从心。因为堑壕生活是一种被外在感官浸透的生存；尽管我们的举动被军纪牢牢辖制，我们身上的兽性本能却始终占着上风。那时我站在那里，全无心思去解剖周遭的环境。我所渴望的，是从它的压抑里解脱出来。听着远处德国人车轮辘辘的声响，我想起从前认识的一位德国老家庭女教师，想起她常谈起「亲爱的老毛奇和俾斯麦」，谈起她在威斯特伐利亚那个安静的家——她父亲是那里的一位新教牧师。我纳闷威斯特伐利亚到底是个怎样的地方，懊悔在这世界忙着流血之前没多看看它。因为出来打仗之前，对英格兰之外的事物，我只有最模糊的概念。
嗯，我人就在这儿，我那尚未完结的小命随时可能了结；因为尽管傍晚的空气静得像教堂，一颗「圆筒」旋即飞来，就近用那不洁的轰响与一团黑烟把我的沉思砸了个粉碎。一只老鼠从洋铁罐与炸破的沙袋间窜过，战壕的气息借着漂白粉的味儿重又立住了威。我去掩体取左轮手枪、要赶那薄暮时分的「待命」与验枪典礼，路上听见弗卢克的声音；就在支援壕一道拐弯处过去一点，他正问连里一名投弹兵见没见他那「军官伙计」往那边去过。弗卢克急着要告诉我：我要休假了。我没等检查弟兄们的步枪，没出几分钟便沿着「老肯特路」战壕往下走了。枫树据点正照例挨着傍晚的炮击，而「老肯特路」几分钟前刚有一人被一发「嗖—砰」打死，故而与多特雷尔骑马回莫朗库尔时我心头一宽；次晨坐在那匹慢吞吞的小马背后，赶往梅里库尔火车站时也一宽；休假专列在鲁昂停下半小时、迟迟才打定主意咣当咣当往阿弗尔去时，听见晚风中鲁昂那醇厚的钟声，又是一宽。这般层层递进的庆幸一路延续，直到我置身肯辛顿一幢安静的宅子里——我在伊芙琳姨母一位老友家借宿一夜。
六月里一个好天气的星期日傍晚，起居室的窗户敞开着，隔壁有人在弹钢琴——置身其中，这种感觉如今看来竟与我所归自的那番不自然的境况同样怪异。书、画、家具，一切都显得和善、恒久，与当下时世及其烦扰毫无瓜葛。我感到与周遭脱了节——倒像坐在一间医生候诊室里，等着被宣告得了某种不治之症。琴声暗示这位专家自己有着美满的家庭生活，可那与我之来去毫无干系。屋里弥漫着一种温柔的安宁；可我再不能把命算作自己的了。那沉思般的乐声叫我猝不及防；我不过是从西线来的一个闯入者。可屋里却有一样东西意外地让我想起战壕——笼里一只不吭声的金丝雀。我曾见过坑道兵从坑道里出来时，手里便拎着笼中的金丝雀。

### II / 二

===Original===
Correspondingly queer (though I didn’t consciously observe it at the time) was the experience of returning to France after sleeping seven nights in a proper bed and wearing civilian clothes. The personal implications were obvious, since everybody at home seemed to know that the long-planned offensive was due to kick off at the end of June. Officers going on leave had been cautioned to say nothing about it, but even Aunt Evelyn was aware of the impending onslaught. I was disinclined to talk about the trenches; nevertheless I permitted myself to drop a few heavy hints. No one had any notion what the Big Push would be like, except that it would be much bigger than anything which had happened before. And somehow those previous battles hadn’t divulged themselves very distinctly to anyone except the actual participators, who had so far proved inarticulate reporters.
As regards my own adventures, I had decided to say nothing to my aunt about the raid. Nevertheless it all slipped out on the second evening, probably after she had been telling me how splendidly Mrs. Ampney’s nephew had done out in Mesopotamia. Also I didn’t omit to mention that I had been recommended for a Military Cross. “But I thought you were only looking after the horses,” she expostulated, clutching my hand; her anxious face made me wish I’d held my tongue about it. Of course, Aunt Evelyn wanted me to do well in the war, but she couldn’t enjoy being reminded that “do be careful to wear your warm overcoat, dearie,” was no precaution against German bombs and bullets. Afterwards I excused myself by thinking that she was bound to find out sooner or later, especially if I got killed.

===Chinese===
休假睡足了七夜正经床铺、又穿过几天便衣之后，重返法国——这番体验是同样的怪异（尽管当时我并未有意识地留意到）。其中含义是明摆着的：家乡人人都似乎知道，那筹备已久的攻势定在六月底发动。休假军官都被叮嘱不可走漏风声，可连伊芙琳姨母都察觉到这场风暴将至。我不大愿谈战壕；不过我还是容许自己重重地漏了几句口风。没人知道「大攻势」会是什么模样，只晓得它将比此前任何事都大得多。而先前那些仗，除了亲身参与的人，对谁都未透露得多清楚；而那些参与者，迄今为止证明都是不善言辞的叙述者。
至于我自己的奇遇，我已决计不向姨母提那次突袭。可到了第二天晚上，它还是漏了出来，多半是在她对我讲安普尼太太的外甥在美索不达米亚干得多么出色之后。我也没有漏掉提及我被荐举了一枚军功十字勋章。「可我以为你只是照看马匹的呀，」她抓住我的手，分辩道；她那一脸焦虑，叫我不免后悔多嘴。姨母当然盼我在战争里有所建树，可一被提醒「千万穿暖和的大衣呀，宝贝」对德国人的炸弹和子弹并不顶事，她就高兴不起来了。事后我替自己开脱：这事她迟早总归要知道，尤其我若被打死的话。

===Original===
Next day I walked across the fields to Butley and had tea with my old friend Captain Huxtable. I found him chubby-cheeked as ever, and keeping up what might be called a Justice of the Peace attitude towards the war. Any able-bodied man not serving in H.M. Forces should be required to show a thundering good reason for it, and the sooner conscription came in the better. That was his opinion; in the meantime he was working his farm with two elderly men and a boy; “and that’s about all an old crock like me can do for his country.” I gave him to understand that it was a jolly fine life out at the Front, and, for the moment, I probably believed what I was saying. I wasn’t going to wreck my leave with facing facts, and I’d succeeded in convincing myself that I really wanted to go back. Captain Huxtable and I decided, between us, that the Push would finish the war by Christmas. While we talked, pacing to and fro in the garden, with his surly black retriever at our heels, the rooks cawed applaudingly in the clump of elms near by as though all was well with England on that June afternoon. I knew that the Captain would have asked nothing better than to go over the top with his old regiment, if only he’d been thirty years younger, and I wished I could have told him so, when we were standing at his gate. But English reticence prohibited all that sort of thing, and I merely remarked that Aunt Evelyn’s lightning-conductor had been blown off the chimney in the spring and she said it wasn’t worth while having it put up again. He laughed and said she must be getting war-weary; she had always been so particular about the lightning-conductor. “We old ’uns can’t expect to be feeling very cock-a-hoop in these days,” he added, wrinkling up his shrewd and kindly little eyes and giving my hand a farewell squeeze which meant more than he could say aloud.

===Chinese===
第二天我穿过田野走到巴特利，与老朋友赫克斯特布尔上尉一起喝下午茶。他像从前那样两颊圆鼓鼓的，对这场战争保持着一种可称之为「治安法官派头」的姿态。任何壮健男子不投身皇家军队的，都该被要求给出一个响当当的理由；征兵制越早实行越好。这便是他的看法；与此同时，他靠着两个老人和一个男孩在经营他的农场；「我这么个半残的老家伙，能为国做的也就这点事了。」我让他以为前线的日子好得很，那一刻，我多半自己也信了这话。我不愿用面对现实来毁掉我的休假，且已成功说服自己：我是真心想回去的。赫克斯特布尔上尉和我一致断定，这场攻势到圣诞节就能了结这场战争。我们一面谈着一面在花园里踱来踱去，他那只气鼓鼓的黑寻回犬跟在脚后；近旁一丛榆树上的秃鼻鸦呱呱叫着，仿佛在鼓掌，好似那个六月的下午英格兰万事如意。我知道，只要能年轻三十岁，上尉最盼望的莫过于随他的老团跃出掩体冲锋了；我恨不得在我们站在他家院门时把这话告诉他。可英国人的矜持不许人这样开口，我只得说伊芙琳姨母家的避雷针春天被风吹下了烟囱，她说不值得再装上去了。他笑道，她想必也开始厌战了；她一向对那避雷针顶讲究的。「我们这些老家伙，这种日子指望不来多精神了，」他补一句，那双精明而和气的小眼睛眯起来，把我的手用力一握道别——这一握里头的意思，比他能说出口的多得多。

===Original===
