When Aunt Evelyn wondered whether I’d like anyone to come to dinner on my last evening (she called it Friday night) I replied that I’d rather we were alone. There were very few to ask, and, as she said, people were difficult to get hold of nowadays. So, after a dinner which included two of my favourite puddings, we made the best of a bad job by playing cribbage (a game we had been addicted to when I was at home for my school holidays) while the black Persian cat washed his face with his paw and blinked contentedly at the fire which had been lit though there was no need for it, the night being warm and still. We also had the grey parrot brought up from the kitchen. Clinging sideways to the bars of his cage, Popsy seemed less aware of the war than anyone I’d met. But perhaps he sensed the pang I felt when saying goodbye to him next morning; parrots understand more than they pretend to, and this one had always liked me. He wasn’t much of a talker, though he could imitate Aunt Evelyn calling the cats.
Next morning she contrived to be stoically chatty until I had seen her turn back to the house door and the village taxi was rattling me down the hill. She had sensibly refrained from coming up to London to see me off. But at Waterloo Station I was visibly reminded that going back for the Push was rather rough on one’s relations, however incapable they might be of sharing the experience. There were two leave trains and I watched the people coming away after the first one had gone out. Some sauntered away with assumed unconcern; they chatted and smiled. Others hurried past me with a crucified look; I noticed a well-dressed woman biting her gloved fingers; her eyes stared fixedly; she was returning alone to a silent house on a fine Sunday afternoon.

===Chinese===
伊芙琳姨母琢磨着我最后一晚（她称之为星期五夜里）要不要请谁来吃饭，我答我宁可就咱俩。能请的本来也没几个，再说，照她讲，如今人都不好找。于是在一顿有两样我爱吃的布丁的晚饭之后，我们以打「十五点」纸牌来将就这不大好过的时光（这是我放假在家时我们俩都上瘾的游戏）；那只黑波斯猫用爪子洗脸，又对着火炉满足地眨眼睛——火是白生的，那夜又暖又静。我们还将那只灰鹦鹉从厨房端了上来。波普西侧身攀着笼栏，似乎比我所遇见的任何人都更不知这场战争为何物。但说不定它也觉察到了我次晨向它道别时心头那一阵酸楚；鹦鹉懂的比它们装出来的多，而这只一向喜欢我。它不算会说话，不过能学伊芙琳姨母唤猫的腔调。
第二天早晨，她强撑着絮絮叨叨，直到我看着她转身回了家门、村子的出租车载着我咯噔咯噔下了山。她明智地没上伦敦来送我。可在滑铁卢车站，我眼前分明地被提醒：为这场攻势而回去，对自家的亲人未免太狠，纵然他们再无可能分担这番经历。有两趟休假专列，我看着头一趟开走之后人们离去的模样。有人故作若无其事地踱开；他们说说笑笑。有人从我身边匆匆而过，一脸被钉十字架的神情；我看见一位衣着考究的妇人在咬她戴着手套的手指；她双眼直瞪着；在一个晴朗的星期日下午，她独自回到一座寂静的宅子。

===Original===
But I had nobody to see me off, so I could settle myself in the corner of a carriage, light my pipe and open a Sunday paper (though goodness knows what it contained, apart from communiqués, casualty lists, and reassuring news from Galicia, Bukovina, and other opaque arenas of war). It would have been nice to read the first-class cricket averages for a change, and their absence was an apt epitome of the life we were condemned to. While the train hurried out of London I watched the flitting gardens of suburban houses. In my foxhunting days I had scorned the suburbs, but now there was something positively alluring in the spectacle of a City man taking it easy on his little lawn at Surbiton. Woking Cemetery was a less attractive scene, and my eyes recoiled from it to reassure themselves that my parcels were still safe on the rack, for those parcels were the important outcome of my previous day’s shopping.
Armed with Aunt Evelyn’s membership ticket (posted back to her afterwards) I had invaded the Army and Navy Stores and procured a superb salmon, two bottles of old brandy, an automatic pistol, and two pairs of wire-cutters with rubber-covered handles. The salmon was now my chief concern. I was concerned about its future freshness, for I had overstayed my leave by twenty-four hours. A rich restaurant dinner followed by a mechanical drawing-room comedy hadn’t made the risk of Kinjack’s displeasure seem worth while; but I felt that the salmon spelt safety at Battalion Headquarters. Probably the word “smelt” also entered my apprehensive mind. The brandy claimed that it had been born in 1838, so one day more or less couldn’t affect its condition, as long as I kept an eye on it (for such bottles were liable to lose themselves on a leave boat). The wire-cutters were my private contribution to the Great Offensive. I had often cursed the savage bluntness of our Company’s wire-cutters, and it occurred to me, in the Army and Navy Stores, that if we were going over the top we might want to cut our own wire first, to say nothing of the German wire (although our artillery would have made holes in that, I hoped). So I bought these very civilized ones, which looked almost too good for the Front Line. The man in the Weapon Department at the Stores had been persuasive about a periscope (probably prismatic) but I came to the conclusion that a periscope was a back number in my case. I shouldn’t be in the trench long enough to need it. Apart from the wire-cutters and the pistol, all other “trench requisites” appeared redundant. I couldn’t see myself leading my platoon with Mortleman’s Patent Sound Absorbers plugged in my ears, and a combined Compass-Barometer also failed to attract me. The automatic pistol wasn’t “warranted to stop a man,” but it could be slipped into the pocket. It was only a plaything, but I was weary of my Colt revolver, with which I knew I couldn’t hit anything, although I had blazed it off a few times in the dark when I was pretending to be important in No Man’s Land. The only object I could be sure of hitting was myself, and I decided (in the Army and Navy Stores) that I might conceivably find it necessary to put myself out of my misery, if the worst came to the worst and I was lying out in a shell-hole with something more serious than a Blighty wound. To blow one’s brains out with that clumsy Colt was unthinkable. The automatic pistol, on the other hand, was quite a charming little weapon. Not that I’d ever been fond of firearms. I had never shot at a bird or an animal in my life, though I’d often felt that my position as a sportsman would be stronger if I were “a good man with a gun.”

===Chinese===
可没人来送我，于是我能舒舒服服在车厢角落坐下，点上烟斗，摊开一份星期日报纸（天晓得里面除了战报、伤亡名单、以及来自加利西亚、布科维纳和其他晦暗不明战场的「好消息」之外还有什么）。换换口味读读一等板球的击球平均分该多好，它们的缺席正是我们所被判定的那种生活的恰当写照。火车驶出伦敦时，我望着郊区住宅一座座掠过的花园。猎狐那阵子我曾瞧不起郊区，可如今，看着一位伦敦城的先生在瑟比顿自家那片小草坪上悠闲自在，竟有说不出的诱人。沃金公墓那一景便没那么悦目了，我的目光从上头缩回来，转而去确认我那几只包裹还稳稳当当地待在行李架上——因为这几只包裹是我前一日采买的要紧成果。
揣着伊芙琳姨母的会员证（事后寄还给她），我闯进了海陆军百货店，置办了一条上等的鲑鱼、两瓶陈年白兰地、一支自动手枪、两把胶皮把的铁丝剪。这条鲑鱼眼下是我最大的牵挂。我牵挂它日后的鲜度，因为我已超假二十四小时。一顿阔绰的馆子晚餐，外加一出机械的客厅喜剧，并没让金杰克不悦这一风险显得划算；但我感觉那条鲑鱼便是营部里的护身符。说不定「闻」这个字眼也悄悄溜进了我惴惴的心里。白兰地自称生于一八三八，那么差一天半天也不打紧，只要我看紧它（因为这种瓶子在休假船上很容易走失）。那两把铁丝剪，是我对这场伟大攻势的个人奉献。我常咒骂我们连里那把粗钝不堪的铁丝剪；在海陆军百货店里我忽然想到：要跃出掩体，我们也许得先剪开自家的铁丝网——更别说德国人的铁丝网了（虽说我们的炮兵会在那上头打些缺口，我希望）。于是我买了这两把颇为文明的，看起来好得几乎不配进前沿。百货店武器部那位售货员力荐一架潜望镜（多半是棱镜式的），可我断定潜望镜于我已是过时的物件。我在战壕里待不了那么久，用不上它。除掉铁丝剪和手枪，其余一切「战壕必备」都属多余。我可想象不出自己耳朵里塞着「莫特尔曼消音器」专利、领着排里弟兄行进；那种「罗盘—气压计」二合一也引不起我的兴致。自动手枪「不保证能放倒一个人」，可它能揣进口袋里。它不过是个玩意儿，但我已厌倦了我的柯尔特左轮——我知道用它什么都打不中，尽管在无人区装作要人时，我也在黑暗里胡乱放过几枪。我唯一有把握命中的目标只有我自己；于是（在海陆军百货店里）我打定主意：万一最坏的最坏，我躺在弹坑里、挨的又比一处能回国的伤更重，我说不定需要了结自己。用那支笨拙的柯尔特自尽是不可想象的。自动手枪则不然，倒是一支颇为可爱的小武器。倒不是我向来喜欢枪械。我平生从未向一只鸟、一只兽开过枪，尽管我时常觉得，倘若我是「使枪的好手」，我作为一名运动家的地位会更稳些。

===Original===
The truth was that the only explosive weapon I owned before the war was a toy pistol which made a noise but discharged nothing. Sitting in the wrong-way leave train I remembered how, when about nine years old, I used to go up to the little sweet-shop in the village and buy “three penn’orth of percussion caps” for my pistol; and how the buxom old woman used to ask briskly, “Anything else today, Master George?” Whereupon I would be compelled to decide between clove and peppermint bulls’ eyes, with a bar of chocolate-cream to make it up to sixpence. Twenty years was a long time ago; but already the village green as I saw it last week was beginning to seem almost as remote.﻿ ﻿… However, it was no use dreaming about all that now; Kinjack’s salmon was my immediate problem, and as soon as I was on board the crowded boat, I consulted an obliging steward and my fishy insurance policy was providentially accommodated in the cold-storage cupboard. Consequently my mind was unperturbed when we steamed out of Southampton Water. I watched the woods on the Isle of Wight, hazily receding in the heat. And when the Isle of Wight was out of sight﻿—well, there was nothing to be done about it.

===Chinese===
实情是，战前我唯一的爆炸性武器，是一只会响、却不射出任何东西的玩具手枪。坐在反向而行的休假火车里，我想起约莫九岁时，常到村里那家小糖果店去给我的枪买「三便士的火帽」；那位丰腴的老妇人总爱利落地问：「今儿个还要点别的吗，乔治少爷？」于是我便不得不在丁香薄荷两种靶子糖之间作出抉择，再添一条奶油巧克力凑足六便士。二十年前是够久的了；可即便是上周所见的村中草地，如今也已开始显得近乎遥远。……然而，眼下做梦也无济于事；金杰克那条鲑鱼是我眼下的当务之急，一登上挤满人的船，我便求一位热心的侍者，我那张「鱼形保单」便蒙老天照应，被安置进了冷藏柜。于是船驶出南安普敦湾时我心无挂碍。我望着怀特岛上的树林在暑气里朦胧退远。而当怀特岛也消失在视野之外——嗯，无可如何了。

===Original===
At Havre I was instructed, by the all-knowing authority responsible for my return, to get out of the train at Corbie. Havre was a glitter of lights winking on dark slabbing water. Soon the glumly-laden train was groaning away from the wharves, and we nodded and snored through the night. Daylight came, and we crawled past green landscapes blurred with drizzling rain. Of my compartment companions I remember nothing except that one of them talked irrepressibly about his father’s farm in Suffolk. His father, he said, owned a bull who had produced sixty black and white calves. This information was received with apathy. The Battalion was at Bussy, a three mile walk in late afternoon sunshine. I kept to the shady side of the road, for the salmon in its hamper was still my constant care. Bussy came in sight as a pleasant little place on a tributary of the Ancre. A few of our men were bathing, and I thought how young and lighthearted they looked, splashing one another and shouting as they rocked a crazy boat under some lofty poplars that shivered in a sunset breeze. How different to the trudging figures in full marching order; and how difficult to embody them in the crouching imprisonment of trench warfare!
With an unsoldierly sigh I picked up my packages and plodded on in search of C Company, who were billeted in some buildings round a friendly farmhouse. There I found Flook and despatched him to Kinjack’s Headquarters with the hamper and a bottle of brandy. Barton, to whom I entrusted the second bottle, told me that I was a cunning old Kangaroo, and then regaled me with all the rumours about next week’s operations. “The bombardment begins on Saturday,” he said, “so we’re having Battalion Sports tomorrow, in case we get moved back to Morlancourt.” Then Durley came in with Jenkins, one of the new officers who had been posted to the Battalion while I was away. Fewnings, the gentle ex-schoolmaster, had been appointed Lewis Gun officer, but he still messed with us; he now entered with the air of a man who had been teaching Euclid and Algebra all day. The Brigadier, he remarked, had ticked him off that afternoon, because he was wearing a light-coloured shirt; but no fault had been found with his Lewis Gun team organization, and, as he remarked, it wouldn’t make much odds what sort of shirt he was wearing in a week or two. Neither Durley nor I had ever been favoured with a word from our Brigadier, perhaps because our shirts were the orthodox colour. It was odd, how seldom those graduated autocrats found time to realize that a few kind words could make a platoon commander consider them jolly good Generals.

===Chinese===
在阿弗尔，那位全知全能、负责遣我归队的上级令我到科尔比下车。阿弗尔是一片在乌黑平整的水面上眨眼的灯火。很快，那辆阴沉沉载满人的火车便哼哧着驶离码头，我们打着瞌睡、鼾声起伏地过了一夜。天亮了，火车在蒙蒙细雨里模模糊糊的绿色乡野间爬行。同车厢的人我一个也不记得，只除了其中一个抑制不住地谈他在萨福克他父亲的农场。他说他父亲有一头公牛，已经下了六十头黑白花的小牛犊。这条信息被无动于衷地接收了。营部在比西，下午晚些时候的阳光里走三英里即到。我贴着路的阴凉一边走，因为篮里那条鲑鱼始终是我时刻的牵挂。比西映入眼帘，是昂克河一条支流上宜人的小地方。我们有几个弟兄在洗澡，我想他们看上去多年轻、多没心没肺啊——彼此泼水、喊叫，在几棵高耸、在傍晚微风里簌簌发抖的白杨树下摇着一只破船。与全副武装步履沉重的身影何其不同；要把那些人塞进堑壕战这种蜷缩的囚禁里，又何其难！
我叹了一口不大像军人的气，拎起包裹，继续跋涉去找 C 连——他们驻在一座和气的农庄四周的几栋房子里。我在那里找到弗卢克，差他提着篮子和一瓶白兰地送往金杰克的营部。第二瓶我托付给巴顿，他说我是个狡猾的老袋鼠，接着便把下周行动的种种传闻一股脑儿讲给我听。「炮击星期六开始，」他说，「所以咱们明天开营运动会，免得突然被调回莫朗库尔。」随后德利和詹金斯一起进来——詹金斯是我休假期间新派到营里的军官之一。那位温顺的前教书先生弗宁斯被任命为路易斯机枪官，但仍与我们同灶；他这会儿进来，一副教了一整天欧几里得几何和代数的模样。他说，旅长下午把他训了一顿，因为他穿了件浅色衬衫；可他的路易斯机枪班组织得无可挑剔；再说，照他讲，一两个礼拜之后穿什么颜色的衬衫也没什么要紧了。我和德利都不曾蒙我们旅长赐过一句话，也许是因为我们的衬衫颜色合乎规矩。怪的是，那些等阶森严的独裁者，竟极少腾出工夫意识到：几句和善的话，就能让一名排长觉得他们是挺不错的将军。

===Original===
But there was harmony in our Company Mess, as if our certainty of a volcanic future had put an end to the occasional squabblings which occurred when we were on one another’s nerves. A rank animal healthiness pervaded our existence during those days of busy living and inward foreboding The behaviour of our servants expressed it; they were competing for the favours of a handsome young woman in the farmhouse, and a comedy of primitive courtship was being enacted in the kitchen. Death would be lying in wait for the troops next week, and now the flavour of life was doubly strong. As I went to my room across the road, the cool night smelt of mown grass and leafy gardens. Away toward Corbie there was the sound of a train, and bullfrogs croaked continuously in the marshes along the river. I wasn’t sorry to be back; I was sure of that; we’d all got to go through it, and I was trying to convert the idea of death in battle into an emotional experience. Courage, I argued, is a beautiful thing, and next week’s attack is what I have been waiting for since I first joined the army. I am happy tonight, and I don’t suppose I’ll be dead in a month’s time. Going into my billet I almost fell over a goat which was tethered among some currant bushes in the garden.
---
Five days passed us by. We did easy field-training; the Battalion Sports were a great success, and we were defeated, in an officers’ tug-of-war, by our 9th Battalion who were resting a few miles away. Saturday evening brought a feeling of finality, for we were moving up to Morlancourt on Monday and the intense bombardment had begun that morning. Barton and I (and our bottle of ’38 brandy) dined at Battalion Headquarters. Kinjack was full of confidence; he told us that the French were holding on well at Verdun, which would make all the difference. But the doctor looked thoughtful, and even the brandy couldn’t make Barton optimistic about his ability to command a company in open warfare.

===Chinese===
可在我们连的食堂里却一派和睦，仿佛那场确凿无疑、山雨欲来的未来，已把我们彼此神经紧张时偶发的口角都了结了。那些忙碌度日、内心忐忑的日子里，一种粗粝的、动物式的康健弥漫着我们的生活。勤务兵们的举止正是其写照；他们在争抢农庄里一位漂亮少妇的青睐，厨房里正上演一出原始求偶的喜剧。下个星期，死神就要在前头等着队伍了，此刻生命的滋味便加倍浓烈。我穿过马路去自己的屋子时，清凉的夜里有割过的青草和枝叶繁茂的花园的气息。朝科尔比那边传来一列火车的声响，河沿岸的沼泽里牛蛙咕呱不止。我不以回来为憾；这一点我确定；我们终归都要过这一关，我正努力把战死沙场这一念头转化为一种情感体验。勇气，我论证道，是美的东西；下周的进攻，正是我自打参军以来一直在等的。今夜我是快乐的，我想，我不至于一个月之内就死了。走进住处时，我差点被一只拴在园子里茶藨子丛中的山羊绊一跤。
---
五天过去了。我们做着轻松的野外训练；营运动会大获成功，我们在一场军官拔河里输给了几英里外休整的我们第九营。星期六傍晚带来一种了结之感，因为星期一我们就开回莫朗库尔，而清晨起那场猛烈的炮击便已开始。巴顿和我（还有我们那瓶三八年白兰地）在营部用晚饭。金杰克信心十足；他告诉我们法国人在凡尔登守得不错，这便能扭转全局。可军医神色凝重，即便是那白兰地，也叫巴顿对自己在运动战中指挥一个连的能力乐观不起来。

## IV Battle / IV 战斗

### I / 一

===Original===
On the morning of a Battalion move I made it my business to keep out of the way until the last moment. At the end of a march I had my definite duties, but before we started Barton was always in such a stew that my absence was a positive advantage to him. So on Monday, after bolting my breakfast while Flook waited to pack the mugs and plates in the mess-box, I left Barton shouting irritably for the Sergeant-Major and wandered away to sit by the river until the whistles began to blow. Durley and Jenkins had gone to make sure that the billets were being left clean and tidy. In the green orchard behind the farm buildings the men were putting their kits together, their voices sounding as jolly as though they were off for a summer holiday. For me, it was a luxury to be alone for a few minutes, watching the yellow irises, and the ribbon weeds that swayed like fishes in the dimpling stream. I was sorry to be saying goodbye to the Marais and its grey-green pools and creeks and the congregation of poplar stems that upheld a cool whispering roof. Water-haunting birds whistled and piped, swinging on the bullrushes and tufted reeds, and a tribe of little green and gold frogs hopped about in the grass without caring whether they arrived anywhere. All this was obviously preferable to a battle, and it was a perfect morning to be reading a book beside the river.
But on the horizon the bombardment bumped and thudded in a continuous bubbling grumble. After a long stare at sun-flecked foliage and idly reflective alleys I bustled back to the farmyard to find my platoon all present and correct. Before I’d finished my formal inspection Barton emerged from the house with bulging pockets, his burly figure hung like a Christmas tree with haversack, water-bottle, revolver, field-glasses, gas-mask, map-case, and other oddments. The Battalion moved off at eight o’clock; by twelve-thirty it was at Morlancourt, which was now congested with infantry and supply columns, and “lousy with guns” as the saying was. A colony of camouflage-daubed tents had sprung up close to the village; this was the New Main Dressing Station. We were in our usual billets—Durley and I in the room containing a representation of the Eiffel Tower and a ludicrous oleograph of our Saviour preaching from a boat, which we always referred to as jocular Jesus. After a sultry dinner, the day ended with torrents of rain. While I lay on the floor in my fleabag the blackness of the night framed in the window was lit with incessant glare and flash of guns. But I fell asleep to the sound of full gutters and rainwater gurgling and trickling into a well, and those were comfortable noises, for they signified that I had a roof over my head. As for my fleabag, it was no hardship; I have never slept more soundly in any bed.

===Chinese===
营部开拔的那天早晨，我一贯的方针便是躲得远远的，直到最后一刻才露面。行军到了终点我自有分内的活计，可动身之前，巴顿总是急得团团转，我不在场反倒是对他莫大的成全。于是星期一那天，我狼吞虎咽吃完早饭——弗卢克在一旁等着把杯盘装进食堂箱里——便撇下气急败坏嚷着找军士长的巴顿，信步走到河边坐下，直到哨声响起。德利和詹金斯已去查看驻地是否收拾干净。农舍后面那片青葱的果园里，弟兄们正收拾行装，嗓门热闹得仿佛要出门去过暑假。于我而言，能独处片刻实是难得的奢侈——看那黄色的鸢尾，看那在涟漪丛生的溪水里如鱼般摇曳的带状水草。要与这马尔代这片灰绿色的池塘水汊道别，我心下怅然——还有那一丛白杨树干撑起的一顶清凉细语的穹盖。水鸟们啾啾唧唧，在蒲草与芦苇丛上荡着秋千；一族族金绿相间的小青蛙在草间蹦跳，全不在乎蹦到哪里去。这一切自然比一场战斗可取得多，而这样一个清晨，最宜在河边读一本书。
然而天边那场炮击却在不停地咕咚闷响，像一锅沸而不止的闷气。我对那日光斑驳的枝叶和那几条引人遐思的幽径凝望良久，才赶忙回到农庄院子里，只见我排里弟兄已悉数到齐、整整齐齐。我那正式的检查还没做完，巴顿便从屋里冒了出来，口袋鼓鼓囊囊的，他那壮硕的身躯挂得像棵圣诞树——背包、水壶、左轮手枪、望远镜、防毒面具、地图袋，还有各色零碎。营部八点钟开拔；到十二点半便到了莫朗库尔，此时村里已挤满了步兵和辎重队，照那句行话说是「枪多得生虱子」。村子附近冒出一片涂了迷彩的帐篷群，这便是新的主要包扎站。我们驻在老地方——我和德利那间屋里挂着一幅埃菲尔铁塔的画，还有一幅滑稽的彩色石印画，画的是我主从船上讲道，我们一向管它叫「爱开玩笑的耶稣」。一顿闷热的晚饭之后，这天以倾盆大雨收场。我躺在地上的睡袋里，窗外框出的那一片漆黑被大炮不停的火光闪得通明。可我是听着满溢的檐沟和雨水咕噜噜淌进一口井里的声音睡着的，这些声音令人心安，因为它们意味着我头顶上有片屋顶。至于那睡袋，算不得什么苦；我一辈子没在哪张床上睡得比这更香过。

===Original===
Operation Orders were circulated next morning. They notified us that Thursday was “Z” (or zero) day. The Seventh Division Battle Plan didn’t look aggressively unpleasant on paper as I transcribed it into my notebook. Rose Trench, Orchard Alley, Apple Alley, and Willow Avenue, were among the first objectives in our sector, and my mind very properly insisted on their gentler associations. Nevertheless this topographical Arcadia was to be seized, cleared, and occupied when the historic moment arrived and in conjunction with the French the Fourth Army took the offensive, establishing as a primary objective a line Montauban–Pozières, passing to the south of Mametz Wood. There wasn’t going to be any mistake about it this time. We decided, with quite a glow of excitement, that the Fourth Army was going to fairly wipe the floor with the Bodies. In the meantime our Corps Intelligence Summary (known as *Comic Cuts*) reported on June 27th that three enemy balloons had been set on fire and destroyed on the previous afternoon; also that a large number of enemy batteries had been silenced by our artillery. The anonymous humorist who compiled *Comic Cuts* was also able to announce that the Russians had captured a redoubt and some heavy guns at Czartovijsk, which, he explained, was forty-four miles northeast of Luck. At Martinpuich a large yellowish explosion had been observed. On Tuesday afternoon I went up to the Line with Durley, on some preliminary errand, for we were to relieve a battalion of the Border Regiment next day, in the sector in front of Fricourt Cemetery. Our Batteries were firing strenuously all along the countryside, with very little retaliation.
As we passed the gun-pits where some Heavies were hidden in a hollow called Gibraltar, I remarked on a sickly sweet smell which I attributed to the yellow weeds which were abundant there, but Durley explained that it was the lingering aroma of gas-shells. When we rode down the slope to 71. North that familiar resort appeared much the same as usual, except for the impressive accumulations of war material which were dumped along the road. Durley remarked that he supposed the old spot would never be the same again after this week; and already it seemed to us as if the old days when Mansfield and Ormand were with our company had become an experience to be looked back on with regret. The Bois Français sector had been a sort of village, but we should soon be leaving it behind us in our vindictive explorations of Rose Trench, Apple Alley, and Willow Avenue.
On our way up to the front-line we met a staff-officer who was wearing well-cut riding boots and evidently in a hurry to rejoin his horse. Larks were rejoicing aloft, and the usual symbolic scarlet poppies lolled over the sides of the communication trench; but he squeezed past us without so much as a nod, for the afternoon was too noisy to be idyllic, in spite of the larks and poppies which were so popular with war-correspondents. “I suppose those brass-hats do know a hell of a lot about it all, don’t they, Julian?” I queried. Durley replied that he hoped they’d learnt something since last autumn when they’d allowed the infantry to educate themselves at Loos, regardless of expense. “They’ve got to learn their job as they go along, like the rest of us,” he added sagely. Five sausage balloons were visible beyond the skyline, peacefully tethered to their mother earth. It was our duty to desire their destruction, and to believe that Corps Intelligence had the matter well in hand. What we did up in the Front Line I don’t remember; but while we were remounting our horses at 71. North two privates were engaged in a good-humoured scuffle; one had the other’s head under his arm. Why should I remember that and forget so much else?

===Chinese===
次日早晨作战命令传了下来。通知我们星期四是「Z」（即零时）日。我把第七师的作战计划抄进笔记本时，纸面上看着倒也没有咄咄逼人的不快之感。玫瑰壕、果园巷、苹果巷和柳树大道都在我防区首批目标之列，而我的脑子，自然死死咬住这些名字里那点温柔的情调。然而待到那个历史性时刻降临、第四集团军与法军并肩发起进攻时，这片如田园诗般的地志是要被夺取、肃清并占领的，其首要目标是确立一条由蒙托邦至波济耶尔的战线，从马梅斯树林以南通过。这一回可不会再出差错了。我们怀着相当激动的热望断定：第四集团军定会把那帮德国佬打得落花流水。与此同时，我军的军情报简报（人称《连环漫画》）报道说，六月二十七日，三只敌方气球已于前日下午被击中起火焚毁；又称我方炮兵已使敌方大量炮台哑火。那位编《连环漫画》的无名幽默家还能宣布：俄国人已在恰尔托维斯克攻克一处据点及若干重炮，据他解释，该地位于卢克东北四十四英里。马丁皮什曾观察到一次巨大的泛黄爆炸。星期二下午，我与德利上一趟火线办点先头差事，因为我们次日要换防边境团的一个营，接手弗里库尔公墓前方的防区。我们的炮兵正沿着整个乡间卖力地开火，几乎没遭到什么还击。
我们经过一处名为直布罗陀的洼地，那里藏着几门重炮。我闻到一股甜腻发呕的气味，归咎于那里长的茂盛的黄草，可德利说那是毒气弹久久不散的余味。当我们骑马下坡到 71 号点（北），那个熟门熟门的去处看上去与往常别无二致，只是沿途堆着一堆堆令人印象深刻的军需物资。德利说，他想这老地方过完这个礼拜就再也不一样了；而在我们看来，曼斯菲尔德和奥曼德还在我们连里时的那些旧日，似乎已成了值得抱憾回首的往事。布瓦弗朗赛防区本来像个村落，可我们很快就要把它抛在身后，到玫瑰壕、苹果巷、柳树大道那些地方去进行我们满怀报复的探查了。
上前沿那一路上，我们遇见一位参谋军官，脚蹬裁剪考究的马靴，显然急着要回到他的马那里去。云雀在高空欢唱，那惯常的、富于象征意味的猩红罂粟花从交通壕壕沿上慵懒地垂下来；可他从我们身边挤过，连个头都不点，因为这下午太嘈杂了，谈不上什么诗情画意——尽管云雀和罂粟花很受战地记者们的青睐。「那些大檐帽对这档子事倒确实知道一大堆吧，是不是，朱利安？」我问道。德利答道，他只盼他们自去年秋天让步兵不惜血本在卢斯自行「学习」之后，总算学到了点什么。「他们也得跟咱们一样，边干边学手艺啊，」他煞有智虑地补了一句。地平线那头看得见五只香肠气球，安安分分地拴在它们的母土上。我们的本分是巴望它们被毁掉，并相信军部情报处对此尽在掌握。在前沿战壕里我们干了些什么，我已记不得了；但在 71 号点（北）重新上马时，有两个列兵正起劲地、亲热地扭打在一起，一个把另一个的头夹在腋下。为什么我偏记得这个，却忘了那么多别的？

===Original===
Wednesday morning was miserably wet. Junior officers, being at a loss to know where to put themselves, were continually meeting one another along the muddy street, and gathering in groups to exchange cheerful remarks; there was little else to be done, and solitude produced the sinking sensation appropriate to the circumstances. The men were in their billets, and they too were keeping their spirits up as vocally as they could. At noon Barton came back from the Colonel’s final conference of company commanders. A couple of hours later the anticlimax arrived. We were told that all arrangements for the show were in temporary abeyance. A popular song, “All Dressed Up and Nowhere to Go,” provided the obvious comment, and our confidence in Operation Orders oozed away. Was it the wet weather, we wondered, or had the artillery preparation been inadequate? Uncertainty ended with an inanimate message; we were to go up to the line that evening. The attack was postponed forty-eight hours. No one knew why.
At five o’clock C Company fell in, about eighty strong. The men were without packs; they carried extra ammunition, two Mills bombs, two smoke helmets, and a waterproof sheet with jersey rolled inside; their emergency rations consisted of two tins of bully beef, eight hard biscuits, and canteen packed with grocery ration. In spite of the anticlimax (which had made us feel that perhaps this was only going to be a second edition of the Battle of Loos) my personal impression was that we were setting out for the other end of nowhere. I had slipped a book into my haversack and it was a comfort to be carrying it, for Thomas Hardy’s England was between its covers. But if any familiar quotation was in my mind during the bustle of departure, it may well have been “we brought nothing into this world, and it is certain we can carry nothing out of it.” We had trudged that way up to the Citadel and 71. North many times before; but never in such a bloodred light as now, when we halted with the sunset behind us and the whole sky mountainous with the magnificence of retreating rainclouds. Tours of trenches had been routine, with an ordinary chance of casualties. But this time we seemed to have left Morlancourt behind us forever, and even a single company of Flintshire Fusiliers (with a ten minute interval between it and B and D Companies) was justified in feeling that the eyes of Europe were upon it. As for myself, I felt nothing worth recording—merely a sense of being irrevocably involved in something bigger than had ever happened before. And the symbolism of the sunset was wasted on the rank and file, who were concerned with the not infrequent badness of their boots, the discomfort caused by perspiration, and the toils and troubles of keeping pace with what was required of them till further notice. By nine o’clock we had relieved the Border Regiment. The mud was bad, but the sky was clear. The bombardment went on steadily, with periods of intensity; but that infernal shindy was taken for granted and was an aid to optimism. I felt rather lonely without Durley, who had been left behind with the dozen officers who were in reserve.
New Trench, which we took over, had been a good deal knocked about, but we passed an unharassed night. We were opposite Sunken Road Trench, which was 300 yards away up a slope. Gaps had been cut in our wire for the attacking battalion to pass through. Early on the next afternoon Kinjack came up to inspect the gaps. With the assistance of his big periscope he soon discovered that the wire wasn’t properly cut. It must be done that night, he said. Barton brought me the news. I was huddled up in a little dog-kennel of a dugout, reading *Tess of the d’Urbervilles* and trying to forget about the shells which were hurrying and hurrooshing overhead. I was meditating about England, visualizing a grey day down in Sussex; dark green woodlands with pigeons circling above the treetops; dogs barking, cocks crowing, and all the casual tappings and twinklings of the countryside. I thought of the huntsman walking out in his long white coat with the hounds; of Parson Colwood pulling up weeds in his garden till teatime; of Captain Huxtable helping his men get in the last load of hay while a shower of rain moved along the blurred Weald below his meadows. It was for all that, I supposed, that I was in the front-line with soaked feet, trench-mouth, and feeling short of sleep, for the previous night had been vigilant though uneventful. Barton’s head and shoulders butting past the gas-blanket in the dugout doorway wrecked my reverie; he wanted me to come out and have a squint at the uncut wire, which was no daydream since it was going to affect the fortunes of a still undiminished New Army Battalion. Putting *Tess* in my pocket, I followed him to the fire-trench, which was cumbered with gas-cylinders and boxes of smoke-bombs. A smoke-cloud was to be let off later in the afternoon, for no special reason (except, perhaps, to make us cough and wipe our eyes, since what wind there was blew the smoke along our trench). Shells were banging away on the rising ground behind Fricourt and the low ridge of Contalmaison. A young yellowhammer was fluttering about in the trench, and I wondered how it had got there: it seemed out of place, perching on a body which lay trussed in a waterproof sheet. As for the gaps in the wire, they looked too bad for words and only one night remained for widening them.

===Chinese===
星期三早晨下着凄苦的雨。下级军官们无所适从，不知该把自己往哪儿搁，便不断在那条泥泞的街上碰头，三三两两聚起来彼此说几句打气的话；除此之外无事可做，而独处只会生出与此情此景相称的下坠之感。弟兄们在营房里，也在尽力用嗓门给自己提气。正午时分，巴顿从营长召集各连连长的最后一次会议上回来。两小时后，那场虎头蛇尾来了。我们被告知，这场戏的全部安排暂且搁置。一首流行歌曲《打扮停当却无处可去》便是再贴切不过的注脚，我们对作战命令的那份信心随之溜走。是天雨之故，还是炮火准备不足？我们纳闷。这场不确定以一条死气沉沉的命令告终：我们当晚就要上火线。进攻推迟四十八小时。没人知道为什么。
五点钟，C 连集合，约八十人。弟兄们没背背包，多带了弹药、两枚米尔斯手榴弹、两顶防烟头盔、一张防雨布（里面卷着一件线衣）；他们的应急口粮是两罐咸牛肉、八块硬饼干，饭盒里装着杂货配给。尽管这场虎头蛇尾使我们觉得，这说不定不过是卢斯战役的重演，可我私下的感觉是：我们这是在朝着一个前不着村后不着店的去处进发。我往背包里塞了一本书，带着它是一种安慰，因为那书皮之间是托马斯·哈代的英格兰。然而动身那阵忙乱之中，我脑子里若真有什么耳熟的引文，多半是「我们没有带什么到世上来，也不能带什么去」。这条路通往堡垒和 71 号点（北），我们从前走过许多趟了；却从没有一回走得像如今这般血红的暮色里——我们停下时，夕阳在身后，整片天空被退去雨云的壮丽堆成山峦。蹲战壕本是例行公事，伤亡的概率平平。可这一回，我们仿佛已把莫朗库尔永远抛在了身后；即便是弗林特郡燧发枪兵团区区一个连（它和 B 连、D 连之间隔着十分钟），也有理由觉得整个欧洲的目光都落在了它身上。至于我，我感到的没什么值得记录——只觉得已被卷入一件比此前发生的任何事都更大的事里，无可挽回。那落日的象征意味对当兵的则是白搭，他们操心的是自己那常常不顶事的靴子，是汗湿的不适，是赶上「在接到新的命令之前」所要求的那些事所带来的辛苦麻烦。到九点钟我们已换下了边境团。烂泥糟得很，可天是晴的。炮击稳稳地进行着，间或有更猛烈的时段；不过那一阵骇人的喧闹已被当作理所当然，反倒叫人乐观。没有德利在身边，我觉得有点孤单——他作为预备队的十几名军官之一被留在了后头。
我们接防的新壕被打得千疮百孔，可我们过了一个没受骚扰的夜。我们对面的沉路壕，在斜坡上三百码开外。我们自己的铁丝网上已剪开了几个缺口，供进攻的营通过。次日下午早些时候，金杰克上来察看这些缺口。借助他那架大潜望镜，他很快便发现铁丝没剪透。必须当夜干完，他说。巴顿把这话带给了我。我正蜷在一间狗窝般的小掩体里读《德伯家的苔丝》，想忘掉头顶上那些急急忙忙、呼啸而过的炮弹。我正出神地想着英格兰，在脑海里描绘苏塞克斯一个阴天——深绿的林地，鸽子在树梢上盘旋；狗吠、鸡鸣，还有乡间一切漫不经心的叩击与闪烁。我想起猎狐人穿着长长的白衣带着猎犬出门散步；想起科尔伍德牧师在自家花园里一直拔草拔到喝茶时分；想起赫克斯特布尔上尉帮他的雇工抢收最后一车干草，而一阵阵雨正沿着他草场下方那片朦胧的威尔德原野移动。我大约就是为了这一切，才在前沿战壕里泡着冰凉的脚、患着战壕口炎、困得睁不开眼的吧——上一夜虽无战事，却一夜警醒。巴顿的脑袋和肩膀从掩体门口的防毒气帘子边拱进来，搅了我的遐思；他要我出去瞧瞧那没剪透的铁丝网——这可不是白日梦，因为这事关系着一整个尚无减员的基钦纳新军营的命运。我把《苔丝》揣进口袋，跟着他来到前沿战斗壕，壕里堆满了毒气筒和烟幕弹箱。当天傍晚晚些时候要放一阵烟幕，没什么特别的理由（也许只是为了叫我们咳嗽抹眼泪，因为那点风正好把烟沿着我们这条壕吹）。弗里库尔身后的高地和康塔尔梅松那道低矮的山脊上，炮弹正乒乒乓乓地炸。一只小黄鹀在壕里扑腾，我纳闷它怎么跑到这儿来了：它栖在一具裹在防雨布里的尸体上，显得格格不入。至于铁丝网上的缺口，简直糟得无法形容，而只剩一夜工夫去把它们拓宽了。

===Original===
When I was back in the dugout I found myself fingering with pardonable pride my two pairs of wire-cutters from the Army and Navy Stores. It is possible that I overestimated their usefulness, but their presence did seem providential. Any fool could foresee what happened when troops got bunched up as they left their trench for a daylight attack; and I knew that, in spite of obstinate indentations to the source of supplies, we hadn’t got a decent pair of wire-cutters in the Battalion.
The big bugs back at Brigade and Divisional H.Q. were studying trench-maps with corrugated brows, for the “greatest battle in history” was timed to explode on Saturday morning. They were too busy to concern themselves with the ant-like activities of individual platoon commanders, and if they sent a sympathetic Staff Captain up to have a look round he couldn’t produce wire-cutters like a conjurer. But the fact remained that insistence on small (and often irrelevant) details was a proverbial characteristic of Staff organization, and on the eve of battle poor old Barton would probably be filling in a “return” stating how many men in his company had got varicose veins or married their deceased wife’s sister. In the meantime my casual purchase at “the Stores” had, perhaps, lessened the likelihood of the Manchesters getting bunched up and mown down by machine-guns when they went over the top to attack Sunken Road Trench. And what would the Manchesters say about the Flintshire Fusiliers if the wire wasn’t properly cut? So it seemed to me that our prestige as a Regular Battalion had been entrusted to my care on a front of several hundred yards.
Anyhow I was ready with my party as soon as it began to be dark. There were only eight of them (mostly from the other companies) and we were unable to do anything before midnight owing to rather lively shelling. I remember waiting there in the gloom and watching an unearthly little conflagration caused by some phosphorus bombs up the hill on our right. When we did get started I soon discovered that cutting tangles of barbed wire in the dark in a desperate hurry is a job that needs ingenuity, even when your wire-cutters have rubber-covered handles and are fresh from the Army and Navy Stores. More than once we were driven in by shells which landed in front of our trench (some of them were our own dropping short); two men were wounded and some of the others were reluctant to resume work. In the first greying of dawn only three of us were still at it. Kendle (a nineteen year old lance-corporal from my platoon) and Worgan (one of the tough characters of our company) were slicing away for all they were worth; but as the light increased I began to realize the unimpressive effect of the snippings and snatchings which had made such a mess of our leather gloves. We had been working three and a half hours but the hedge hadn’t suffered much damage, it seemed. Kendle disappeared into the trench and sauntered back to me, puffing a surreptitious Woodbine. I was making a last onslaught on a clawing thicket which couldn’t have been more hostile if it had been put there by the Germans. “We can’t do any more in this daylight,” said Kendle. I straightened my stiff and weary back and looked at him. His jaunty fag-smoking demeanour and freckled boyish face seemed to defy the darkness we had emerged from. That moment has impressed itself strongly on my memory; young Kendle was remarkable for his cheerfulness and courage, and his cheeky jokes. Many a company had its Kendle, until the war broke his spirit.… The large solicitous countenance of old man Barton now appeared above the parapet; with almost aunt-like anxiety he urged us to come in before we got sniped. But there had been no sniping that night, and the machine-gun at Wing Corner had been silent. Wing Corner was at the edge of the skeleton village of Fricourt, whose ruinous church tower was now distinctly visible against the dark green wood. The Germans, coming up from their foundering dugouts, would soon be staring grimly across at us while they waited for the relentless bombardment to begin again. As we got down into the trench young Kendle remarked that my new wire-cutters were a fair treat.
Next day, in warm and breezy weather, we moved to our battle-assembly position. For C Company “battle-assembly position” meant being broken up into ammunition-carrying parties, while Barton, Jenkins, and myself occupied an inglorious dugout in the support line. The Manchesters were due to relieve us at 9 a.m., but there was still no sign of them at 10:30, so Barton, who was in a free and easy mood (caused by our immunity from tomorrow’s attack) led the company away and left New Trench to look after itself. I had made up my mind to have another cut at the wire, which I now regarded with personal enmity, enjoying at the same time a self-admiring belief that much depended on my efforts. Worgan stayed behind with me. Kendle was unwilling to be left out of the adventure, but two of us would be less conspicuous than three, and my feeling for Kendle was somewhat protective. It was queer to be in an empty front-line trench on a fine morning, with everything quite peaceful after a violent early bombardment. Queerer still to be creeping about in the long grass (which might well have been longer, I thought), and shearing savagely at the tangles which had bewildered us in the dark but were now at our mercy. As Worgan said, we were giving it a proper haircut this journey.

===Chinese===
回到掩体后，我发觉自己正带着情有可原的自豪感摩挲那两把从海陆军百货店买来的铁丝剪。我也许高估了它们的用处，但有它们在手，的确像是天意。随便哪个傻瓜都预见得到，部队白天进攻、一出战壕便会挤作一团时会发生什么；而我知道，尽管向后勤来源一再开了不少领料单，我们全营连一把像样的铁丝剪都没有。
远在旅部和师部的那些大人物正皱着眉头研究战壕地图，因为「历史上最伟大的战役」定于星期六早晨引爆。他们忙得无暇顾及某个排长蚂蚁般的小动作，就算派一位有同情心的参谋上尉上来看一看，他也变不出铁丝剪来。可事实仍是：对小（往往也是不相干）细节的死磕，是参谋机构出了名的脾性，而在战斗前夜，可怜的老巴顿多半正在填一份「报表」，说明他连里有多少人得了静脉曲张，或娶了自己亡妻的妹妹。与此同时，我在「百货店」随手的这笔采购，也许已经降低了曼彻斯特团在跃出掩体进攻沉路壕时挤作一团、被机枪扫倒的可能性。再说，要是铁丝没剪透，曼彻斯特团会怎么说弗林特郡燧发枪兵团呢？于是乎，我们这个正规营的威望，似乎已在好几百码的正面交到了我手里来照管。
无论如何，天一擦黑我便带着我那队人马就绪了。总共只有八个人（多半来自其他连），而由于炮击颇为热闹，午夜之前我们什么都干不了。我记得在昏暗中干等，看着右首坡上一阵磷弹引起的不似人间的无名小火。等到当真动手时，我很快便发现：在黑地里十万火急地剪开一团团缠结的铁刺网，是件需要点巧劲儿的事，哪怕你的铁丝剪带胶皮把手、是刚从海陆军百货店出炉的新货。我们不止一次被落在壕前的炮弹赶回来（其中几发是我们自己的炮打近了）；两人负伤，剩下的人里有几个不情不愿再上手。到了天刚蒙蒙亮的时分，只剩我们三个还在干。肯德尔（我排里一名十九岁的下士）和沃根（连里的一条硬汉）正拼命地铰着；可随着天色渐亮，我开始意识到我们那一通又铰又扯——把我们皮手套糟蹋得不成样子——的成效实在不起眼。我们已经干了三个半钟头，可那道树篱似乎并没受多大损伤。肯德尔钻进战壕不见了，又踱回到我跟前，偷着吸一支「忍冬」牌香烟。我正对一丛张牙舞爪的荆棘做最后的猛攻——那荆棘就算原样搬到德国人那里都未必更敌视我们。「这光线下没法再干了，」肯德尔说。我直起僵硬疲惫的腰，看着他。他满不在乎抽着烟的神情、那张生着雀斑的男孩脸，仿佛在挑衅我们刚走出来的那片黑暗。那一刻深深印在了我的记忆里；年轻的肯德尔以他的乐观、勇气和他那些嬉皮笑脸的玩笑引人注目。每个连都有它的肯德尔，直到战争磨掉他的锐气。……老巴顿那张殷勤的大脸这时从胸墙后头探了出来；他几乎是像姨母那样焦急地催我们进去，免得挨冷枪。可那夜并无冷枪，翼角那边的机枪也一直没作声。翼角在已成骷髅的弗里库尔村边上，那座残破的教堂尖塔衬着深绿的树林，如今看得清清楚楚。德国人从他们摇摇欲坠的掩体里爬上来，马上便要阴沉地隔着这距离盯着我们，等着那无休无止的炮击再度开始。我们钻进战壕时，年轻的肯德尔说，我那把新铁丝剪可真是个好家伙。
次日，天气暖和，有风，我们移到了战斗集结位置。对 C 连而言，「战斗集结位置」意味着被拆成几支运弹小队，而巴顿、詹金斯和我则占着支援壕里一间毫不起眼的掩体。曼彻斯特团定于上午九点来换防，可十点半了还不见人影，于是心情又轻松又自在的巴顿（这份轻松自在源于我们明天不必参加进攻）便把连队带走，把新壕丢下不管了。我已打定主意再去铰一回那铁丝网——我如今把它视作私人仇敌，同时怀揣着一种颇为自赏的信念：成败多取决于我的努力。沃根留下来陪我。肯德尔不愿被这一桩奇遇落下，可两个人总比三个人不显眼，而我对肯德尔多少有一种保护的念头。在这样的大晴天置身于一条空无一人的前沿战壕，真是古怪——清晨那阵猛烈的炮击之后，一切竟如此平静。在没膝的深草里（我心想这草本可以更长些）匍匐穿行，对着那些在黑暗里叫我们一筹莫展、如今却任我们宰割的乱荆条猛剪，就更加古怪。用沃根的话说，这一回我们给它来了个正经的理发。

===Original===
Lying on my stomach I glanced now and again at the hostile slope which overlooked us, wondering whether anyone would take a potshot at us, or speculating on a possible visitation of machine-gun bullets from Wing Corner. Barton’s ignorance of what we were doing made it seem like an escapade, and the excitement was by no means disagreeable. It was rather like going out to weed a neglected garden after being warned that there might be a tiger among the gooseberry bushes. I should have been astonished if someone could have told me that I was an interesting example of human egotism. Yet such was the truth. I was cutting the wire by daylight because common sense warned me that the lives of several hundred soldiers might depend on it being done properly. I was excited and pleased with myself while I was doing it. And I had entirely forgotten that tomorrow Six Army Corps would attack, and whatever else happened, a tragic slaughter was inevitable. But if I had been intelligent enough to realize all that, my talents would have been serving in some more exalted place, probably Corps Intelligence Headquarters. Anyhow, at the end of an hour and a half the gaps were real good ones, and Barton’s red face and glittering pince-nez were bobbing up and down beyond the parapet with sotto voce incitements to prudence. Soon afterwards we dropped into the trench and the Manchesters began to arrive. It had been great fun, I said, flourishing my wire-cutters.
Early in the afternoon the Doctor bustled up from Battalion Headquarters to tell me that my M.C. had come through. This gratifying little event increased my blindness to the bloodstained future. Homeliness and humanity beamed in Barton’s congratulations; and the little doctor, who would soon be dressing the wounds of moaning men, unpicked his own faded medal-ribbon, produced a needle and thread, and sewed the white and purple portent on to my tunic. For the rest of the day and, indeed, for the remainder of my military career, the left side of my chest was more often in my mind than the right—a habit which was common to a multitude of wearers of Military Cross ribbons. Books about war psychology ought to contain a chapter on “medal-reflexes” and “decoration complexes.” Much might be written, even here, about medals and their stimulating effect on those who really risked their lives for them. But the safest thing to be said is that nobody knew how much a decoration was worth except the man who received it. Outwardly the distribution of them became more and more fortuitous and debased as the War went on; and no one knew it better than the infantry, who rightly insisted that medal-ribbons earned at the Base ought to be a different colour.
But I must return to June 30th, which ended with a sullen bombardment from the British guns and a congestion of troops in the support trench outside our dugout. They had lost their way, and I remember how the exhausted men propped themselves against the sides of the trench while their exasperated Adjutant and a confused civilian Colonel grumbled to Barton about the ambiguity of their operation orders. They were to attack on our left, and they vanished in that direction, leaving me with my Military Cross and a foreboding that disaster awaited them. Since they came within the limited zone of my observations I can record the fact that they left their trench early next morning at a wrong zero hour and got badly cut up by the artillery support which ought to have made things easy for them.

===Chinese===
我趴在地上，时不时瞥一眼俯瞰着我们的那道敌方斜坡，纳闷会不会有人朝我们放一记冷枪，或琢磨着翼角那边会不会赏我们一梭子机枪弹。巴顿对我们所做的事一无所知，倒让这事像一桩越轨的小冒险，而这份刺激并不叫人不快。那情形有点像被警告说醋栗丛里可能藏着一只老虎，却仍出去给一片荒废的园子除草。若有人能告诉我，我正是一个人类自大心理的有趣标本，我准会大吃一惊。然而事实正是如此。我白天铰铁丝，是因为常识提醒我，几百号士兵的性命可能取决于这事是否办得妥帖。我干的时候又兴奋又得意。而我已把明天将有六个军发起进攻、无论怎样都将是一场惨烈屠杀这件事，忘得一干二净。可我若有那份聪明能想到这一切，我这本事早该在某个更高的去处效力了，多半是军部情报处。无论如何，一个半钟头下来，那几个缺口当真铰得不错了；巴顿涨红的脸和亮闪闪的夹鼻眼镜在胸墙那头一上一下地探着，压低嗓子劝我们小心。不久我们便跳进战壕，曼彻斯特团开始抵达。我挥着我的铁丝剪说，可真过瘾啊。
下午早些时候，军医官从营部兴冲冲地跑来告诉我，我的军功十字勋章（M.C.）批下来了。这件叫人高兴的小事，越发叫我看不见那血腥的未来。巴顿的道贺里透着家人的热忱与人情味；而那位个头不大的军医——不久便要为呻吟的伤员们裹伤——把自己那根褪色的勋带拆下来，摸出针线，把这白紫相间的不祥之物缝到了我的军装上衣上。从那天往后的余生，事实上在我军旅生涯剩下的全部时日里，我胸膛左侧比右侧更频繁地浮上心头——这是众多军功十字勋带佩戴者的通病。论战争心理学的书里，本当有一章专论「勋章反射」与「勋章情结」。即便在这儿，关于勋章、关于它对那些当真为之冒过命的人的刺激作用，也大可写上许多。可最稳妥的说法是：一枚勋章到底值多少，只有领受它的人自己心里有数。表面上看，随着战事推移，勋章的颁发越发随意、越发贬值；这一点谁也没有步兵更清楚，他们理直气壮地坚持说，在后方基地挣来的勋带应当是另一种颜色。
可我必须回到六月三十日。这一天以英军大炮一阵闷声不乐的炮击告终，还有我们掩体外支援壕里一支部队的拥堵。他们迷了路，我还记得那些精疲力竭的士兵怎样斜靠在壕壁上，而他们那位恼怒的副官和一位不知所措的平民事出身的上校，向巴顿抱怨他们的作战命令含糊不清。他们要在我们左翼进攻，说罢便朝那个方向消失，留下我和我的军功十字勋章，以及一种大祸将临他们的预感。既然他们落在我有限的观察范围之内，我便可记下这样一桩事实：他们次日清早在一个错误的零时跃出掩体，被本应为他们扫清道路的炮火支援狠狠剁了一顿。

### II / 二

===Original===
On July the first the weather, after an early morning mist, was of the kind commonly called heavenly. Down in our frowsty cellar we breakfasted at six, unwashed and apprehensive. Our table, appropriately enough, was an empty ammunition-box. At six-forty-five the final bombardment began, and there was nothing for us to do except sit round our candle until the tornado ended. For more than forty minutes the air vibrated and the earth rocked and shuddered. Through the sustained uproar the tap and rattle of machine-guns could be identified; but except for the whistle of bullets no retaliation came our way until a few 5.9 shells shook the roof of our dugout. Barton and I sat speechless, deafened and stupefied by the seismic state of affairs, and when he lit a cigarette the match flame staggered crazily. Afterwards I asked him what he had been thinking about. His reply was “Carpet slippers and kettle-holders.” My own mind had been working in much the same style, for during that cannonading cataclysm the following refrain was running in my head:

> They come as a boon and a blessing to men,

 The Something, the Owl, and the Waverley Pen.

For the life of me I couldn’t remember what the first one was called. Was it the Shakespeare? Was it the Dickens? Anyhow it was an advertisement which I’d often seen in smoky railway stations. Then the bombardment lifted and lessened, our vertigo abated, and we looked at one another in dazed relief. Two Brigades of our Division were now going over the top on our right. Our Brigade was to attack “when the main assault had reached its final objective.” In our fortunate role of privileged spectators Barton and I went up the stairs to see what we could from Kingston Road Trench. We left Jenkins crouching in a corner, where he remained most of the day. His haggard blinking face haunts my memory. He was an example of the paralysing effect which such an experience could produce on a nervous system sensitive to noise, for he was a good officer both before and afterwards. I felt no sympathy for him at the time, but I do now. From the support trench, which Barton called “our opera box,” I observed as much of the battle as the formation of the country allowed, the rising ground on the right making it impossible to see anything of the attack towards Mametz. A small shiny black notebook contains my pencilled particulars, and nothing will be gained by embroidering them with afterthoughts. I cannot turn my field-glasses on to the past.

===Chinese===
七月一日这天，清晨一阵薄雾散去后，天气便是那种通常被称作「天堂般」的好天。在下面那间闷浊的地下室里，我们六点钟吃早饭，没洗漱，满心忐忑。我们的餐桌——再合适不过——是一只空的弹药箱。六点四十五分，最后的炮击开始，我们除了围着我们的蜡烛坐着、等那场龙卷风过去，别无他事可做。四十多分钟里，空气震颤，大地摇摆战栗。透过那持续不断的轰鸣，可以辨认出机枪的嗒嗒与咯咯声；可除了子弹的呼啸，并没有什么还击落到我们头上，直到几发五点九英寸的炮弹震动了我们掩体的顶。巴顿和我一言不发地坐着，被这地震般的局面震得耳聋、震得发懵；他点香烟时，那火柴的火苗摇摇晃晃。事后我问他当时在想什么。他答道：「绒毡拖鞋和水壶把手。」我自己的脑子运转得也差不多是这副模样，因为在那场炮火的天灾里，我脑子里一直在跑着这么一段广告词：

> 它们是世人的福分与恩赐，

 那个什么、猫头鹰，还有韦弗利牌钢笔。

我怎么也想不起头一样东西到底叫什么。是莎士比亚？是狄更斯？无论如何，那是一则我常在烟雾缭绕的火车站见到的广告。接着炮火延伸、减弱，我们的眩晕也消退了，我们恍然地、如释重负地面面相觑。此时我师的两个旅正在我们右翼跃出掩体。我旅则要等「主攻达到其最终目标时」再行进攻。巴顿和我以享有特权的旁观者这一幸运身份，登上台阶，到金斯敦路战壕去看我们能看到的。我们把詹金斯丢在一个角落里蜷着，他那一整天大部分时间都待在那儿。他那憔悴、眨巴着的脸至今萦绕在我记忆里。他正是这类经历可能对一个对噪声敏感的神经系统造成瘫痪性影响的一个范例，因为他在此之前和之后都是个好军官。当时我对他没有一丝同情，可如今有了。从那条被巴顿称作「我们的包厢」的支援壕里，我把地形所允许我看到的战斗都看了——右侧的高地使我根本看不见朝马梅斯方向的进攻。一本亮闪闪的黑色小本子里记着我用铅笔记下的细节，事后用回想来添油加醋并无益处。我没法把望远镜对准过去。

===Original===
> 7:45. The barrage is now working to the right of Fricourt and beyond. I can see the 21st Division advancing about three-quarters of a mile away on the left and a few Germans coming to meet them, apparently surrendering. Our men in small parties (not extended in line) go steadily on to the German front-line. Brilliant sunshine and a haze of smoke drifting along the landscape. Some Yorkshires a little way below on the left, watching the show and cheering as if at a football match. The noise almost as bad as ever.
> 9:30. Came back to dugout and had a shave. 21st Division still going across the open, apparently without casualties. The sunlight flashes on bayonets as the tiny figures move quietly forward and disappear beyond mounds of trench debris. A few runners come back and ammunition parties go across. Trench-mortars are knocking hell out of Sunken Road trench and the ground where the Manchester will attack soon. Noise not so bad now and very little retaliation.
> 9:50. Fricourt half-hidden by clouds of drifting smoke, blue, pinkish and grey. Shrapnel bursting in small bluish-white puffs with tiny flashes. The birds seem bewildered; a lark begins to go up and then flies feebly along, thinking better of it. Others flutter above the trench with querulous cries, weak on the wing. I can see seven of our balloons, on the right. On the left our men still filing across in twenties and thirties. Another huge explosion in Fricourt and a cloud of brown-pink smoke. Some bursts are yellowish.
> 10:50. I can see the Manchesters down in New Trench, getting ready to go over. Figures filing down the trench. Two of them have gone out to look at our wire gaps! Have just eaten my last orange.… I am staring at a sunlit picture of Hell, and still the breeze shakes the yellow weeds, and the poppies glow under Crawley Ridge where some shells fell a few minutes ago. Manchesters are sending forward some scouts. A bayonet glitters. A runner comes back across the open to their Battalion Headquarters, close here on the right, 21st Division still trotting along the skyline toward La Boisselle. Barrage going strong to the right of Contalmaison Ridge. Heavy shelling toward Mametz.
> 12:15. Quieter the last two hours. Manchesters still waiting. Germans putting over a few shrapnel shells. Silly if I got hit! Weather cloudless and hot. A lark singing confidently overhead.
> 1:30. Manchesters attack at 2:30. Mametz and Montauban reported taken. Mametz consolidated.
> 2:30. Manchesters left New Trench and apparently took Sunken Road Trench, bearing rather to the right. Could see about 400. Many walked casually across with sloped arms. There were about forty casualties on the left (from machine-gun in Fricourt). Through my glasses I could see one man moving his left arm up and down as he lay on his side; his face was a crimson patch. Others lay still in the sunlight while the swarm of figures disappeared over the hill. Fricourt was a cloud of pinkish smoke. Lively machine-gun fire on the far side of the hill. At 2:50 no one to be seen in No Man’s Land except the casualties (about halfway across). Our dugout shelled again since 2:30.
> 5:00. I saw about thirty of our A Company crawl across to Sunken Road from New Trench. Germans put a few big shells on the Cemetery and traversed Kingston Road with machine-gun. Manchester wounded still out there. Remainder of A Company went across—about 100 altogether. Manchesters reported held up in Bois Français Support. Their Colonel went across and was killed.
> 8:00. Staff Captain of our Brigade has been along. Told Barton that Seventh Division has reached its objectives with some difficulty, except on this Brigade front. Manchesters are in trouble, and Fricourt attack has failed. Several hundred prisoners brought in on our sector.
> 9:30. Our A Company holds Rectangle and Sunken Road. Jenkins gone off in charge of a carrying-party. Seemed all right again. C Company now reduced to six runners, two stretcher-bearers, Company-Sergeant-Major, signallers, and Barton’s servant. Flook away on carrying-party. Sky cloudy westward. Red sunset. Heavy gunfire on the left.
> 2:30. (Next afternoon.) Adjutant has just been up here, excited, optimistic, and unshaven. He went across last night to ginger up A Company who did very well, thanks to the bombers. About forty casualties; only four killed. Fricourt and Rose Trench occupied this morning without resistance. I am now lying out in front of our trench in the long grass, basking in sunshine where yesterday there were bullets. Our new front-line on the hill is being shelled. Fricourt is full of troops wandering about in search of souvenirs. The village was a ruin and is now a dust heap. A gunner (Forward Observation Officer) has just been along here with a German helmet in his hand. Said Fricourt is full of dead; he saw one officer lying across a smashed machine-gun with his head bashed in—“a fine looking chap,” he said, with some emotion, which rather surprised me.
> 8:15. Queer feeling, seeing people moving about freely between here and Fricourt. Dumps being made. Shacks and shelters being put up under skeleton trees and all sorts of transport arriving at Cemetery Cross Roads. We stay here till tomorrow morning. Feel a bit of a fraud.

===Chinese===
> 七点四十五分。弹幕现正向弗里库尔以右及更远处推进。我看得见左方约四分之三英里外第二十一师在推进，还有几个德国人迎上去，像是要投降。我方的人三五成群（并非散兵线）稳步推进至德军前沿。阳光灿烂，一缕烟霞沿着地貌飘移。左下方不远处有些约克郡团的弟兄在看热闹，像看足球赛似的欢呼。嘈杂声几乎和先前一样糟。
> 九点三十分。回到掩体刮了脸。第二十一师仍在开阔地上推进，似乎无伤亡。阳光在刺刀上闪烁，那些小小的身影静静地向前移动，消失在战壕瓦砾堆成的土丘之后。几个传令兵跑回来，运弹小队穿过去。战壕迫击炮正把沉路壕、以及曼彻斯特团即将进攻的那片地轰得鬼哭狼嚎。眼下嘈杂声小些了，还击也微乎其微。
> 九点五十分。弗里库尔半隐在飘移的烟团里，蓝、粉、灰相间。榴霰弹炸成小小的蓝白色烟团，夹着细小的闪光。鸟儿们似是给弄懵了；一只云雀刚开始往上爬，又怯生生地横飞开去，改了主意。别的在战壕上空扑腾，叫声哀怨，翅膀发软。右边我看得见我们的七只气球。左边，我方的人仍二十三十地鱼贯穿过。弗里库尔又一次巨大的爆炸，腾起一团褐粉色的烟。有几发炸出来是泛黄的。
> 十点五十分。我看得见曼彻斯特团在新壕里准备跃出。身影在壕里鱼贯而下。他们中有两个人竟走出去看我们剪的铁丝缺口！我刚吃完最后一只橙子。……我正凝视着一幅阳光普照下的地狱图，而微风仍在摇动黄色的野草，罂粟花在克劳利山脊下发光——几分钟前那里还落下过几发炮弹。曼彻斯特团派出了一些侦察兵。一把刺刀闪闪发亮。一名传令兵从开阔地跑回他们的营部——就在这儿右边不远处。第二十一师仍沿着天际小跑着朝拉布瓦塞尔去。弹幕在康塔尔梅松山脊右侧打得正欢。马梅斯方向重炮轰击。
> 十二点十五分。过去两小时安静些了。曼彻斯特团仍在等。德国人打过几发榴霰弹来。我要是这会儿挨一下未免太蠢！天气无云、炎热。一只云雀在头顶自信地唱着。
> 一点三十分。曼彻斯特团两点半进攻。据报道，马梅斯与蒙托邦已被攻克。马梅斯已巩固。
> 两点三十分。曼彻斯特团跃出新壕，看来已拿下沉路壕，略微偏右。约能看到四百人。许多人端着枪随随便便就走过去了。左翼约四十人伤亡（来自弗里库尔的机枪）。透过望远镜我看见一个人侧躺着，左臂一上一下地动；他的脸是一片殷红。其他人一动不动地躺在阳光里，而那一群身影消失在山头那边。弗里库尔是一团粉色的烟。山那边机枪打得热闹。两点五十分，无人区里除了伤亡者（约在中途）已看不见人影。我们的掩体自两点半起又挨了炮。
> 五点整。我看见我们 A 连约三十人从新壕爬向沉路。德国人往公墓落下几发大口径炮弹，并用机枪扫射金斯敦路。曼彻斯特团的伤员还躺在那儿。A 连余部也过去了——总共约一百人。据报道曼彻斯特团在布瓦弗朗赛支援壕受阻。他们的营长亲自过去，阵亡了。
> 八点整。我旅的参谋上尉来过。告诉巴顿第七师除我旅这一正面外，均已较艰难地达到目标。曼彻斯特团陷入困境，对弗里库尔的进攻失败。我们这个防区带进来几百名俘虏。
> 九点三十分。我们 A 连据守矩形据点和沉路。詹金斯带一支运输队走了。看上去又没事了。C 连如今只剩下六名传令兵、两名担架兵、连队军士长、信号兵和巴顿的勤务兵。弗卢克随运输队去了。西边天空多云。红日落。左方重炮声。
> 两点三十分。（次日下午。）副官刚从上面来，激动、乐观、胡子拉碴。他昨夜过去给 A 连打气，他们干得非常漂亮，多亏了投弹兵。约四十人伤亡；仅四人阵亡。今晨已无抵抗地占领弗里库尔和玫瑰壕。我现在躺在我们战壕前的深草里，晒着太阳——昨天这儿还是子弹横飞之地。我们在山上的新前沿正挨着炮击。弗里库尔满是四处游荡找纪念品的士兵。这村子本是废墟，如今成了土堆。一名炮兵（前进观测官）刚从这儿过，手里拿着一顶德国钢盔。他说弗里库尔遍地是死人；他看见一名军官横在一挺砸烂的机枪上，脑袋被砸烂了——「一个挺英俊的小伙，」他带着几分动情说，这倒颇叫我意外。
> 八点十五分。看到人在这一带与弗里库尔之间自由走动，感觉怪怪的。物资堆在堆放点。骨架般的树下搭起棚屋与掩体，各种运输车辆抵达公墓十字路口。我们在这儿待到明早。觉得自己有点像个骗子。

### III / 三

===Original===
Early next morning we took leave of our subterranean sanctuary in Kingston Road, joined the Battalion at 71. North, and marched a couple of miles to a concentration point between Mametz and Carnoy. There, in a wide hollow, the four units of our Brigade piled arms, lay down on the grass, and took their boots off. Most of them had been without sleep for two nights and the immediate forecast was “murky.” But every man had a waterproof sheet to sit on, helmets were exchanged for woollen caps, unshaven faces felt gratitude for generous sunshine, and bare feet stretched contented toes. Our Division having done well, there was a confident feeling in the air. But we had heard of partial and complete failures in other parts of the line, and the name of Gommecourt had already reached us with ugly implications. It was obvious that some of us would soon be lacing up our boots for the last time, and the current rumour, “They say we’ve got to attack some Wood or other,” could not fail to cause an uneasy visceral sensation. However one felt that big things were happening, and my Military Cross was a comfort to me. It was a definite personal possession to be lived up to, I thought. I watched the men dozing in odd ungainly attitudes, half listened to their talk about the souvenirs they’d picked up in the German trenches, or stared at some captured guns being brought down the lane which led to Mametz.
A few of the men were wandering about, and my meditations were disturbed by Kinjack, who had given orders that everyone was to rest all day. “Tell those men to lie down,” he shouted, adding—as he returned to his bivouac on the slope—“The bastards’ll be glad to before they’re much older.” It was believed that his brusque manners had prevented him getting promotion, but everyone knew that it would be a bad day for the Battalion when Kinjack got his Brigade.
Evening fell calm and overcast, with a blurred orange sunset. Sitting among rank grass and thistles I stared pensively down at the four Battalions grouped in the hollow. Thin smoke rose from the little bivouac fires which had been used for tea making; among the gruff murmuring which came up with the smoke, the nasal chant of a mouth organ did its best to “keep the home fires burning.” In front of the hollow the open ground sloped treeless to Bazentin Ridge, dull green and striped with seams of trenches cut in the chalky soil. Field-guns were firing on the right and some aeroplanes hummed overhead. Beyond that hill our future awaited us. There could be no turning back from it.… I would have liked Flook to bring me an orange, but he was away with Jenkins and the carrying-party, and oranges were almost as remote as the sunset. Poor Flook will be awfully worried about not being with his officer-bloke, I thought, imagining his stolid red face puffing along under a box of ammunition.… I went down the hill just in time to hear that we’d got orders to go up and dig a trench somewhere in front of Mametz.

===Chinese===
第二天清早，我们告别了金斯敦路那座地下的庇护所，在 71 号点（北）与营队会合，朝马梅斯和卡尔努瓦之间的一处集结点行进了两英里。在那里，一片开阔的洼地里，我旅的四个单位架起了枪，在草地上躺下，脱了靴子。他们大多数已两夜没合眼，眼前的前景「昏暗」。可每人都有张防雨布当坐垫，钢盔换成绒线帽，没刮胡子的脸承着慷慨阳光的恩惠，光脚丫舒坦地伸展开脚趾。我师既已打得不坏，空气里便有一种自信。可我们也听说了别处战线局部或彻底的失利，戈姆库尔这个名字已带着不祥的意味传到了我们耳中。显然我们中有的人很快就要最后一次系上靴带了，而眼下这条传闻——「他们说要我们去进攻什么树林来着」——不能不叫人腹中一阵发紧。然而人总觉得有大事件正在发生，而我的军功十字勋章于我是一种慰藉。它是一件确凿的、有待我配得上的个人所有物，我想。我看着弟兄们以种种古怪笨拙的姿势打盹，半听半不听他们谈着从德国人战壕里捡来的纪念品，或望着几门缴获的炮正沿着通向马梅斯的小路被拖下去。
有几个弟兄在四处闲荡，我的沉思被金杰克打断了——他已下过令，所有人都要歇上一整天。「叫那些人躺下，」他嚷道，又补了一句——这时他正回坡上他的棚屋去——「这帮狗娘养的，用不了多久就会巴望能躺一躺了。」人们相信，是他那副粗鲁的做派使他一直升不上去；可人人都知道，等金杰克当上旅长那一天，对全营便是倒霉的日子。
傍晚降临，沉静而阴天，落日是一片模糊的橘红。坐在繁茂的乱草和蓟丛间，我若有所思地俯瞰着聚在洼地里的那四个营。煮茶用的小棚屋火堆升起淡淡炊烟；伴着随烟升起的粗哑低语，一只口琴用鼻音般的调子卖力地「让家里的炉火燃着」。洼地前方，开阔地光秃秃地向巴藏丹山脊倾斜下去，暗绿，被一道道挖在白垩土里的战壕纹出条条缝隙。野战炮在右方开火，几架飞机在头顶嗡嗡作响。那座山的那一边，我们的未来在等着我们。再没有回头的路了。……我本想叫弗卢克给我拿只橙子来，可他随詹金斯和运输队去了，橙子几乎和落日一样遥远。可怜的弗卢克此刻一定急得不行，为不能待在他「军官伙计」身边，我想，一面想象着他那张木讷的红脸，在一箱弹药底下气喘吁吁地赶路。……我下山时，正好赶上听说我们接到命令，要上去在马梅斯前方某处挖一条战壕。

===Original===
For a few minutes the hollow was full of the subdued hubbub and commotion of troops getting into their equipment. Two battalions had been called out; the Royal Irish moved off ahead of us. As we went up the lane toward Mametz I felt that I was leaving all my previous war experience behind me. For the first time I was among the debris of an attack. After going a very short distance we made the first of many halts, and I saw, arranged by the roadside, about fifty of the British dead. Many of them were Gordon Highlanders. There were Devons and South Staffordshires among them, but they were beyond regimental rivalry now—their fingers mingled in bloodstained bunches, as though acknowledging the companionship of death. There was much battle gear lying about, and some dead horses. There were rags and shreds of clothing, boots riddled and torn, and when we came to the old German front-line, a sour pervasive stench which differed from anything my nostrils had known before. Meanwhile we made our continually retarded progress up the hill, and I scrutinized these battle effects with partially complacent curiosity. I wanted to be able to say that I had seen “the horrors of war”; and here they were, nearly three days old.
No one in the glumly halted column knew what was delaying us. After four hours we had only progressed 1,500 yards and were among some ruined buildings on the outskirts of the village. I have dim remembrance of the strangeness of the place and our uneasy dawdling in its midnight desolation. Kinjack was somewhere ahead of us with a guide. The guide, having presumably lost his way, was having a much hotter time than we were. So far we had done nothing except file past a tool-dump, where the men had collected picks, shovels, coils of wire, and corkscrew stakes. At 2 a.m. we really began to move, passing through Mametz and along a communication trench. There were some badly mangled bodies about. Although I’d been with the Battalion nearly eight months, these were the first newly dead Germans I had seen. It gave me a bit of a shock when I saw, in the glimmer of daybreak, a dumpy, baggy-trousered man lying half sideways with one elbow up as if defending his lolling head; the face was grey and waxen, with a stiff little moustache; he looked like a ghastly doll, grotesque and undignified. Beside him was a scorched and mutilated figure whose contorted attitude revealed bristly cheeks, a grinning blood-smeared mouth and clenched teeth. These dead were unlike our own; perhaps it was the strange uniform, perhaps their look of butchered hostility. Anyhow they were one with the little trench direction boards whose unfamiliar lettering seemed to epitomize that queer feeling I used to have when I stared across No Man’s Land, ignorant of the humanity which was on the other side.
Leaving the trench we filed across the open hillside with Mametz Wood looming on the opposite slope. It was a dense wood of old trees and undergrowth. The Staff of our Division had assumed that the near side was now unoccupied. But as soon as we had halted in a sunken road an uproar broke out at the edge of the wood, which demonstrated with machine-guns and bombs that the Staff had guessed wrong.
Kinjack promptly ordered A Company forward to get in touch with the Royal Irish, whose covering parties were having a bombing fight in the Wood. Our men were fired on as they went along the road and forced to take cover in a quarry. I remember feeling nervous and incompetent while I wondered what on earth I should do if called on to lead a party out “into the blue.” But the clouds were now reddening, and we were fed-up with the whole performance. Messages went back and our guns chucked a lot of shrapnel which burst over the near side of the Wood and enabled the Irish to withdraw. We then, as Kinjack described it afterwards, “did a guy”; but it was a slow one for we weren’t back at our camping ground until 8:30 a.m. The expedition had lasted nearly eleven hours and we had walked less than three miles, which was about all we could congratulate ourselves on. The Royal Irish had had sixty casualties; we had one killed and four wounded. From a military point of view the operations had enabled the Staff to discover that Mametz Wood was still full of Germans, so that it was impossible to dig a trench on the bluff within fifty yards of it, as had been suggested. It was obvious now that a few strong patrols could have clarified the situation more economically than 1,000 men with picks and shovels. The necessary information had been obtained, however, and the Staff could hardly be expected to go up and investigate such enigmas for themselves. But this sort of warfare was a new experience for all of us, and the difficulties of extempore organization must have been considerable.

===Chinese===
洼地里一时充满了部队整装的低沉喧闹和忙乱。两个营被点将出动；皇家爱尔兰团走在我们前头。沿那条小路朝马梅斯上行时，我感到我正把此前全部的战争经历抛在身后。我头一回置身于一场进攻的残骸之中。走出很短一段距离，我们便开始了许多次停顿中的第一次，我看到路旁整整齐齐码着约五十具英军尸体。其中许多是戈登高地团的。中间也有德文郡团和南斯塔福德郡团的，可他们已超越了团队之间的较量——他们的手指血迹斑斑地交缠成一簇簇，仿佛在认领死亡这种伙伴关系。四下里散落着许多战斗装备，还有几匹死马。有破布和衣衫的碎条，有被打得千疮百孔、撕裂的靴子；而待我们走到那条旧的德军前沿战壕时，一股酸腐弥漫、无孔不入的恶臭扑鼻而来，与我这双鼻孔从前所识的任何气味都不同。与此同时，我们在这上山路上不断地走走停停，我带着几分自满的好奇打量着这些战场的痕迹。我想要能够说一句：我见过「战争的恐怖」；而它们就在眼前，已近三天之久。
这支阴郁地停住脚步的队伍里，没人知道是什么耽搁了我们。四个钟头下来我们才推进了一千五百码，停在了村口几栋残破的建筑之间。我依稀记得那地方的怪异，记得我们在它午夜般的荒凉里不安地磨蹭。金杰克带着一名向导在我们前头某处。那向导，想必是迷了路，正挨着比我们更难受的时光。到此刻为止，我们什么也没干，只是从一个工具堆旁鱼贯而过——弟兄们在那里领了镐、锹、一卷卷铁丝和螺旋形桩子。凌晨两点我们才当真动起来，穿过马梅斯，沿着一条交通壕前行。四下里有一些被毁得惨不忍睹的尸体。我随营队已近八个月，可这些还是我头一次见到的、新死的德国人。晨光熹微中我看见一个矮胖、穿着灯笼裤的男人半侧着躺，一只胳膊支起来，仿佛在护着他那颗耷拉的头，不觉心头一震；那张脸灰白蜡黄，硬硬地蓄着一撮小胡子；他像一具可怖的玩偶，怪诞而失了尊严。他旁边是一具烧焦、残缺的身影，那扭曲的姿态露出扎着胡茬的脸颊、龇着牙的血污的嘴和咬紧的牙关。这些死人跟我们自己的不一样；也许是那陌生的军装，也许是他们那副被宰割的敌意的神情。无论如何，他们与那些小小的战壕方向牌融为一体，牌上那些陌生的字母，仿佛正凝缩着我从前隔着无人区张望时常有的那种怪异之感——那时我对另一边的人性一无所知。
出了战壕，我们鱼贯穿过开阔的山坡，马梅斯树林在对面山坡上阴森地隆起。那是一片老树与灌木丛的密林。我师的参谋们原以为靠近我们这一侧如今已无人占领。可我们刚在一条沉路上停下，树林边缘便炸开了锅，用机枪和手榴弹向他们证明：参谋们猜错了。
金杰克当即命令 A 连前出与皇家爱尔兰团取得联系——他们的掩护队在林子里正打着一场手榴弹战。我们的人沿路行进时遭到射击，被迫躲进一处采石场。我记得自己当时又紧张又无措，一边纳闷：万一叫我去带一队人「跃入未知的蓝色」里去，我究竟该怎么办。可此时云层正泛红，而我们已对整桩事厌烦透顶。消息传回去，我方大炮甩过去一批榴霰弹，在树林近侧炸开，使爱尔兰人得以撤出。接着我们——金杰克后来形容说——「溜之大吉」了；不过溜得慢吞吞的，因为直到早晨八点半我们才回到营地。这次出征历时近十一个钟头，走了不到三英里，这大概便是我们唯一可聊以自慰之处。皇家爱尔兰团伤亡六十人；我们一人阵亡、四人负伤。从军事角度看，这一系列行动使参谋部得以查明：马梅斯树林里仍满是德国人，因此如先前所建议、在距林子五十码以内的崖上挖一条战壕是不可能的。如今显而易见，几支强有力的巡逻队本可比一千个拿镐扛锹的人更经济地澄清局面。然而必要的信息既已获得，也不能指望参谋们亲自上去解开这些谜团。不过这种打法对我们所有人都是头一遭，临时组织的难处想必不小。

===Original===
During the morning we were a silent battalion, except for snoring. Some eight-inch guns were firing about 200 yards from the hollow, but our slumbers were inured to noises which would have kept us wide awake in civilian life. We were lucky to be dry, for the sky was overcast. At one o’clock our old enemy the rain arrived in full force. Four hours’ deluge left the troops drenched and disconsolate, and then Dottrell made one of his providential appearances with the rations. Dixies of hot tea, and the rum issue, made all the difference to our outlook. It seemed to me that the Quartermaster symbolized that region of temporary security which awaited us when our present adversities were ended. He had a cheery word for everyone, and his jocularity was judicious. What were the jokes he made, I wonder? Their helpfulness must be taken for granted. I can only remember his chaffing an officer named Woolman, whose dumpy figure had bulged abnormally since we came up to the battle area. Woolman’s young lady in England had sent him a bulletproof waistcoat; so far it had only caused its wearer to perspire profusely; and although reputed to be extremely vulnerable, it had inspired a humorist in his company to refer to him as “Asbestos Bill.”
Time seems to have obliterated the laughter of the war. I cannot hear it in my head. How strange such laughter would sound, could I but recover it as it was on such an evening as I am describing, when we all knew that we’d got to do an attack that night; for shortsighted Barton and the other company commanders had just returned from a reconnaissance of the ground which had left them little wiser than when they started. In the meantime we’d got some rum inside us and could find something to laugh about. Our laughter leapt up, like the flames of camp fires in the dusk, soon to be stamped out, or extinguished by our impartial opponent the rain. The consoling apparition of Dottrell departed, and I don’t suppose he did much laughing once he was alone with his homeward rattling limbers.
Zero hour was forty-five minutes after midnight. Two companies were to attack on a 600 yard front and the Royal Irish were to do the same on our right. Barton’s company was to be in reserve; owing to the absence of the carrying-party it could only muster about thirty men.
At nine o’clock we started up the sunken road to Mametz. As a result of the rain, yesterday’s dry going had been trodden to a quagmire. Progress was slow owing to the congestion of troops in front. We had only a couple of thousand yards to go, but at one time it seemed unlikely that the assaulting companies would be in position by zero hour. It was pitch dark as we struggled through the mud, and we got there with fifteen minutes to spare, having taken three and a half hours to go a mile and a quarter.
Barton arranged his men along a shallow support trench on the edge of Bottom Wood, which was a copse just to the left of the ground we’d visited the night before. Almost at once the short preliminary bombardment began and the darkness became diabolic with the din and flash of the old old story. Not for the first time—I wondered whether shells ever collided in the air. Silence and suspense came after. Barton and I talked in undertones; he thought I’d better borrow his electric torch and find out the nearest way to Battalion Headquarters.

===Chinese===
整个上午我们营鸦雀无声，除了鼾声。几门八英寸炮在离洼地约两百码处开火，可我们的睡眠早已习惯了这些在平民生活中本会叫我们彻夜难眠的声响。我们能保持身上干燥已是幸运，因为天阴着。一点钟，我们的老对头雨倾盆而至。四个钟头的大雨把部队浇得湿透、心灰，这时多特雷尔押着口粮又一次如天兵天将般现身了。几饭盒热茶，加上朗姆酒配额，使我们的心境大为改观。在我看来，这位军需官象征着当我们眼下的苦难终了之后、等候着我们的那片短暂的安稳之地。他对人人都有句吉利话，他的诙谐也恰到好处。他都讲了些什么笑话呢，我纳闷。那些笑话的帮衬之效，只能视为理所当然了。我只记得他打趣一位名叫伍尔曼的军官——自打我们开到战区，他那矮胖的身材已鼓胀得异乎寻常。伍尔曼在英国的未婚妻给他寄来一件防弹背心；到那时为止，这背心除了让它的主人汗如雨下之外别无作为；虽说被认作极其「易伤」，倒启发了他连里的一位幽默家称他为「石棉比尔」。
时间似乎已抹去了那场战争的笑声。我在脑子里听不见它了。那笑声该是多么陌生啊——倘若我能像我所描写的那个傍晚那样重新召回它的话：那时我们人人都知道，当晚就要发动一场进攻；因为近视的巴顿和其他连长刚从一次地形侦察回来，而侦察并没有让他们比出发时聪明多少。与此同时，我们肚里灌了几口朗姆酒，总能找到点可笑的事。我们的笑声蹿起来，像黄昏中营火的火苗，不久便被踩灭，或被我们那位不偏不倚的对手——雨——浇熄。多特雷尔那慰藉的身影离去了，我想他一旦独自面对他那咯噔咯噔回程的弹药车，怕是也笑不出多少来。
零时定在午夜过后四十五分钟。两个连将在六百码的正面上进攻，皇家爱尔兰团在我们右翼如法炮制。巴顿的连作为预备队；由于运输队不在，它只能凑出约三十人。
九点钟我们沿那条通向马梅斯的沉路上去。因那场雨，昨日干爽的路已被踩成了泥淖。由于前方部队拥堵，行进缓慢。我们只剩两三千码要走，可有一阵子看来，主攻的几个连未必能在零时之前就位。我们在烂泥里挣扎，四下里漆黑一片；我们到底赶到了，还提前十五分钟，为走这一英里零四分之一码花了三个半钟头。
巴顿把弟兄们部署在低谷树林边缘一道浅浅的支援壕里——那片小树林就在我们昨夜去过的那片地的左边。几乎立刻，短短的预备炮击便开始了，黑暗在那老一套的喧嚣与闪光里变得妖魔般。这不是头一回——我纳闷炮弹会不会在空中相撞。之后是沉默与悬望。巴顿和我低声交谈；他觉得我最好借他的手电筒用用，去认一认到营部最近的路。

===Original===
Everyone was anonymous in the dark, but “It’s me, Kendle, Sir,” from a looming figure beside me implied an intention to share my explorations. We groped our way into the wood, and very soon I muttered that unless we were careful we’d get lost, which was true enough, for my sense of direction had already become uncertain. While we hesitated, some shells exploded all round us in the undergrowth with an effect of crashing stupidity. But we laughed, encouraging each other with mutual bravado, until we found a path. Along this path came someone in a hurry. He bumped into me and I flashed the torch on his face. He was an officer who had joined us the week before. He had now lost all control of himself and I gathered from his incoherent utterances that he was on his way to Headquarters to tell Kinjack that his Company hadn’t moved yet because they didn’t know which way to go to find the Germans. This wasn’t surprising; but I felt alarmed about his reception at Headquarters, for Kinjack had already got an idea that this poor devil was “cold-footed.” So, with an assumption of ferocity, I pulled out my automatic pistol, gripped him by the shoulder, and told him that if he didn’t go straight back to “Asbestos Bill” I’d shoot him, adding that Kinjack would certainly shoot him if he rolled up at Headquarters with such a story and in such a state of “windup.” This sobered him and he took my advice, though I doubt whether he did any damage to the Germans. (Ten days later he was killed in what I can only call a bona fide manner.) So far, I thought, my contribution to this attack is a queer one; I have saved one of our officers from being court-martialled for cowardice. I then remarked to Kendle that this seemed to be the shortest way to Battalion Headquarters and we found our own way back to Barton without further incident. I told Barton that “Asbestos Bill” seemed to be marking time, in spite of his bulletproof waistcoat.
The men were sitting on the rough-hewn fire-step, and soon we were all dozing. Barton’s bulky figure nodded beside me, and Kendle fell fast asleep with his head against my shoulder. We remained like this until my luminous watch indicated twenty past two. Then a runner arrived with a verbal message. “C Company bombers to go up at once.” With a dozen men behind me I followed him through Bottom Wood. Darkness was giving way to unrevealing twilight as we emerged from the trees and went up a shell-pited slope. It was about 500 yards across the open to the newly captured Quadrangle Trench. Just before we got there a second runner overtook us to say that my bombers were to go back again. I sent them back. I cannot say why I went on myself; but I did, and Kendle stayed with me.

===Chinese===
黑暗里人人都是无名的，可我身边一个朦胧的身影一声「是我，肯德尔，长官」，意味着他要跟我一道去探路。我们摸进林子，很快我便嘟囔，说再不当心就要迷路了——这话不假，因为我已辨不准方向。我们正犹豫间，几发炮弹在我们四周的灌木丛里炸开，效果蠢笨而暴烈。可我们笑了起来，彼此虚张声势地打气，直到找到一条小径。沿这条小径急匆匆来了一个人。他撞到我身上，我把手电照在他脸上。是上礼拜才到我们这儿的一名军官。他这时已完全失控，我从他那语无伦次的话里听出，他正要去营部告诉金杰克：他们连还没动，因为他们不知道该往哪边走才能找到德国人。这倒不奇怪；可我担心他到了营部的下场，因为金杰克已有了个印象，认为这个可怜虫「怯战」。于是，我装出一副凶相，拔出我的自动手枪，抓住他的肩膀，告诉他要是他不立刻回「石棉比尔」那儿去，我就毙了他，并补一句：金杰克见他以这样一副「吓破了胆」的状态、带着这样一番说辞出现在营部，肯定会毙了他。这话让他清醒过来，他听从了我的劝告，尽管我怀疑他对德国人造成过什么损伤。（十天后他阵亡了，死于我只能称之为「货真价实」的方式。）到目前为止，我想，我对这场进攻的贡献真是古怪：我救了我们一名军官，使他免于因怯战受军法审判。随后我对肯德尔说，看来这条路去营部最近，于是我们自个儿摸回巴顿那里，再没出什么岔子。我对巴顿说，「石棉比尔」虽然穿着防弹背心，看来却不过是在原地踏步。
弟兄们坐在粗陋的火阶上，很快我们也都打起盹来。巴顿壮硕的身躯在我身边一点头一点头，肯德尔把头靠在我肩上睡熟了。我们这样待着，直到我那块夜光表指到两点二十。然后一名传令兵带来一句口信。「C 连投弹兵立刻上去。」我带着身后十几个人跟着他穿过低谷树林。我们从树丛里出来、上一个弹坑累累的斜坡时，黑暗正让位于一片什么都看不清的薄暮。穿过这片开阔地到新攻下的四边形战壕，约有五百码。我们快到时，第二名传令兵追上来，说我的投弹兵要原路返回。我把他们打发回去了。我说不清为什么我自己还要往前走；可我走了，而肯德尔留了下来陪我。

===Original===
There wasn’t much wire in front of Quadrangle Trench. I entered it at a strong-point on the extreme left and found three officers sitting on the fire-step with hunched shoulders and glum unenterprising faces. Two others had gone away wounded. I was told that Edmunds, the Battalion Observation Officer, had gone down to explain the situation to Kinjack; we were in touch with the Northumberland Fusiliers on our left. Nevertheless I felt that there must be something to be done. Exploring to the right I found young Fernby, whose demeanour was a contrast to the apathetic trio in the sandbagged strong-point. Fernby had only been out from England a few weeks but he appeared quite at home in his new surroundings. His face showed that he was exulting in the fact that he didn’t feel afraid. He told me that no one knew what had happened on our right; the Royal Irish were believed to have failed. We went along the trench which was less than waist deep. The Germans had evidently been digging when we attacked, and had left their packs and other equipment ranged along the reverse edge of the trench. I stared about me; the smoke-drifted twilight was alive with intense movement, and there was a wild strangeness in the scene which somehow excited me. Our men seemed a bit out of hand and I couldn’t see any of the responsible N.C.O.s; some of the troops were firing excitedly at the Wood; others were rummaging in the German packs. Fernby said that we were being sniped from the trees on both sides. Mametz Wood was a menacing wall of gloom, and now an outburst of rapid thudding explosions began from that direction. There was a sap from the Quadrangle to the Wood, and along this the Germans were bombing. In all this confusion I formed the obvious notion that we ought to be deepening the trench. Daylight would be on us at once, and we were along a slope exposed to enfilade fire from the Wood. I told Fernby to make the men dig for all they were worth, and went to the right with Kendle. The Germans had left a lot of shovels, but we were making no use of them. Two tough-looking privates were disputing the ownership of a pair of field-glasses, so I pulled out my pistol and urged them, with ferocious objurgations, to chuck all that fooling and dig. I seem to be getting pretty handy with my pistol, I thought, for the conditions in Quadrangle Trench were giving me a sort of angry impetus. In some places it was only a foot deep, and already men were lying wounded and killed by sniping. There were high-booted German bodies, too, and in the blear beginning of daylight they seemed as much the victims of a catastrophe as the men who had attacked them. As I stepped over one of the Germans an impulse made me lift him up from the miserable ditch. Propped against the bank, his blond face was undisfigured, except by the mud which I wiped from his eyes and mouth with my coat sleeve. He’d evidently been killed while digging, for his tunic was knotted loosely about his shoulders. He didn’t look to be more than eighteen. Hoisting him a little higher, I thought what a gentle face he had, and remembered that this was the first time I’d ever touched one of our enemies with my hands. Perhaps I had some dim sense of the futility which had put an end to this good-looking youth. Anyhow I hadn’t expected the Battle of the Somme to be quite like this.… Kendle, who had been trying to do something for a badly wounded man, now rejoined me, and we continued, mostly on all fours, along the dwindling trench. We passed no one until we came to a bombing post—three serious-minded men who said that no one had been further than that yet. Being in an exploring frame of mind, I took a bag of bombs and crawled another sixty or seventy yards with Kendle close behind me. The trench became a shallow groove and ended where the ground overlooked a little valley along which there was a light railway line. We stared across at the Wood. From the other side of the valley came an occasional rifle-shot, and a helmet bobbed up for a moment. Kendle remarked that from that point anyone could see into the whole of our trench on the slope behind us. I said we must have our strong-post here and told him to go back for the bombers and a Lewis gun. I felt adventurous and it seemed as if Kendle and I were having great fun together. Kendle thought so too. The helmet bobbed up again. “I’ll just have a shot at him,” he said, wriggling away from the crumbling bank which gave us cover. At this moment Fernby appeared with two men and a Lewis gun. Kendle was half kneeling against some broken ground; I remember seeing him push his tin hat back from his forehead and then raise himself a few inches to take aim. After firing once he looked at us with a lively smile; a second later he fell sideways. A blotchy mark showed where the bullet had hit him just above the eyes.

===Chinese===
四边形战壕前面没什么铁丝网。我从最左端的一个坚强据点进去，发现三名军官坐在火阶上，耸着肩，脸色阴沉、毫无干劲。另有两人已负伤离去。我被告知，营观测官埃德蒙兹已下去向金杰克说明情况；我们左翼与诺森伯兰燧发枪兵团保持着联系。然而我仍觉得总该有什么可做的。往右探去，我遇见了年轻的弗恩比，他的神气与沙袋坚强据点里那三个无精打采的人形成对照。弗恩比从英国出来才几个礼拜，却似乎在新环境里如鱼得水。他脸上流露着一种因「自己并不害怕」而生的得意。他告诉我，没人知道我们右翼发生了什么；据说皇家爱尔兰团失败了。我们沿着那条不及腰深的战壕前行。德国人进攻时显然正在挖壕，把背包和其他装备一字排在战壕的后壕沿上。我四下张望；飘着烟的薄暮里到处是紧张的动静，场面有一种荒野的陌生，叫我莫名兴奋。我们的人似乎有点失控，我又看不见一个负责的军士；有的士兵正激动地朝树林开枪；有的在翻德国人的背包。弗恩比说我们正遭两侧树上的冷枪。马梅斯树林是一堵咄咄逼人的阴暗之墙，这时从那个方向起了一阵急促的闷雷般的爆炸。从四边形战壕到树林有一条壕支线，德国人正沿此投弹。在这一片混乱里，我形成了一个再明显不过的念头：我们应当加深这条战壕。天马上就要亮了，而我们沿一道斜坡布防，暴露在树林的侧射火力之下。我叫弗恩比让弟兄们拼命挖，自己带着肯德尔往右去。德国人留下了许多铁锹，我们却没用上。两个长相粗悍的列兵正争夺一副望远镜的归属，我便拔出手枪，恶狠狠地咒骂着，逼他们丢掉那套蠢把戏、赶紧挖。我这手枪倒是越用越顺手了，我想，因为四边形战壕里的局面给了我一种愤怒的推力。有些地方壕深只有一英尺，已有人因冷枪而躺倒负伤或死去。也有穿高统靴的德国人尸体，在朦胧初生的天光里，他们与攻打他们的人一样，像是某种灾祸的遇难者。我跨过其中一个德国人时，一股冲动使我把他从那凄惨的沟里扶起来。靠壕壁支住后，他那张金发的脸并无毁损，只有一些泥——我用衣袖替他擦去眼上和嘴边的泥。他显然是在挖壕时被打死的，因为他的军装上衣松松地系在肩上。他看上去不过十八岁。把他再扶高一点时，我想，他这张脸多么温柔；我也想起，这是我头一回亲手触碰我们的一个敌人。也许我隐隐感到了那种结束了这个漂亮少年的无谓。无论如何，我没料到索姆河战役竟会是这样一番模样。……肯德尔方才一直在设法救助一个重伤员，这时回到我身边，我们多半四肢着地、沿着那条越走越浅的战壕继续前行。我们再没碰见任何人，直到来到一个投弹哨——三个神情严肃的人说，还没人比这更往前过。我正怀着探险的心思，便拎起一袋手榴弹，与紧跟身后的肯德尔又爬了六七十码。战壕变成一道浅沟，到一处俯瞰小山谷的地面便到了头，山谷里有一条轻便铁路。我们隔着谷地望着那片树林。山谷对岸偶尔飞来一记步枪声，有一只钢盔冒了一下。肯德尔说，从那一点能把我们身后斜坡上整条战壕看得一清二楚。我说我们必须把坚强据点设在这里，叫他回去带投弹兵和一挺路易斯机枪来。我颇觉冒险，仿佛肯德尔和我正在一起玩一场痛快的游戏。肯德尔也这么想。那只钢盔又冒了一下。「我就给他一枪，」他说着，从给我们掩蔽的那段正在崩塌的壕边挪开。就在这时，弗恩比带着两个人和一挺路易斯机枪出现了。肯德尔正半跪着靠在一些破碎的地面上；我记得看见他把钢盔从额前往后推了推，然后抬起身子几英寸瞄准。开了一枪之后，他活泼地朝我们一笑；一秒钟后，他侧身倒下。一块污渍标出子弹击中他的位置——就在双眼上方。

===Original===
The circumstances being what they were, I had no justification for feeling either shocked or astonished by the sudden extinction of Lance-Corporal Kendle. But after blank awareness that he was killed, all feelings tightened and contracted to a single intention—to settle that sniper on the other side of the valley. If I had stopped to think, I shouldn’t have gone at all. As it was, I discarded my tin hat and equipment, slung a bag of bombs across my shoulder, abruptly informed Fernby that I was going to find out who *was* there, and set off at a downhill double. While I was running I pulled the safety-pin out of a Mills bomb; my right hand being loaded, I did the same for my left. I mention this because I was obliged to extract the second safety-pin with my teeth, and the grating sensation reminded me that I was halfway across and not so reckless as I had been when I started. I was even a little out of breath as I trotted up the opposite slope. Just before I arrived at the top I slowed up and threw my two bombs. Then I rushed at the bank, vaguely expecting some sort of scuffle with my imagined enemy. I had lost my temper with the man who had shot Kendle; quite unexpectedly, I found myself looking down into a well-conducted trench with a great many Germans in it. Fortunately for me, they were already retreating. It had not occurred to them that they were being attacked by a single fool; and Fernby, with presence of mind which probably saved me, had covered my advance by traversing the top of the trench with his Lewis gun. I slung a few more bombs, but they fell short of the clumsy field-grey figures, some of whom half turned to fire their rifles over the left shoulder as they ran across the open toward the wood, while a crowd of jostling helmets vanished along the trench. Idiotically elated, I stood there with my finger in my right ear and emitted a series of “view-holloas” (a gesture which ought to win the approval of people who still regard war as a form of outdoor sport). Having thus failed to commit suicide, I proceeded to occupy the trench—that is to say I sat down on the fire-step, very much out of breath, and hoped to God the Germans wouldn’t come back again.
The trench was deep and roomy, with a fine view of our men in the Quadrangle, but I had no idea what to do now I had got possession of it. The word “consolidation” passed through my mind; but I couldn’t consolidate by myself. Naturally, I didn’t underestimate the magnitude of my achievement in capturing the trench on which the Royal Irish had made a frontal attack in the dark.
