And I saw it then, as I see it now—a dreadful place, a place of horror and desolation which no imagination could have invented. Also it was a place where a man of strong spirit might know himself utterly powerless against death and destruction, and yet stand up and defy gross darkness and stupefying shellfire, discovering in himself the invincible resistance of an animal or an insect, and an endurance which he might, in after days, forget or disbelieve.
Anyhow, there I was, leading that little procession of Flintshire Fusiliers many of whom had never seen a front-line trench before. At that juncture they asked no compensation for their efforts except a mug of hot tea. The tea would have been a miracle, and we didn’t get it till next morning, but there was some comfort in the fact that it wasn’t raining.

===Chinese===
我们所到达的，是一段十二天前从十三号营启程的旅程的终点。一站又一站，我们行军到了这片生机全无的地带——它在老远之外便以它炮火的闪烁与低吼威吓过我们。此刻，我们正沿着一条深沟摸黑、跌跌撞撞地走向那片不属于人世的浩劫中指定给我们的位置。那夜想必有些朦胧的月光，因为我记得那些蜷在交通壕壕壁上的人影；在那一种可怕的微光里看见他们——（也许是山脊那头一枚将熄的照明弹散开的白光？）我拿不准他们是睡着了，还是已经死了，因为许多人的姿势就像死了一样，怪诞而扭曲。可你会说，这没什么新鲜的；不过是一队疲惫的人，在一边深一脚浅一脚地朝前沿战壕摸去的同时，挤过那些死了的或打盹的人。然而那夜的换防对我有其意义，尽管在人类经验里它已被重复了百万次。我，一个脑子里装着自己那一小点尘世经历的、孤单的人，正在重新走进那个被称作「阿米吉多顿」（Armageddon）的东西的真切的黑暗与灾祸。我当时看见它，正如我现在看见它——一个可怕的地方，一个任何想象力都发明不出的恐怖与荒凉之地。它也是一个地方：在那里，一个精神强悍的人明知自己对死亡与毁灭完全无能为力，却仍能挺身而起，蔑视那浓重的黑暗与令人目眩的炮火，在自己身上发现一只动物或一只昆虫那种不可战胜的抵抗，以及一种他日后也许会忘却、甚至不肯相信自己曾拥有过的耐力。
无论如何，我人就在那里，领着那一小队弗林特郡燧发枪兵，他们中许多人此前从未见过一条前沿战壕。在那个当口，他们对自己这番辛苦所求的报酬，不过是一杯热茶。那茶本来会是个奇迹，可我们直到第二天早晨才喝上；唯一的一点安慰是，至少天没下雨。

===Original===
It was nearly four o’clock when we found ourselves in the Hindenburg Main Trench. After telling me to post the sentries, Leake disappeared down some stairs to the Tunnel (which will be described later on). The Company we were relieving had already departed, so there was no one to give me any information. At first I didn’t even know for certain that we were in the front-line. The trench was a sort of gully, deep, wide, and unfinished looking. The sentries had to clamber up a bank of loose earth before they could see over the top. Our Company was only about eighty strong and its sector was fully 600 yards. The distance between the sentry-posts made me aware of our inadequacy in that wilderness. I had no right to feel homeless, but I did; and if I had needed to be reminded of my forlorn situation as a living creature I could have done it merely by thinking of a Field Cashier. Fifty franc notes were comfortable things, but they were no earthly use up here, and the words “Field Cashier” would have epitomized my remoteness from snugness and security and from all assurance that I should be alive and kicking the week after next. But it would soon be Sunday morning; such ideas weren’t wholesome, and there was a certain haggard curiosity attached to the proceedings; combined with the self-dramatizing desperation which enabled a good many of us to worry our way through much worse emergencies than mine.
When I had posted the exhausted sentries, with as much cheeriness as I could muster, I went along to look for the Company on our left. Rather expecting to find one of our own companies, I came round a corner to a place where the trench was unusually wide. There I found myself among a sort of panic party which I was able to identify as a platoon (thirty or forty strong). They were jostling one another in their haste to get through a cavernous doorway, and as I stood astonished one of them breathlessly told me that “the Germans were coming over.” Two officers were shepherding them downstairs and before I’d had time to think the whole lot had vanished. The Battalion they belonged to was one of those amateur ones which were at such a disadvantage owing to lack of discipline and the absence of trained N.C.O.s. Anyhow their behaviour seemed to indicate that the Tunnel in the Hindenburg Trench was having a lowering effect on their morale.

===Chinese===
等我们在兴登堡主壕里站定，已是将近四点钟。莱克叫我布置好哨兵，便自顾自沿一段楼梯下到「隧道」里去了（隧道日后还会讲到）。我们要换防的那个连早已离去，所以没有人能给我任何交代。起初我甚至拿不准我们到底是不是在前沿。这条战壕像一道沟渠，深、宽、看起来像是未完工。哨兵得爬上一道松土堆，才能从顶上望出去。我们连只有八十来人，而其防区足有六百码。哨位之间相隔的距离，让我意识到自己在这片荒原里的捉襟见肘。我本没资格觉得自己无家可归，可我确实这么觉得；倘若我需要有人提醒自己作为一个活物的凄凉处境，我只需想一想「野战出纳官」就够了。五十法郎一张的钞票本是舒坦之物，可在这儿一点用都没有；而「野战出纳官」这几个字，便足以概括我与安逸安全之间的遥远距离，概括我无法确信自己下下个礼拜是否还能活蹦乱跳。不过马上就是星期天早晨了；这种念头可不健康，何况眼下的种种举动里自有一种惨淡的好奇心；再加上一种自我戏剧化的孤注一掷，正是这种孤注一掷让我们不少人熬过了比我这场更凶险的危机。
我把那些筋疲力尽的哨兵布置下去，尽量装出一副高兴的样子，然后沿着战壕去找我们左翼的那个连。我本以为会碰见我们自己营的一个连，不料拐过一个弯，来到一段格外宽的战壕。我在那儿撞见了一群惊慌失措的人，看得出是一个排（三四十人）。他们你推我搡地急着钻进一个洞窟般的门道，我正愕然间，其中一个上气不接下气地告诉我「德国人冲过来了」。两名军官正把他们往楼下赶，我还来不及多想，整帮人已消失得无影无踪。他们所属的营，是那种因军纪涣散、缺少训练有素的军士而大为吃亏的业余队伍。不管怎样，他们的行为似乎说明，兴登堡战壕里的那条隧道，对他们的士气颇有打击作用。

===Original===
Out in No Man’s Land there was no sign of any German activity. The only remarkable thing was the unbroken silence. I was in a sort of twilight, for there was a moony glimmer in the low-clouded sky; but the unknown territory in front was dark, and I stared out at it like a man looking from the side of a ship. Returning to my own sector I met a runner with a verbal message from Battalion H.Q. B Company’s front was to be thoroughly patrolled at once. Realizing the futility of sending any of my few spare men out on patrol (they’d been walking about for seven hours and were dead beat), I lost my temper, quietly and inwardly. Shirley and Rees were nowhere to be seen and it wouldn’t have been fair to send them out, inexperienced as they were. So I stumped along to our right-flank post, told them to pass it along that a patrol was going out from right to left, and then started sulkily out for a solitary stroll in No Man’s Land. I felt more annoyed with Battalion Headquarters than with the enemy. There was no wire in front of the trench, which was, of course, constructed for people facing the other way. I counted my steps; 200 steps straight ahead; then I began to walk the presumptive 600 steps to the left. But it isn’t easy to count your steps in the dark among shell-holes, and after a problematic 400 I lost confidence in my automatic pistol, which I was grasping in my right-hand breeches pocket. Here I am, I thought, alone out in this godforsaken bit of ground, with quite a good chance of bumping into a Boche strong-post. Apparently there was only one reassuring action which I could perform; so I expressed my opinion of the War by relieving myself (for it must be remembered that there are other reliefs beside Battalion reliefs). I insured my sense of direction by placing my pistol on the ground with its muzzle pointing the way I was going. Feeling less lonely and afraid, I finished my patrol without having met so much as a dead body, and regained the trench exactly opposite our left-hand post, after being huskily challenged by an irresolute sentry, who, as I realized at the time, was the greatest danger I had encountered. It was now just beginning to be more daylight than darkness, and when I stumbled down a shaft to the underground trench I left the sentries shivering under a red and rainy-looking sky.
There were fifty steps down the shaft; the earthy smell of that triumph of Teutonic military engineering was strongly suggestive of appearing in the Roll of Honour and being buried until the Day of Judgment. Dry-mouthed and chilled to the bone, I lay in a wire-netting bunk and listened to the dismal snorings of my companions. Along the Tunnel the air blew deathly cold and seasoned with mephitic odours. In vain I envied the snorers; but I was getting accustomed to lack of sleep, and three hours later I was gulping some peculiar tea with morose enjoyment. Owing to the scarcity of water (which had to be brought up by the Transport who were eight miles back, at Blairville) washing wasn’t possible; but I contrived a refreshing shave, utilizing the dregs of my tea.

===Chinese===
外头的无人区没有任何德国人活动的迹象。唯一值得注意的是那一片不破的寂静。我处在一种半明半暗之中，因为低垂的云层里有一抹月色般的微光；但前方那片未知的土地是黑的，我像一个从船舷往外望的人那样盯着它。回到自己防区，我遇见一名传令兵，带来营部的一句口信：B 连正面要立刻彻底巡逻一遍。想到把我那几个有限的闲人派去巡逻是多么徒劳（他们已经连走了七个钟头，累得要死），我暗暗地生了气，不声不响地。雪利和里斯不知去向，而他们毫无经验，派出去也不公平。于是我闷闷地走到右翼哨位，叫他们往后传：有一支巡逻队将从右往左出去；然后我便赌气似的，独自一人出去，到无人区溜达。我对营部的恼火，胜过对敌人的。战壕前头没有铁丝网，这战壕本来就是给朝着另一边的人修的。我数着自己的步子；笔直往前走两百步；然后开始向左走那推算中的六百步。但在黑暗里、在弹坑之间数步子可不容易，走到一个存疑的四百步时，我对揣在右边裤兜里的自动手枪失去了信心。我，我想，孤身一人在这一片鬼不生蛋的地方晃荡，撞上一个德国佬据点的可能性相当不小。看来我能做的、唯一叫自己安心的事只有一桩；于是我便以解手之举，表达了我对这场战争的看法（须知除了营级换防之外，还有别的「解手」）。我把手枪放在地上、枪口朝着我要走的方向，以此给自己保住方向感。觉得不那么孤单、不那么害怕了，我完成了巡逻，连一具尸体都没碰上；正好对着我们左翼哨位回到战壕里，此前被一个犹犹豫豫的哨兵用沙哑的嗓音盘问过——我当时就意识到，他才是我这一路上遇到的最大危险。此时天色已是亮多于暗，我跌跌撞撞从一口竖井下到地下战壕时，把哨兵们留在一抹红彤彤、像要下雨的天色底下打着寒颤。
竖井往下五十级台阶；这一座日耳曼军事工程杰作所散发出来的泥土气息，强烈地叫人联想到自己已登上伤亡名册、被埋到末日审判那天。我口干舌燥、冷透骨髓，躺在一张铁丝网床上，听着同伴们凄惨的鼾声。沿着隧道吹过来的风冷得像死，还调着股地狱般的恶臭。我徒然地羡慕那些打鼾的人；不过我已渐渐习惯了缺觉，三个钟头之后，我便在闷闷不乐中津津有味地灌着一种味道古怪的茶。因为缺水（水得靠八英里之外、在布莱尔维尔的运输队送上来），洗漱是不可能的；但我设法用茶根儿凑合着刮了个令人精神一振的脸。

===Original===
By ten o’clock I was above ground again, in charge of a fatigue-party. We went halfway back to St. Martin, to an ammunition dump, whence we carried up boxes of trench mortar bombs. I carried a box myself, as the conditions were vile and it seemed the only method of convincing the men that it had to be done. We were out nearly seven hours; it rained all day and the trenches were a morass of glue-like mud. The unmitigated misery of that carrying-party was a typical infantry experience of discomfort without actual danger. Even if the ground had been dry the boxes would have been too heavy for most of the men; but we were lucky in one way; the wet weather was causing the artillery to spend an inactive Sunday. It was a yellow corpse-like day, more like November than April, and the landscape was desolate and treeless. What we were doing was quite unexceptional; millions of soldiers endured the same sort of thing and got badly shelled into the bargain. Nevertheless I can believe that my party, staggering and floundering under its loads, would have made an impressive picture of “Despair.” The background, too, was appropriate. We were among the debris of the intense bombardment of ten days before, for we were passing along and across the Hindenburg Outpost Trench, with its belt of wire (fifty yards deep in places); here and there these rusty jungles had been flattened by tanks. The Outpost Trench was about 200 yards from the Main Trench, which was now our front-line. It had been solidly made, ten feet deep, with timbered fire-steps, splayed sides, and timbered steps at intervals to front and rear and to machine-gun emplacements. Now it was wrecked as though by earthquake and eruption. Concrete strong-posts were smashed and tilted sideways; everywhere the chalky soil was pocked and pitted with huge shell-holes; and wherever we looked the mangled effigies of the dead were our memento mori. Shell-twisted and dismembered, the Germans maintained the violent attitudes in which they had died. The British had mostly been killed by bullets or bombs, so they looked more resigned. But I can remember a pair of hands (nationality unknown) which protruded from the soaked ashen soil like the roots of a tree turned upside down; one hand seemed to be pointing at the sky with an accusing gesture. Each time I passed that place the protest of those fingers became more expressive of an appeal to God in defiance of those who made the War. Who made the War? I laughed hysterically as the thought passed through my mud-stained mind. But I only laughed mentally, for my box of Stokes-gun ammunition left me no breath to spare for an angry guffaw. And the dead were the dead; this was no time to be pitying them or asking silly questions about their outraged lives. Such sights must be taken for granted, I thought, as I gasped and slithered and stumbled with my disconsolate crew. Floating on the surface of the flooded trench was the mask of a human face which had detached itself from the skull.

===Chinese===
到十点钟我又上了地面，带着一支劳役队。我们往回走到离圣马丁一半路的地方，到一个弹药堆，从那里把一箱箱战壕迫击炮弹搬上去。我自己也搬了一箱，因为条件太恶劣，似乎只有这个办法才能让弟兄们相信：这活儿非干不可。我们出去了将近七个钟头；雨下了一整天，战壕成了一片胶泥的沼泽。那支搬运队所受的无尽的苦，是典型的步兵体验：一种没有实际危险的难受。即便地面是干的，那些箱子对大多数弟兄来说也太重；不过我们有一点走运：这样的雨天让炮兵也过了一个无所事事的星期天。那是一个蜡黄、像尸体一样的日子，比十一月还像十一月，风景荒凉、一棵树也没有。我们所做的事毫不稀奇；数以百万计的士兵承受过同样的遭遇，还外搭挨一顿狠炸。可我相信，我这一队人马，在负荷之下跌跌撞撞、深一脚浅一脚，会构成一幅动人的「绝望」图景。背景也很相称。我们正走在十天前那场猛烈炮击的残骸之中，因为我们沿着、也横穿过兴登堡前哨壕，以及它那一带铁丝网（有的地方深达五十码）；这些锈迹斑斑的丛林，有的已被坦克碾平。前哨壕离主壕——也就是我们眼下前沿——约两百码。它本修得结结实实，深十英尺，带有木质的火力阶、斜削的壕壁，以及间隔分布、通向前后和机枪工事的木质阶梯。如今它像被地震和火山喷发毁掉了一般。混凝土据点被砸碎、歪斜着；白垩土到处被巨大的弹坑弄得坑坑洼洼；我们无论看向何处，那些死者被毁坏的形象，都是我们的「莫忘汝终有一死」。被炮火扭曲、肢解的德国人，保持着他们死去时那种暴烈的姿势。英国人大多死于子弹或炸弹，所以看上去比较认命。可我记得有一双手（国籍不明），从湿透的灰烬般的泥土里伸出来，像一棵被连根拔起的树的根；一只手似乎以控诉的姿态，指着天空。每次我经过那个地方，那几根手指的抗议，便越发像是向着上帝的申诉，蔑视着那些发动这场战争的人。是谁发动了这场战争？这念头从我那沾满烂泥的脑子里掠过时，我歇斯底里地笑了一下。不过我只是在心里笑，因为我那箱斯托克斯迫击炮弹让我连喘口气的工夫都没有，更别说愤怒地哈哈大笑。死者已矣；此刻不是怜悯他们、或为他们被蹂躏的生命胡思乱想的时候。这种景象必须习以为常，我想，一边喘着气、打着滑、跌跌撞撞地带着我这群垂头丧气的弟兄走着。在被水淹没的战壕水面上，漂浮着一张人脸的面具——它已从头骨上脱落下来。

### V / 五

===Original===
Plastered with mud and soaked to the skin, the fatigue-party clumped down the steps to the Tunnel. The carrying job was finished; but a stimulating surprise awaited me, for Leake was just back from Battalion H.Q. (somewhere along the Tunnel) and he breezily informed me that I’d been detailed to take command of a hundred bombers in the attack which had been arranged for next morning. “Twenty-five bombers from each Company; you’re to act as reserve for the Cameronians,” he remarked. I stared at him over my mug of reviving but trench-flavoured tea (made with chlorinated water), and asked him to tell me some more. He said, “Well, they’re a bit hazy about it at Headquarters, but the General is frightfully keen on our doing an underground attack along the Tunnel, as well as along the main trench up above. You’ve got to go and discuss the tactical situation with one of the Company commanders up in the front line on our right.” All that I knew about the tactical situation was that if one went along the Tunnel one arrived at a point where a block had been made by blowing it in. On the other side one bumped into the Germans. Above ground there was a barrier and the situation was similar. Bombing along a Tunnel in the dark.… Had the War Office issued a textbook on the subject?… I lit my pipe, but failed to enjoy it, probably because the stewed tea had left such a queer taste in my mouth.

===Chinese===
那支劳役队糊满烂泥、浑身湿透，咚咚地踩着台阶下到隧道里。搬运活儿干完了；可一个令人振奋的意外正等着我：莱克刚从营部（在隧道里某处）回来，他兴冲冲地告诉我，我已被指派去指挥一百名投弹兵，参加定在第二天早晨的进攻。「每个连出二十五名投弹兵；你去做卡梅伦苏格兰步枪兵的预备队，」他说。我从那杯提神但带着战壕味的茶（用加过氯的水煮的）上方瞪着他，请他再讲详细些。他说：「这个嘛，营部那边也不大清楚，不过将军特别热衷于让我们沿隧道来一次地下进攻，与上头地表主壕的进攻相配合。你得去跟我们右翼前沿的一位连长讨论一下战术形势。」我对战术形势的全部了解就是：沿隧道往前走，会走到一个被炸塌封死的地方。另一边便是德国人。地面上有一道德军障碍，情形相似。在黑暗中沿隧道扔手榴弹……陆军部就这个题目出过教材吗？……我点上烟斗，却没品出滋味来，多半是那杯煮茶在我嘴里留下的怪味作祟。

===Original===
Ruminating on the comfortless responsibility imposed on me by this enterprise, I waited until nightfall. Then a superbly cheerful little guide bustled me along a maze of waterlogged ditches until I found myself in a small dugout with some friendly Scotch officers and a couple of flame-wagging candles. The dugout felt more like old times than the Hindenburg Tunnel, but the officers made me feel incompetent and uninformed, for they were loquacious about local trench topography which meant nothing to my newly-arrived mind. So I puffed out my Military Cross ribbon (the dugout contained two others), nodded my head knowingly, and took an acquiescent share in the discussion of the strategic situation. Details of organization were offered me and I made a few smudgy notes. The Cams didn’t think that there was much chance of my party being called on to support them, and they were hoping that the underground attack would be eliminated from operation orders.
I emerged from the desperation jollity of their little den with only a blurred notion of what it was all about. The objective was to clear the trench for 500 yards while other battalions went over the top on our left to attack Fontaine-les-Croiselles. But I was, at the best of times, only an opportunist officer; technical talk in the Army always made me feel mutely inefficient. And now I was floundering home in the dark to organize my command, put something plausible on paper, and take it along to the Adjutant. If only I could consult the Doctor, I thought; for he was back from leave, though I hadn’t seen him yet. It seemed to me, in my confused and exhausted condition, that I was at a crisis in my military career; and, as usual, my main fear was that I should make a fool of myself. The idea of making a fool of oneself in that murderous mix-up now appears to me rather a ludicrous one; for I see myself merely as a blundering flustered little beetle; and if someone happens to put his foot on a beetle, it is unjust to accuse the unlucky insect of having made a fool of itself. When I got back to Leake and Rees and Shirley I felt so lost and perplexed that I went straight on to Battalion H.Q.

===Chinese===
一边琢磨着这桩差事强加给我的、不自在的责任，我一边等到天黑。随后，一位快活得过分的小向导，匆匆带我穿过一座水淹的壕沟迷宫，直到我走进一间小掩体，里面有几位和气的苏格兰军官和两支摇曳着火苗的蜡烛。这掩体比兴登堡隧道更像从前那种日子，可那几位军官让我觉得自己既无能又无知，因为他们滔滔不绝地谈论着本地战壕的地形，而我这个新来的人听了如闻天书。于是我鼓起我那枚军功十字勋章的勋带（掩体里还有两人佩戴着），煞有介事地点着头，在那场战略形势讨论里装出一副随声附和的模样。组织上的细节给我列了一些，我潦草地记了几笔。那些卡梅伦人觉得我的队伍被叫上去支援他们的可能性不大，他们正盼着这次地下进攻能从作战命令里取消。
我从他们那间「绝望的快活」小窝里出来时，对整桩事仍只有一个模糊的概念。目标是清出五百码的战壕，与此同时其他营在我们左翼跃出掩体冲锋，去攻取枫丹莱克鲁瓦塞尔。可我这个人，在最好的时候也不过是个随机应变的军官；军中的技术谈话总让我感到一种哑口无言的无力。而眼下，我正在黑暗里跌跌撞撞地往回走，要去组织我的部队，在纸上写点什么像样的东西，再送到副官那里去。要能跟军医商量一下就好了，我想；他已休假回来，虽然我还没见着他。在我又乱又累的状况下，我觉得自己正处在军旅生涯的一个危机上；而一如既往，我最大的担心是：自己会出洋相。在那场杀气腾腾的大混乱里出洋相这个念头，如今在我看来相当可笑；因为我现在看自己，不过是只乱闯乱撞、手忙脚乱的小甲虫；倘若有人一脚踩到一只甲虫身上，责怪那只倒霉的虫子「出了洋相」，未免太不公道。我回到莱克、里斯和雪利那里时，觉得自己既迷茫又不知所措，便径直再往前去了营部。

===Original===
The Tunnel was a few inches higher than a tall man walking upright; it was fitted with bunks and recessed rooms; in places it was crowded with men of various units, but there were long intervals of unwholesome-smelling solitude. Prying my way along with an electric torch, I glimpsed an assortment of vague shapes, boxes, tins, fragments of broken furniture and frowsy mattresses. It seemed a long way to Headquarters, and the Tunnel was memorable but not fortifying to a fatigued explorer who hadn’t slept for more than an hour at a stretch or taken his clothes off since last Tuesday. Once, when I tripped and recovered myself by grabbing the wall, my tentative patch of brightness revealed somebody half-hidden under a blanket. Not a very clever spot to be taking a nap, I thought, as I stooped to shake him by the shoulder. He refused to wake up, so I gave him a kick. “God blast you, where’s Battalion Headquarters?” My nerves were on edge; and what right had he to be having a good sleep, when I never seemed to get five minutes’ rest?… Then my beam settled on the livid face of a dead German whose fingers still clutched the blackened gash on his neck.… Stumbling on, I could only mutter to myself that this was really a bit too thick. (That, however, was an exaggeration; there is nothing remarkable about a dead body in a European War, or a squashed beetle in a cellar.) At Headquarters I found the Adjutant alone, worried and preoccupied with clerical work. He had worked in an office, at accountancy, I believe, before the War; and now most of his fighting was done in writing, though he had served his apprenticeship as a brave and indefatigable platoon commander. He told me that the underground attack had been washed out by a providential counter-order from Division, and asked me to send my organization scheme along as soon as possible. “Right-O!” I replied, and groped my way back again feeling the reverse of my reply. By a stroke of luck I discovered Ralph Wilmot, sitting by himself in a small recessed room—his dark hair smoothly brushed and his countenance pensive but unperturbed. He might conceivably have been twiddling a liqueur glass in a Piccadilly restaurant. Unfortunately he had no liquid refreshment to offer, but his philosophic way of greeting me was a consolation and in him I confided my dilemma. With an understanding air he assumed his monocle, deliberated for a while, snuffed the candle wick, and wrote out an authoritative looking document headed “Organization of F.F. Parties.” The gist of it was “15 Bombers (each carrying 10 bombs) and Rifle Grenadiers (each carrying 5 grenades). 5 Carriers (also act as bayonet men), 1 Full Rank.” There wasn’t much in it, he remarked, as he appended “a little bit of skite about consolidation and defensive flanks.” It certainly looked simple enough when it was done, though I had been at my wits’ end about it.

===Chinese===
这隧道比一个高个子直立行走的高度只高出几英寸；里头设有床铺和凹进去的小室；有些地方挤满了各个单位的士兵，但也有长长的、散发着令人不适气味的空寂地段。我打着手电筒一路摸索，瞥见一堆模糊的形状、箱子、洋铁罐、破家具的碎片和发霉的床垫。到营部似乎有很长一段路，而这条隧道，对一个已经一连好几天每次睡不过一个钟头、从上星期二起就没脱过衣服的疲惫探路者来说，令人难忘，却不能给他添什么气力。有一次我被绊了一下，伸手抓住洞壁才稳住身子，我那束试探的光照亮了一个人，半隐在一条毯子底下。这地方睡觉得不妙吧，我想着，弯下腰去摇他肩膀。他不肯醒，我便踢了他一脚。「老天咒你，营部在哪儿？」我神经绷得紧紧的；他凭什么睡得这么香，而我好像连五分钟的歇都捞不着？……随即我的光柱落在了一张死德国人的青灰色的脸上——那人的手指仍紧紧捂着自己脖子上那道发黑的伤口。……我跌跌撞撞往前走，只能喃喃自语：这实在有点过分了。（不过这话有点夸张；欧洲战争里出现一具尸体，或地窖里出现一只被踩扁的甲虫，都没什么可大惊小怪的。）在营部，我只见到副官一个人，他正愁眉苦脸、埋头于文书工作。战前他好像在一家办公室里干过会计；如今他的仗大多是在纸上打的，尽管他当年也曾以一名勇敢而不知疲倦的排长起家。他告诉我，那次地下进攻已被师里一道恩赐般的反命令取消了，并叫我尽快把我的编组方案送去。「好嘞！」我答道，又摸黑往回走，心里觉着跟自己答的恰恰相反。运气不错，我撞见了拉尔夫·威尔莫特——他独自坐在一间凹进去的小室里，黑头发梳得光光的，神色沉思而不惊。他倒大可以去皮卡迪利大街一家饭店里，晃着一只利口酒杯的。可惜他没什么液体的慰问品可以招待我，但他那哲人式的招呼却是一种安慰，我便把自己的难处向他吐露。他一脸善解人意地戴起单片眼镜，沉吟片刻，剪了剪烛芯，然后写下了一份看上去颇为权威的文件，抬头是「燧发枪兵团投弹队编组」（Organization of F.F. Parties）。要旨是：「投弹兵 15 名（每人携弹 10 枚）及步枪枪榴弹兵（每人携弹 5 枚）。搬运手 5 名（兼刺刀手），全副武装 1 名。」这里头没什么学问，他说着，又附上「一小段关于巩固阵地和防御侧翼的废话」。写完之后看起来的确简单得很，尽管此前我曾为此一筹莫展。

===Original===
While he was fixing up my future for me I gazed around and thought what a queer refuge I’d found for what might possibly be my final night on earth. Dugout though it was, the narrow chamber contained a foggy mirror and a clock. The clock wasn’t ticking, but its dumb face stared at me, an idiot reminder of real rooms and desirable domesticity. Outside the doorless doorway people were continually passing in both directions with a sound of shuffling feet and mumbling voices. I caught sight of a red-capped Staff Officer, and a party of sappers carrying picks and shovels. The Tunnel was a sort of highway and the night had brought a considerable congestion of traffic. When we’d sent my document along to the Adjutant there was nothing more to be done except sit and wait for operation orders. It was now about ten o’clock.
As evidence of my own soldierly qualities I would like to be able to declare that we eagerly discussed every aspect of the situation as regards next morning’s attack. But the truth is that we said nothing at all about it. The thing had to be attempted and there was an end of it (until zero hour). The Brigadier and his Staff (none too bright at map-references) were hoping to satisfy (vicariously) General Whincop (who’d got an unpopular bee in his bonnet about the rum ration, and had ordered an impossible raid, two months ago, which had been prevented by a providential thaw and caused numerous deaths in a subsequently sacrificed battalion).

===Chinese===
他替我安排未来的时候，我四下打量，心想我竟在这样一个古怪地方，度过我在世上也许是最后一个夜晚。这虽是个掩体，狭小的房间里却有一面雾蒙蒙的镜子和一座钟。钟不在走，可它那张哑脸直瞪着我，像是真实房间与可向往的家庭生活的一个傻瓜式的提醒。门外那没有门的门口，人们不断地从两个方向走过，发出拖着脚的脚步声和含混的人声。我瞥见一名戴红帽的参谋军官，还有一队扛着镐和锹的工兵。隧道成了一条大马路，夜色带来了相当拥挤的交通。我们把我的文件送去给副官之后，便没什么可做的了，只能坐着等作战命令。这时大约十点钟。
作为我自己军人素质的证据，我很想宣称：我们热烈地讨论了第二天早晨进攻形势的方方面面。但真相是我们对这事一个字也没说。这桩事非尝试不可，就这么定了（到零时再说）。那位旅长和他的参谋们（地图参照点摸得不太灵光）正盼着能（间接地）满足温科普将军（他对朗姆酒配给抱着一个不受人欢迎的偏执念头，又在两个月前下令搞过一次不可能完成的突袭，那次突袭被一场恩赐般的解冻所阻止，却在一个随后被当作牺牲品的营里造成了许多人的死亡）。

===Original===
Whincop was hoping to satisfy the Corps Commander, of whom we knew nothing at all, except that he had insulted our Colonel on the Doullens road. The Corps Commander hoped to satisfy the Army Commander, who had as usual informed us that we were “pursuing a beaten enemy,” and who had brought the Cavalry up for a “breakthrough.” (It is worth mentioning that the village which was now our Division’s objective was still held by the Germans eight months afterwards.) And the Army Commander, I suppose, was in telephonic communication with the Commander-in-Chief, who with one eye on Marshal Foch, was hoping to satisfy his King and Country. Such being the case, Wilmot and myself were fully justified in leaving the situation to the care of the military caste who were making the most of their Great Opportunity for obtaining medal-ribbons and reputations for leadership; and if I am being caustic and captious about them I can only plead the need for a few minutes’ postwar retaliation. Let the Staff write their own books about the Great War say I. The Infantry were biased against them, and their authentic story will be read with interest.
As for our conversation between ten o’clock and midnight (when my operation orders arrived from the Adjutant) I suppose it was a form of drug, since it was confined to pleasant retrospections of peace. Wilmot was well acquainted with my part of the world and he’d come across many of our local worthies. So we were able to make a little tour of the Kentish Weald and the Sussex border, as though on a couple of mental bicycles. In imagination we cycled along on a fine summer afternoon, passing certain milestones which will always be inseparable from my life history. Outside Squire Maundle’s park gate we shared a distinct picture of his angular attitudes while he addressed his golf-ball among the bell-tinklings and *baa*-ings of sheep on the sunny slopes above Amblehurst (always followed by a taciturn black retriever). Much has been asserted about the brutalized condition of mind to which soldiers were reduced by life in the front-line; I do not deny this, but I am inclined to suggest that there was a proportionate amount of simple-minded sentimentality. As far as I was concerned, no topic could be too homely for the trenches.

===Chinese===
温科普盼着能讨好军长——此公我们一无所知，只晓得他曾在杜伦的路上羞辱过我们的上校。军长盼着能讨好集团军司令，而司令照例通知我们说我们正在「追击败退之敌」，并且把骑兵调了上来准备「突破」。（值得提一句的是，此时作为我们师目标的那个村子，八个月之后仍被德国人占着。）而集团军司令呢，我猜想，正与总司令保持着电话联系，总司令一只眼瞄着福煦元帅（Marshal Foch），盼着能讨好他的国王与祖国。既是这般情形，那么威尔莫特和我，把局面完全交给那些正充分利用其「天赐良机」捞取勋章绶带与领导名声的军人阶层去照料，便完全是情理之中的事了；倘若我对他们刻薄、挑剔，我只能以战后报复几分钟的需要来辩解。让参谋们自己去写大战的回忆录吧，我说。步兵对他们有偏见，而他们那真实的故事读起来想必颇有趣味。
至于从十点到午夜（我的作战命令就是在这段时间由副官送来的）我们之间的谈话，我想那是一种麻醉剂，因为它仅限于对和平岁月的愉快回顾。威尔莫特对我那片地方很熟，他见过我们当地许多有名的人物。于是我们便能作一次肯特郡威尔德与苏塞克斯交界地带的小旅行，仿佛骑着两辆精神上的自行车。我们在想象中、在一个晴朗的夏日午后骑行，经过那些与我一生永远不可分离的里程碑。在乡绅莫恩德尔（Squire Maundle）庄园的大门外，我们共同分享了一幅清晰的画面：他正俯身对着他的高尔夫球，那副棱角分明的姿势，背景是安布尔赫斯特（Amblehurst）上方阳光山坡上的羊群——叮当的铃铛声和*咩咩*的羊叫（他身后总跟着一条沉默寡言的黑寻回犬）。关于士兵被前线生活降低到何种麻木不仁的状态，人们已经说了许多；我不否认这一点，但我倾向于认为，其中也有相当分量的、单纯的感伤主义。就我而言，在战壕里，再家常不过的话题都不嫌家常。

===Original===
Thus, while working-parties and machine-gunners filed past the door with hollow grumbling voices, our private recess in the Hindenburg Tunnel was precariously infused with evocations of rural England and we challenged our surroundings with remembrances of parish names and farmhouses with friendly faces. A cottage garden was not an easy idea to recover convincingly.… Bees among yellow wallflowers on a warm afternoon. The smell of an apple orchard in autumn.… Such details were beyond our evocation. But they were implied when I mentioned Squire Maundle in his four-wheeled dogcart, rumbling along the Dumbridge Road to attend a County Council Meeting.

> “*Secret.* The Bombing Parties of 25 men will rendezvous at 2:30 a.m. tomorrow morning, 16th inst. in shafts near C Coy. H.Q. The greatest care will be taken that each separate Company Party keeps to one side of the Shaft and that the Dump of Bombs be in the trench at the head of these shafts, suitably split. The necessity of keeping absolute silence must be impressed on all men. These parties (under 2nd Lt. Sherston) will come under the orders of O.C. Cameronians at **zero** minus 10. Lt. Dunning and 2 orderlies will act liaison and report to O.C. Cameronians at **zero** minus 5. While the parties are in the shaft they must keep a free passage way clear for runners, etc.”

Such was the document which (had I been less fortunate) would have been my passport to the Stygian shore. In the meantime, with another two hours to sit through, we carried on with our world without end conversation. We were, I think, on the subject of Canterbury Cricket Week when my watch warned me that I must be moving on. As I got up from the table on which we’d been leaning our elbows, a blurred version of my face looked at me from the foggy mirror with an effect of clairvoyance. Hoping that this was an omen of survival, I went along to the rendezvous-shaft and satisfied myself that the Bombing Parties were sitting on the stairs in a bone-chilling draught, with my two subordinate officers in attendance.

===Chinese===
于是，当一支支的劳役队和机枪手们以空洞的抱怨声从门外鱼贯而过时，我们在兴登堡隧道里那处私密的小窝，便摇摇欲坠地注入了英格兰乡村的种种幻象；我们以教区名字和有着友善面孔的农舍为武器，向我们周遭的一切发起挑战。一座村舍花园，要把它真切地唤回脑海里，可不是件容易事。……一个暖和的下午，黄墙花丛中的蜜蜂。秋日苹果园的气息。……这种种细节，是我们唤不回来的。可当我提及乡绅莫恩德尔坐着他的四轮双座马车，沿着邓布里奇路（Dumbridge Road）辘辚前去参加郡议会会议时，这一切都尽在不言中了。

> 「*机密。* 投弹队二十五人，将于明日晨、即本月十六日凌晨二时三十分，在 C 连指挥部附近的竖井内集合。务必注意：各连单独的小队须靠在竖井的一侧；炸弹堆放点设在这些竖井顶头的壕内，妥为分堆。务必使全体人员牢记保持绝对安静。上述各队（由二等舍尔斯顿少尉指挥）将于**零时**减十分归卡梅伦苏格兰步枪兵长官节制。邓宁中尉及两名勤务兵任联络，于**零时**减五分向卡梅伦苏格兰步枪兵长官报到。各队在竖井内时，须留出一条供传令兵等通行的通畅通道。」

这便是那份文件——倘若不是我运气好，它本会是我通往冥河彼岸的通行证。眼下，还得再熬两个钟头，我们便把我们那场没完没了的谈话继续下去。我记得我们正谈到坎特伯雷板球周（Canterbury Cricket Week），我的表提醒我该动了。我从我们一直撑着双肘的那张桌子边站起来时，自己那张模糊了的脸从雾蒙蒙的镜子里望着我，一副洞穿一切的神气。我指望这是我能活下来的吉兆，便动身前往集合竖井，确认投弹队已坐在楼梯上一股冰冷刺骨的穿堂风里，我那两名下属军官也随侍在侧。

===Original===
Zero hour was at 3 a.m. and the prefatory uproar was already rumbling overhead. Having tightened my mud-caked puttees and put my tie straight (there was no rule against wearing a tie in an attack), diffidently I entered the Cameronian H.Q. dugout, which was up against the foot of the stairs. I was among strangers, and Zero minus 10 wasn’t a time for conversational amenities, so I sat self-consciously while the drumming din upstairs was doing its utmost to achieve a reassuring climax. Three o’clock arrived. The tick-tacking telephone-orderly in a corner received a message that the attack had started. They were over the barrier now, and bombing up the trench. The Cameronian Colonel and his Adjutant conversed in the constrained undertones of men who expect disagreeable news. The Colonel was a fine looking man, but his well-disciplined face was haggard with anxiety. Dunning sat in another corner, serious and respectful, with his natural jollity ready to come to the surface whenever it was called for.
At the end of twenty minutes’ tension the Colonel exclaimed abruptly, “Good God, I wish I knew how they’re doing!”… And then, as if regretting his manifestation of feeling, “No harm in having a bit of cake, anyhow.” There was a large homemade cake on the table. I was offered a slice, which I munched with embarrassment. I felt that I had no business to be there at all, let alone helping to make a hole in the Colonel’s cake, which was a jolly good one. I couldn’t believe that these competent officers were counting on me to be of any use to them if I were required to take an active part in the proceedings upstairs. Then the telephone-orderly announced that communication with Captain Macnair’s headquarters had broken down; after that the suspense continued monotonously. I had been sitting there about two and a half hours when it became evident that somebody was descending the steps in a hurry. H.Q. must have kept its cooking utensils on the stairs, for the visitor arrived outside the doorway in a clattering cascade of pots and pans. He was a breathless and dishevelled sergeant, who blurted out an incoherent statement about their having been driven back after advancing a short distance. While the Colonel questioned him in a quiet and controlled voice I rose stiffly to my feet. I don’t remember saying anything or receiving any orders; but I felt that the Cameronian officers were sensitive to the delicacy of my situation. There was no question of another slice of homemade cake. Their unuttered comment was, “Well, old chap, I suppose you’re for it now.”
Leaving them to get what satisfaction they could from the sergeant’s story, I grinned stupidly at Dunning, popped my helmet on my head, and made for the stairway. It must have been a relief to be doing something definite at last, for without pausing to think I started off with the section of twenty-five who were at the top of the stairs. Sergeant Baldock got them on the move at once, although they were chilled and drowsy after sitting there for over three hours. None of them would have been any the worse for a mouthful of rum at that particular moment. In contrast to the wearisome candlelight of the lower regions, the outdoor world was bright and breezy; animated also by enough noise to remind me that some sort of battle was going on. As we bustled along, the flustered little contingent at my heels revived its numbness. I had no idea what I was going to do; our destination was in the brain of the stooping Cameronian guide who trotted ahead of me. On the way we picked up a derelict Lewis gun, which, I thought, might come in handy, though there was no ammunition with it. At the risk of being accused of “taking the wrong half of the conversation” (a favourite phrase of Aunt Evelyn’s) I must say that I felt quite confident. (Looking back on that emergency from my armchair, I find some difficulty in believing that I was there at all.) For about ten minutes we dodged and stumbled up a narrow winding trench. The sun was shining; large neutral clouds voyaged willingly with the wind; I felt intensely alive and rather out of breath. Suddenly we came into the main trench, and where it was widest we met the Cameronians. I must have picked up a bomb on the way, for I had one in my hand when I started my conversation with young Captain Macnair. Our encounter was more absurd than impressive. Macnair and his exhausted men were obviously going in the wrong direction, and I was an incautious newcomer. Consequently I had the advantage of him while he told me that the Germans were all round them and they’d run out of bombs. Feeling myself to be, for the moment, an epitome of Flintshire infallibility, I assumed an air of jaunty unconcern; tossing my bomb carelessly from left hand to right and back again, I inquired, “But where *are* the Germans?”—adding “I can’t see any of them.” This effrontery had its effect (though for some reason I find it difficult to describe this scene without disliking my own behaviour). The Cameronian officers looked around them and recovered their composure. Resolved to show them what intrepid reinforcements we were, I assured Macnair that he needn’t worry any more and we’d soon put things straight. I then led my party past his, halted them, and went up the trench with Sergeant Baldock—an admirably impassive little man who never ceased to behave like a perfectly trained and confidential manservant. After climbing over some sort of barricade, we went about fifty yards without meeting anyone. Observing a good many Mills bombs lying about in little heaps, I sent Baldock back to have them collected and carried further up the trench. Then, with an accelerated heart beat, I went round the corner by myself. Unexpectedly, a small man was there, standing with his back to me, stock-still and watchful, a haversack of bombs slung over his left shoulder. I saw that he was a Cameronian corporal; we did not speak. I also carried a bag of bombs; we went round the next bay. There my adventurous ardour experienced a sobering shock. A fair-haired Scotch private was lying at the side of the trench in a pool of his own blood. His face was grey and serene, and his eyes stared emptily at the sky. A few yards further on the body of a German officer lay crumpled up and still. The wounded Cameronian made me feel angry, and I slung a couple of bombs at our invisible enemies, receiving in reply an egg-bomb, which exploded harmlessly behind me. After that I went bombing busily along, while the corporal (more artful and efficient than I was) dodged in and out of the saps—a precaution which I should have forgotten. Between us we created quite a demonstration of offensiveness, and in this manner arrived at our objective without getting more than a few glimpses of retreating field-grey figures. I had no idea where our objective was, but the corporal informed me that we had reached it, and he seemed to know his business. This, curiously enough, was the first time either of us had spoken since we met.

===Chinese===
零时是凌晨三点，头顶上那场开场锣鼓般的喧嚣已经在轰隆作响。我把糊满泥的绑腿扎紧，把领带理直（进攻时打领带并不犯规），便讪讪地走进卡梅伦人的营部掩体——它就在楼梯脚下。我处在一群陌生人中间，而「零时减十分」这种时候不是寒暄的场合，于是我局促地坐着，头顶上咚咚作响的喧嚣正使出浑身解数，要达到一个令人安心的顶点。三点到了。角落里那个嘀嗒操作的电话兵接到一个消息：进攻已经开始了。他们这时已越过障碍，正沿战壕往上扔手榴弹。卡梅伦上校和他的副官以一种克制的低声交谈着，那是有坏消息等着的人的口气。上校相貌堂堂，但他那张训练有素的脸因焦虑而憔悴。邓宁坐在另一个角落，严肃恭敬，他那与生俱来的快活劲随时准备在需要时浮上脸来。
二十分钟的紧张之后，上校突然喊道：「老天，我多想知道他们打得怎么样！」……接着，像是后悔流露了感情，他又说：「不管怎样，来块蛋糕也无妨嘛。」桌上有一大块自家做的蛋糕。他们给了我一片，我尴尬地嚼着。我觉得自己根本没资格待在这里，更不用说还在啃上校的蛋糕——那可是块顶呱呱的蛋糕。我无法相信，这些干练的军官竟然指望我真能在上头那场行动里帮上什么忙。随后电话兵报告：与麦克奈尔上尉（Captain Macnair）指挥部的通讯中断了；此后这种悬念便单调地延续下去。我在那里坐了大约两个半钟头，忽然听得有人匆匆下楼梯。营部大概把炊具堆在楼梯上了，因为那位来宾抵达门外时，伴着锅碗瓢盆一阵哗啦啦的响。他是一名喘着粗气、衣冠不整的中士，上气不接下气、语无伦次地说：他们往前推进了一小段之后便被打了回来。上校以平静而克制的声音盘问他时，我僵硬地站起身来。我不记得自己说过什么，或接到过什么命令；可我感到卡梅伦的军官们很体谅我的微妙处境。再来一块自家做的蛋糕是别想了。他们没说出口的评论是：「唉，老伙计，我看这下轮到你了。」
我让他们自己去从那位中士的讲述里找点安慰，然后傻乎乎地朝邓宁咧嘴一笑，把头盔往头上一扣，奔向楼梯。终于能做点明确的事了，想必是一种解脱，因为我毫不迟疑地带着在楼梯顶上的那二十五人的一组便出发了。鲍尔多克中士（Sergeant Baldock）立刻让他们动起来，尽管他们在那里坐了三个多钟头、又冷又困。那一刻，他们谁都不会嫌弃一口朗姆酒。与下头那些乏味的烛光形成对比，外头的世界明亮而有风；还有足够的噪声提醒我：某种战斗正在进行。我们一路匆匆前行，脚后头那队慌张的小队伍也振作了麻木的神经。我不知道自己要去做什么；我们的目的地，只存在于那个弓着身子、在我前头小跑的卡梅伦向导的脑子里。路上我们捡到了一挺被遗弃的路易斯机枪，我心想这东西说不定能派上用场，尽管没有弹药。冒着被人指责「只听了对话一半」的危险（这是伊芙琳姨母爱用的一句话），我得说：我当时感觉相当有把握。（事后从扶手椅里回望那场紧急关头，我竟有些难以相信自己当时真在场。）约莫有十分钟，我们在一条狭窄蜿蜒的战壕里闪转腾挪、跌跌撞撞地前进。阳光明媚；大朵大朵中立色彩的云顺遂地随风远航；我感到自己生机勃勃，又有点喘不过气。忽然我们走进了主壕，在它最宽的地方遇见了卡梅伦人。我准是路上捡了一颗手榴弹，因为我和年轻的麦克奈尔上尉搭话时，手里正握着一颗。我们这次相遇与其说威风，不如说滑稽。麦克奈尔和他那些筋疲力尽的弟兄显然正朝着错误的方向走，而我是个冒冒失失的新来者。因此，趁他告诉我德国人把他们包围了、他们的手榴弹也打光了的时候，我占了上风。一时觉得自己成了弗林特郡万无一失的化身，我便装出一副满不在乎的神气；把那颗手榴弹随手从左手抛到右手、又抛回来，问道：「可德国人到底*在*哪儿？」——又添了一句：「我一个都看不见。」这种厚脸皮倒真起了作用（尽管出于某种原因，我发现自己描述这一幕时，总免不了讨厌自己的行径）。卡梅伦的军官们四下看了看，恢复了镇定。下定决心要让他们瞧瞧我们这支援军是如何英勇，我向麦克奈尔保证他不必再担心，我们很快就能把局面理顺。然后我领着我那一队人从他旁边走过去，叫他们停下，自己和中士鲍尔多克——一个极其沉着的小个子，一举一动都像个训练有素、口风极紧的贴身男仆——沿战壕往前走。翻过一道什么路障之后，我们又走了约五十码，没碰见任何人。我看见地上散着一堆堆米尔斯手榴弹（Mills bombs），便打发鲍尔多克回去叫人把它们收集起来，搬到战壕更前头去。然后，我心跳加速，独自拐过了那个弯。不料那儿站着一个小个子男人，背朝着我，一动不动、全神戒备，左肩上挎着一袋手榴弹。我看出他是一名卡梅伦人的下士；我们没有说话。我也提着一袋手榴弹；我们绕过下一个壕的拐角。在那里，我那股冒险的热劲儿遭到了一盆冷水。一个金发的苏格兰列兵躺在战壕边上，泡在一摊自己的血里。他脸色灰白而安详，双眼空洞地望着天空。再往前几码，一具德国军官的尸体蜷缩着，纹丝不动。那名受伤的卡梅伦人让我心里生出一股怒气，我便朝我们看不见的敌人扔了几颗手榴弹，回敬我的是一颗卵形手榴弹，在我身后无害地炸开了。此后我便一路忙着扔手榴弹，而那位下士（比我更机灵、也更老练）在各个支壕间忽进忽出——这种谨慎我本该想不到的。我们俩协力制造了一场相当像样的进攻姿态，就这样、只瞥见过几次退却的德军灰绿色身影，便到达了我们的目标。我根本不知道我们的目标在哪里，但那下士告诉我我们已经到了，而他似乎对自己干的事心里有数。说来奇怪，这是我们俩相遇以来头一回开口说话。

===Original===
The whole affair had been so easy that I felt like pushing forward until we bumped into something more definite. But the corporal had a cooler head and he advised discretion. I told him to remain where he was and started to explore a narrow sap on the left side of the trench. (Not that it matters whether it was on the left side or the right, but it appears to be the only detail I can remember; and when all is said and done, the War was mainly a matter of holes and ditches.) What I expected to find along that sap, I can’t say. Finding nothing, I stopped to listen. There seemed to be a lull in the noise of the attack along the line. A few machine-guns tapped, spiteful and spasmodic. High up in the fresh blue sky an aeroplane droned and glinted. I thought what a queer state of things it all was, and then decided to take a peep at the surrounding country. This was a mistake which ought to have put an end to my terrestrial adventures, for no sooner had I popped my silly head out of the sap than I felt a stupendous blow in the back between my shoulders. My first notion was that a bomb had hit me from behind, but what had really happened was that I had been sniped from in front. Anyhow my foolhardy attitude toward the Second Battle of the Scarpe had been instantaneously altered for the worse. I leant against the side of the sap and shut my eyes.… When I reopened them Sergeant Baldock was beside me, discreet and sympathetic, and to my surprise I discovered that I wasn’t dead. He helped me back to the trench, gently investigated my wound, put a field-dressing on it, and left me sitting there while he went to bring up some men.

===Chinese===
整个过程如此顺利，以至于我直想往前推进，直到撞上点更明确的东西才罢休。可那位下士头脑更冷静，他劝我谨慎从事。我让他留在原地，自己开始去探查战壕左侧的一条支壕。（左边还是右边其实不打紧，但这似乎是我唯一能记起的细节了；归根结底，这场战争主要是桩关于坑和沟的事。）我指望沿着那条支壕找到什么，我说不上来。什么也没找到，我便停下细听。整条战线进攻的噪声似乎暂歇了。几挺机枪断断续续、恶狠狠地嗒嗒着。高高的、澄澈的蓝天上，一架飞机嗡嗡作响、闪着光。我心想这一切真是一种古怪的局面，然后决定探出头去，窥看一下周围的地势。这是一个错误，本该就此终结我在尘世的冒险——因为我那颗蠢头刚从支壕里探出去，便感到背上两肩之间挨了结结实实的一下重击。我的头一个念头是从背后飞来了一颗手榴弹，但实际发生的事是：我挨了从正面射来的一发冷枪。无论如何，我对第二次斯卡普河之役（Second Battle of the Scarpe）那副冒失的姿态，瞬间便急转直下。我靠在支壕壕壁上，闭上了眼睛。……等我重新睁开眼时，鲍尔多克中士已在我身边，审慎而同情；我惊讶地发现自己并没有死。他扶我回到战壕里，轻轻查了查我的伤口，给我裹上一副急救绷带，让我坐在那里，他自己则去带人上来。

===Original===
After a short spell of being deflated and sorry for myself, I began to feel rabidly heroical again, but in a slightly different style, since I was now a wounded hero, with my arm in a superfluous sling. All my seventy-five men were now on the scene (minus a few who had been knocked out by our own shells, which were dropping short). I can remember myself talking volubly to a laconic Stokes-gun officer, who had appeared from nowhere with his weapon and a couple of assistants. I felt that I must make one more onslaught before I turned my back on the War, and my only idea was to collect all available ammunition and then renew the attack while the Stokes-gun officer put up an enthusiastic barrage. It did not occur to me that anything else was happening on Allenby’s Army Front except my own little show. My overstrained nerves had wrought me up to such a pitch of excitement that I was ready for any suicidal exploit. This convulsive energy might have been of some immediate value had there been any objective for it. But there was none; and before I had time to inaugurate anything rash and irrelevant Dunning arrived to relieve me. His air of competent unconcern sobered me down, but I was still inflamed with the offensive spirit and my impetuosity was only snuffed out by a written order from the Cameronian Colonel, who forbade any further advance owing to the attack having failed elsewhere. My ferocity fizzled out then, and I realized that I had a raging thirst. As I was starting my return journey (I must have known then that nothing could stop me till I got to England) the M.O. came sauntering up the trench with the detached demeanour of a gentle botanist. “Trust him to be up there having a look round,” I thought. Within four hours of leaving it I was back in the Tunnel.

===Chinese===
经过一段短暂的自怨自艾、垂头丧气之后，我又开始感到一种近乎狂热的英雄气概，不过换了一种不大一样的调子，因为我现在是一名负伤的英雄，胳膊上多余地挂着一条悬带。我那七十五个人这时都到了现场（减去几个被我们自己打短了的炮弹撂倒的）。我记得自己滔滔不绝地和一个寡言少语的斯托克斯迫击炮军官说话——他带着他的武器和两名助手，不知从哪儿冒了出来。我觉得我必须背过这场战争之前再发起一次冲击，而我唯一的念头便是：把所有能弄到的弹药都收集起来，趁那位斯托克斯迫击炮军官打出一道热情洋溢的弹幕时，重新发起进攻。我压根儿没想到，在艾伦比的集团军正面，除了我这一小出戏之外还发生过别的事。我那过度紧张的神经已把我煽到这样一种亢奋的地步，以至于我什么样的送死勾当都干得出来。这种痉挛般的精力，倘若有什么目标，倒也许能有些即时的价值。可目标并无；而我还来不及启动什么莽撞而文不对题的举动，邓宁便来接替我。他那副干练而漫不经心的神气使我冷静下来，但我仍燃烧着进攻精神，我的这股冲动，直到卡梅伦上校一道书面命令下达才被扑灭——他禁止任何进一步的推进，因为别处的进攻已经失败。我这股凶劲这才泄了下去，我也才意识到自己渴得厉害。我动身往回走时（那时我一定已经明白：在我回到英国之前，什么也拦不住我了），军医官正以一种植物学家似的、超然的派头，沿着战壕慢悠悠地踱上来。「他不愧是他，准是上来看看热闹的，」我想。离开战场四个钟头之内，我便又回到了隧道里。
---
===Original===
Back at Battalion Headquarters in the Tunnel I received from our Colonel and Adjutant generous congratulations on my supposedly dashing display. In the emergency candlelight of that draughty cellar-recess I bade them goodbye with voluble assurances that I should be back in a few weeks; but I was so overstrained and excited that my assurances were noises rather than notions. Probably I should have been equally elated without my wound; but if unwounded, I’d have been still up at the Block with the bombing parties. In the meantime, nothing that happened to me could relieve Battalion H.Q. of its burdens. The Adjutant would go on till he dropped, for he had an inexhaustible sense of duty. I never saw him again; he was killed in the autumn up at Ypres.… I would like to be able to remember that I smiled grimly and departed reticently. But the “bombing show” had increased my self-importance, and my exodus from the front-line was a garrulous one. A German bullet had passed through me leaving a neat hole near my right shoulder-blade and this patriotic perforation had made a different man of me. I now looked at the War, which had been a monstrous tyrant, with liberated eyes. For the time being I had regained my right to call myself a private individual.
The first stage of my return journey took me to the Advanced Dressing Station at Henin. My servant went with me, carrying my haversack. He was a quiet clumsy middle-aged man who always did his best and never complained. While we picked our way along the broken ground of Henin Hill I continued talkative, halting now and again to recover breath and take a last stare at the blighted slope where yesterday I had stumbled to and fro with my working party.
The sky was now overcast and the landscape grey and derelict. The activities of the attack had subsided, and we seemed to be walking in a waste land where dead men had been left out in the rain after being killed for no apparent purpose. Here and there, figures could be seen moving toward the Dressing Station, some of them carrying stretchers.

===Chinese===
回到隧道里的营部，我从我们上校和副官那里，得到了对我那场「据说神勇」表现的慷慨道贺。在那个四面漏风的地窖凹室里、应急的烛光下，我絮絮叨叨地向他们保证，几个礼拜后我就回来；可我实在过度紧张、过度兴奋，以至于我这些保证是些声响，而不是什么念头。说不定我即便没挂彩，也会同样兴高采烈；可若没挂彩，我便会还在那个路障那儿和投弹队在一起。眼下，发生在我身上的任何事，都不能减轻营部的重担。副官会一直干到倒下为止，因为他有一种无穷无尽的责任感。我再没见过他；当年秋天，他在伊普尔阵亡了。……我很想能记得自己当时冷冷一笑、沉默地离去。可那场「投弹戏」助长了我的自大，我离开前沿的过程是一场喋喋不休的退场。一颗德国子弹穿过我的身体，在我右肩胛骨附近留下了一个利落的小洞，这一处爱国的穿孔让我变成了另一个人。我如今以一双解放了的眼睛，去打量这场曾经是庞然大物般的暴君的战争。眼下，我重新获得了自称是一个「独立个体」的权利。
我归途的第一程，是到埃南（Henin）的前进包扎所。我的勤务兵跟着我，背着我的背囊。他是个安静、笨手笨脚的中年人，一向尽力，从不抱怨。我们在埃南山（Henin Hill）那片崎岖的地面上一路挑着路走，我还是话多，时不时停下喘口气，再看我昨天带劳役队跌来撞去的那片荒山坡最后一眼。
这时天已阴了，风景是一片灰败的废墟。进攻的活动已经平息，我们仿佛走在一片荒原里，那里的死人被杀得莫明其妙、然后被丢在雨里。四处可见有人影朝包扎所移动，其中有的抬着担架。

===Original===
It was the midday stagnation which usually followed an early morning attack. The Dressing Station was a small underground place crowded with groaning wounded. Two doctors were doing what they could for men who had paid a heavy price for their freedom. My egocentricity diminished among all that agony. I remember listening to an emotional padre who was painfully aware that he could do nothing except stand about and feel sympathetic. The consolations of the Church of England weren’t much in demand at an Advance Dressing Station. I was there myself merely to go through the formality of being labelled “walking wounded.” I was told to go on to a place called “B. Echelon,” which meant another three miles of muddy walking. Beat to the world, I reached B. Echelon, and found our Quartermaster in a tent with several officers newly arrived from the Base and one or two back from leave. Stimulated by a few gulps of whisky and water, I renewed my volubility and talked nineteen to the dozen until the kind Quartermaster put me into the mess-cart which carried me to a crossroad where I waited for a motorbus. There, after a long wait, I shook hands with my servant, and the handshake seemed to epitomize my goodbye to the Second Battalion. I thanked him for looking after me so well; but one couldn’t wish a man luck when he was going back to the Hindenburg Trench. It may be objected that my attitude towards the Western Front was too intimate; but this was a question of two human beings, one of whom was getting out of it comfortably while the other went back to take his chance in the world’s worst war.… In the bus, wedged among “walking wounded,” I was aware that I had talked quite enough. For an hour and a half we bumped and swayed along ruined roads till we came to the Casualty Clearing Station at Warlencourt. It was seven o’clock and all I got that night was a cup of Bovril and an anti-tetanus injection.
The place was overcrowded with bad cases and I had to wait until after midnight for a bed. I remember sitting in a chair listening to the rain pelting on the roof of the tent and the wailing of a wintry wind. I was too exhausted to sleep; my head had lost control of its thoughts, which continued to reecho my goodbye garrulities; the injection had made me feel chilly and queer, and my wound began to be painful. But I was able to feel sorry for “the poor old Battalion” (which was being relieved that night) and to be thankful for my own lucky escape.
What I’d been through was nothing compared with the sort of thing that many soldiers endured over and over again; nevertheless I condoled with myself on having had no end of a bad time.
Next afternoon a train (with 500 men and 35 officers on board) conveyed me to a Base Hospital. My memories of that train are strange and rather terrible, for it carried a cargo of men in whose minds the horrors they had escaped from were still vitalized and violent. Many of us still had the caked mud of the war zone on our boots and clothes, and every bandaged man was accompanied by his battle experience. Although many of them talked lightly and even facetiously about it, there was an aggregation of enormities in the atmosphere of that train. I overheard some slightly wounded officers who were excitedly remembering their adventures up at Wancourt, where they’d been bombed out of a trench in the dark. Their jargoning voices mingled with the rumble and throb of the train as it journeyed—so safely and sedately—through the environing gloom. The Front Line was behind us; but it could lay its hand on our hearts, though its bludgeoning reality diminished with every mile. It was as if we were pursued by the Arras Battle which had now become a huge and horrible idea. We might be boastful or sagely reconstructive about our experience, in accordance with our different characters. But our minds were still out of breath and our inmost thoughts in disorderly retreat from bellowing darkness and men dying out in shell-holes under the desolation of returning daylight. We were the survivors; few among us would ever tell the truth to our friends and relations in England. We were carrying something in our heads which belonged to us alone, and to those we had left behind us in the battle. There were dying men, too, on board that Red Cross train, men dying for their country in comparative comfort.
We reached our destination after midnight, and the next day I was able to write in my diary, “I am still feeling warlike and quite prepared to go back to the Battalion in a few weeks; I am told that my wound will be healed in a fortnight. The doctor here says I am a lucky man as the bullet missed my jugular vein and spine by a fraction of an inch. I know it would be better for me not to go back to England, where I should probably be landed for at least three months and then have all the hell of returning again in July or August.” But in spite of my self-defensive scribble I was in London on Friday evening, and by no means sorry to be carried through the crowd of patriotic spectators at Charing Cross Station. My stretcher was popped into an ambulance which took me to a big hospital at Denmark Hill. At Charing Cross a woman handed me a bunch of flowers and a leaflet by the Bishop of London who earnestly advised me to lead a clean life and attend Holy Communion.

===Chinese===
那是清晨进攻之后惯有的午间沉寂。包扎所是地下的一处小小地方，挤满了呻吟的伤员。两名医生正为那些为自己自由付出沉重代价的人，尽力而为。在那一屋子的痛苦之中，我的自大缩减了。我记得我听一位动情的随军牧师讲话，他痛苦地意识到：自己除了站在一旁、满怀同情之外，什么也做不了。英国国教的慰藉，在前进包扎所里并不怎么吃香。我在那里，不过是走个被贴上「能走动的伤员」标签的形式。他们叫我往前走到一个叫「B 梯队」（B. Echelon）的地方去，这等于还要在泥里再走三英里。我累得要死，到了 B 梯队，在帐篷里找到了我们的军需官，还有几位刚从基地来的军官和一两个休假回来的。几口威士忌兑水下去，我又来了精神，话篓子打开了，滔滔不绝地讲个没完，直到好心的军需官把我塞进伙食车，载我到一个十字路口等公共汽车。在那里等了许久之后，我和我的勤务兵握了手；这一握，似乎便概括了我与第二营的告别。我谢他把我照料得这么好；可是，当一个人要回到兴登堡战壕去时，你是没法祝他「好运」的。也许有人会非议我对西线的态度过于亲昵；可这是两个活生生的人之间的事——其中一个舒舒服服地要脱身出去了，而另一个却要回去、在这世界上最糟糕的战争里碰运气。……在公共汽车里，我被夹在「能走动的伤员」中间，意识到自己说得已经够多了。我们沿着毁坏的道路颠簸摇晃了一个半钟头，才来到沃伦库尔（Warlencourt）的伤员急救站。这时是七点钟，我那一夜得到的全部，不过是一杯波弗利（Bovril）饮料和一针防破伤风。
这地方挤满了重症病人，我得等到午夜过后才有床。我记得自己坐在一把椅子上，听雨点打在帐篷顶上，听冬般的寒风呼号。我已累到睡不着；脑子管不住自己的念头，它们不停地回响着我那番告别的喋喋不休；那针打下去叫我发冷发怪，伤口也开始疼了。可我仍能为「可怜的老营」（当夜正在换防）感到难过，并为自己幸运脱险而庆幸。
我所经历的，与许多士兵一次又一次所承受的相比，算不了什么；可我还是怜悯自己受了这一场大罪。
次日下午，一列火车（载着五百名士兵和三十五名军官）把我送到一所基地医院。我对那列火车的记忆既奇特又相当可怕，因为它装载着一舱人——在他们脑海里，自己刚刚逃出的恐怖仍然鲜活而猛烈。我们许多人靴子上、衣服上还沾着战区干结的烂泥；每一名缠着绷带的人，都携带着自己的战斗经历。尽管他们中不少人谈起这件事来轻描淡写、甚至插科打诨，那列火车的空气里却聚着一团骇人听闻的东西。我听见一些轻伤军官兴奋地回忆他们在万库尔的历险——他们曾在黑暗里被从一条战壕里炸出来。他们叽叽喳喳的声音，与火车轰隆隆、突突突的声响交织在一起，而火车正——如此安全、如此四平八稳地——穿过四围的黑暗。前沿已被我们甩在身后；可它仍能把一只手按在我们心上，尽管它那种大棒般的现实随着每一英里而减弱。仿佛阿拉斯战役在追赶着我们，它如今已变成了一个巨大而可怕的念头。我们也许会依各自性情的不同，吹嘘或煞有介事地重建自己的经历。但我们的脑子仍在大喘气，我们内心深处的念头仍在凌乱地溃退，逃离那咆哮的黑暗，逃离那些在弹坑里、在黎明归来时的凄凉之下死去的人。我们是幸存者；我们当中极少有人会向英国的亲友说出真相。我们头上带着一样东西，那东西只属于我们自己，以及那些我们留在战场上的人。那列红十字列车上，还有垂死的人，他们在相对的舒适里为国捐躯。
我们在午夜之后抵达目的地，次日我得以在日记里写道：「我仍觉得自己很有些战斗精神，完全做好了几个礼拜后回营的准备；我被告知，我的伤口两星期内便会愈合。这里的医生说我是个走运的人，那颗子弹离我的颈静脉和脊椎只差一点点。我知道，对我来说，不回英国会更好些；要是回了英国，我大概至少要被搁上三个月，然后到了七、八月又得重新经历一遍回前线的种种活罪。」可是，尽管我写下了这番自我开脱的潦草字句，星期五傍晚我已身在伦敦，被人抬着穿过查令十字车站（Charing Cross）那一群爱国的围观群众时，我毫不觉得遗憾。我的担架被塞进一辆救护车，送到了丹麦山（Denmark Hill）一所大医院。在查令十字，一位妇人递给我一束花，还有一份伦敦主教（Bishop of London）的传单，主教恳切地劝我过清洁的生活，并去领圣餐。

## IX Hospital and Convalescence / IX 住院与疗养

### I / 一

===Original===
The first few days were like lying in a boat. Drifting, drifting, I watched the high sunlit windows or the firelight that flickered and glowed on the ceiling when the ward was falling asleep. Outside the hospital a late spring was invading the home service world. Trees were misty-green and sometimes I could hear a blackbird singing. Even the screech and rumble of electric trams was a friendly sound; trams meant safety; the troops in the trenches thought about trams with affection. With an exquisite sense of languor and release I lifted my hand to touch the narcissuses by my bed. They were symbols of an immaculate spirit﻿—creatures whose faces knew nothing of War’s demented language.

For a week, perhaps, I could dream that for me the War was over, because I’d got a neat hole through me and the nurse with her spongings forbade me to have a bath. But I soon emerged from my mental immunity; I began to think; and my thoughts warned me that my second time out in France had altered my outlook (if such a confused condition of mind could be called an outlook). I began to feel that it was my privilege to be bitter about my war experiences; and my attitude toward civilians implied that they couldn’t understand and that it was no earthly use trying to explain things to them. Visitors were, of course, benevolent and respectful; my wound was adequate evidence that I’d “been in the thick of it,” and I allowed myself to hint at heroism and its attendant horrors. But as might have been expected my behaviour varied with my various visitors; or rather it would have done so had my visitors been more various. My inconsistencies might become tedious if tabulated collectively, so I will confine myself to the following imaginary examples.

===Chinese===
头几天像是躺在一艘小船里。飘飘荡荡、飘飘荡荡，我望着高高的、阳光照耀的窗子；病房沉沉睡去时，便望着天花板上明灭闪烁、泛着红光的炉火。医院外头，一场姗姗来迟的春天正悄悄漫进这块国内防务的地界。树木已是朦胧的青绿，有时能听见一只乌鸫在歌唱。就连电车那吱嘎、隆隆的响声也是亲切的；电车意味着安全；战壕里的弟兄们带着柔情想起电车。我怀着一种微妙的慵懒与解脱之感，抬手去摸床边的水仙。它们是一种纯洁无瑕之灵的象征——一些脸上全然不知战争那癫狂语言的小生灵。

约莫有一个星期，我可以梦想这场战争于我已经结束了，因为我身上多了个干净齐整的小洞，而护士给我擦身时不许我洗澡。可我很快便从这种心灵的免役里钻了出来；我开始思索；而我的思索提醒我：这第二次来到法国，已改变了我的看法（倘若脑子里这样一团乱麻也算得上门「看法」的话）。我开始觉得，我有特权对这场战争经历心怀愤懑；我对平民的态度暗示着：他们不懂，向他们解释什么也是白费力气。探视的人，自然是和善而客气的；我的伤足以证明我「确曾身陷火海」，于是我也任自己稍稍暗示些英雄气概及其附带的种种可怖。但不难想见，我的表现因不同的探视者而各异；或者，倘若我的探视者更多样一些，本会如此。我这些前后不一之处若一并列出，未免叫人厌烦，所以我只举下面几个想象中的例子。

===Original===
*Some Senior Officer under whom I’d served*: Modest, politely subordinate, strongly imbued with the “spirit of the Regiment” and quite ready to go out again. “Awfully nice of you to come and see me, sir.” Feeling that I ought to jump out of bed and salute, and that it would be appropriate and pleasant to introduce him to “some of my people” (preferably of impeccable social status). Willingness to discuss active service technicalities and revive memories of shared front-line experience.

*Middle-aged or elderly Male Civilian*: Tendency (in response to sympathetic gratitude for services rendered to King and Country) to assume haggard facial aspect of one who had “been through hell.” Inclination to wish that my wound was a bit worse than it actually was, and have nurses hovering round with discreet reminders that my strength mustn’t be overtaxed. Inability to reveal anything crudely horrifying to civilian sensibilities. “Oh yes, I’ll be out there again by the autumn.” (Grimly wan reply to suggestions that I was now honourably qualified for a home service job.) Secret antagonism to all uncomplimentary references to the German Army.

*Charming Sister of Brother Officer*: Jocular, talkative, debonair, and diffidently heroic. Wishful to be wearing all possible medal-ribbons on pyjama jacket. Able to furnish a bright account of her brother (if still at the front) and suppressing all unpalatable facts about the War. “Jolly decent of you to blow in and see me.”

*Hunting Friend (a few years above Military Service Age)*: Deprecatory about sufferings endured at the front. Tersely desirous of hearing all about last season’s sport. “By Jingo, that must have been a nailing good gallop!” Jokes about the Germans, as if throwing bombs at them was a tolerable substitute for foxhunting. A good deal of guffawing (mitigated by remembrance that I’d got a bullet hole through my lung). Optimistic anticipations of next season’s Opening Meet and an early termination of hostilities on all fronts.

===Chinese===
*我曾在其麾下服役的某位高级军官*：谦逊、礼貌地表示从属、深受「团队精神」熏陶，且很愿意再出国。「您能来看我，真是太好了，长官。」感觉自己理当跳下床敬礼，又觉着把他介绍给「我的一些亲友」（社会地位最好无可挑剔）是得体而愉快的事。乐于讨论现役的种种技术细节，重温共同的前线经历。

*中年或老年的男性平民*：（面对他们感念我为国王与国家效力时）倾向于摆出一副「曾历经地狱」的憔悴神情。巴不得自己的伤比实际的更重一点，让护士们围在身边，得体地提醒大家不可让我劳累。无法向平民的敏感吐露任何赤裸裸的可怖之事。「是啊，到秋天我又要回那边去了。」（这是听见人说我如今已光荣地够格干一份国内差事时，我那苍白而冷峻的回答。）对所有贬损德国陆军的话，暗中怀着抵触。

*战友军官的迷人姐姐*：爱打趣、话多、洒脱，带一点羞怯的英雄气。巴不得睡衣外套上挂满所有能挂的勋章勋带。能生动地讲述她兄弟的事（如果他还在前线），并把关于这场战争一切叫人不快的事实都按下不表。「你能跑来看我，真是太够意思了。」

*猎狐朋友（年纪已超兵役龄几年）*：对前线的苦难不以为意。简短地打听上一季猎狐的种种。「老天爷，那一场准是追得痛快！」拿德国人打趣，仿佛朝他们扔炸弹是猎狐的无伤大雅的替代。哈哈大笑一阵（不过想起我肺上多了个枪眼，笑声有所收敛）。乐观地盼着下一季的开幕猎日和各条战线早日止战。

===Original===
Nevertheless my supposed reactions to any one of these hypothetical visitors could only be temporary. When alone with my fellow patients I was mainly disposed toward a self-pitying estrangement from everyone except the troops in the front-line. (Casualties didn’t count as tragic unless dead or badly maimed.)

When Aunt Evelyn came up to London to see me I felt properly touched by her reticent emotion; embitterment against civilians couldn’t be applied to her. But after she had gone I resented her gentle assumption that I had done enough and could now accept a safe job. I wasn’t going to be messed about like that, I told myself. Yet I knew that the War was unescapable. Sooner or later I should be sent back to the front-line, which was the only place where I could be any use. A cushy wound wasn’t enough to keep me out of it.

===Chinese===
然而，我设想中对待上述任何一个假想访客的反应，都不过是暂时的。与病友独处时，我多半倾向于自怜地与所有人疏离——除了前线战壕里的弟兄们。（伤亡者除非已死或重伤致残，否则都不算可悲。）

伊芙琳姨母上伦敦来看我时，我被她那含蓄的情感真切地打动——对她没法套用对平民的那一套愤懑。可她走后，我又恼火她那温和的设想——以为我已经出够了力，如今大可领一份安全的差事。我才不要被这样摆布呢，我对自己说。然而我知道这场战争是躲不掉的。迟早我会被送回前线，那才是我唯一派得上用场的地方。一点够回国又不致命的轻伤，不足以让我就此置身事外。

===Original===
I couldn’t be free from the War; even this hospital ward was full of it, and every day the oppression increased. Outwardly it was a pleasant place to be lazy in. Morning sunshine slanted through the tall windows, brightening the grey-green walls and the forty beds. Daffodils and tulips made spots of colour under three red-draped lamps which hung from the ceiling. Some officers lay humped in bed, smoking and reading newspapers; others loafed about in dressing-gowns, going to and from the washing room where they scraped the bristles from their contented faces. A raucous gramophone continually ground out popular tunes. In the morning it was ragtime﻿—“Everybody’s Doing It” and “At the Foxtrot Ball.” (“Somewhere a Voice Is Calling, God Send You Back to Me,” and suchlike sentimental songs were reserved for the evening hours.) Before midday no one had enough energy to begin talking war shop, but after that I could always hear scraps of conversation from around the two fireplaces. My eyes were reading one of Lamb’s Essays, but my mind was continually distracted by such phrases as “Barrage lifted at the first objective,” “shelled us with heavy stuff,” “couldn’t raise enough decent N.C.O.s,” “first wave got held up by machine-guns,” and “bombed them out of a sap.”

===Chinese===
我摆脱不开这场战争；连这间病房都装满了它，而且这种压迫与日俱增。表面上，这是个宜于偷懒的好地方。晨光斜斜地穿过那些高窗，照亮灰绿色的墙壁和四十张床。水仙和郁金香在天花板垂下的三盏红灯罩下，缀出一团团色彩。有些军官驼背坐在床上，抽烟看报；还有些披着晨衣晃来晃去，往返于盥洗室之间——他们在那儿刮着心满意足的脸上的胡茬。一架聒噪的留声机不停地磨出流行小调。上午放的是雷格泰姆——「人人都在干」啦、「在狐步舞会上」啦。（「某处有个声音在呼唤，上帝把你送回我身边」之类伤感的歌，要留到傍晚时分才放。）午前还没人有气力开始谈本行的战争经，可过了晌午，我总能从两个壁炉四周听见片段的对话。我眼睛在读兰姆的一篇随笔，心思却屡屡被这样的话打断：「弹幕在第一道目标线上就延伸了」「用重家伙轰了我们」「凑不齐够多像样的军士」「第一波被机枪压住了」「把他们从坑道里炸了出来」。

===Original===
There were no serious cases in the ward, only flesh wounds and sick. These were the lucky ones, already washed clean of squalor and misery and strain. They were lifting their faces to the sunlight, warming their legs by the fire; but there wasn’t much to talk about except the War.

===Chinese===
病房里没有重患，只有些皮肉伤和病号。这些都是运气好的人，已经把污秽、愁苦和紧绷洗刷干净了。他们把脸抬向阳光，在炉边烤着腿；可除了这场战争，也没什么别的可谈。

===Original===
In the evenings they played cards at a table opposite my bed; the blinds were drawn, the electric light was on, and a huge fire glowed on walls and ceiling. Glancing irritably up from my book I criticized the faces of the cardplayers and those who stood watching the game. There was a lean airman in a grey dressing-gown, his narrow whimsical face puffing a cigarette below a turban-like bandage; he’d been brought down by the Germans behind Arras and had spent three days in a bombarded dugout with Prussians, until our men drove them back and rescued him. The Prussians hadn’t treated him badly, he said. His partner was a swarthy Canadian with a low beetling forehead, sneering wide-set eyes, fleshy cheeks, and a loose heavy mouth. I couldn’t like that man, especially when he was boasting how he “did in some prisoners.” Along the ward they were still talking about “counterattacked from the redoubt,” “permanent rank of captain,” “never drew any allowances for six weeks,” “failed to get through their wire”﻿ ﻿… I was beginning to feel the need for escape from such reminders. My brain was screwed up tight, and when people came to see me I answered their questions excitedly and said things I hadn’t intended to say.

===Chinese===
傍晚，他们在我的床对面一张桌边打牌；百叶窗拉下，电灯亮着，巨大的炉火在墙壁和天花板上发着光。我烦躁地从书上抬眼，挑剔那些打牌的人和围观者的脸。有个瘦瘦的飞行员，穿着灰色晨衣，那张窄而古怪的脸在缠头巾似的绷带下叼着香烟——德国人把他击落在阿拉斯后方，他曾与普鲁士人在一个挨炸的掩体里待了三天，直到我们的人把他们赶回去、把他救出来。他说那些普鲁士人待他不坏。他的搭档是个黝黑的加拿大人，眉骨低低压下来，两只分得很开的眼睛带着讥讽，两颊多肉，一张松垮厚重的大嘴。我喜欢不上那个人，尤其是他吹嘘自己如何「收拾了几个俘虏」的时候。沿着病房这头，人们还在谈着「从据点反攻啦」「上尉的实授军衔啦」「整整六周没领到任何津贴啦」「没能突破他们的铁丝网啦」……我开始感到需要逃离这些提醒。我的脑子拧得紧紧的，有人来看我时，我激动地回答他们的问题，说出些本不想说的话。

===Original===
From the munition factory across the road, machinery throbbed and droned and crashed like the treading of giants; the noise got on my nerves. I was being worried by bad dreams. More than once I wasn’t sure whether I was awake or asleep; the ward was half shadow and half sinking firelight, and the beds were quiet with huddled sleepers. Shapes of mutilated soldiers came crawling across the floor; the floor seemed to be littered with fragments of mangled flesh. Faces glared upward; hands clutched at neck or belly; a livid grinning face with bristly moustache peered at me above the edge of my bed; his hands clawed at the sheets. Some were like the dummy figures used to deceive snipers; others were alive and looked at me reproachfully, as though envying me the warm safety of life which they’d longed for when they shivered in the gloomy dawn, waiting for the whistles to blow and the bombardment to lift.﻿ ﻿… A young English private in battle equipment pulled himself painfully toward me and fumbled in his tunic for a letter; as he reached forward to give it to me his head lolled sideways and he collapsed; there was a hole in his jaw and the blood spread across his white face like ink spilt on blotting-paper.

===Chinese===
路对面那家兵工厂里，机器像巨人的脚步一般抽动、轰鸣、撞击；这声音叫人心烦。我被噩梦所纠缠。不止一次，我分不清自己是醒着还是睡着；病房半是阴影、半是沉落的炉光，床铺静谧，蜷着一床床的睡客。残缺士兵的身影爬过地板向我爬来；地板上仿佛到处散落着血肉模糊的碎块。一张张脸向上瞪视；一只只手揪住自己的脖子或腹部；一张青灰色的、龇牙咧脸的脸，硬茬胡子，从我床沿上方盯着我；他的手在床单上抓挠。有的像是用来骗过狙击手的假人；有的却是活的，责难地望着我，仿佛嫉妒我生命中那份温暖与安全——而当他们在阴暗的黎明里发抖、等着哨子吹响、等着炮火延伸时，正是盼着这样的温暖与安全。……一个英国青年列兵，全副武装，痛苦地拖着身子向我爬来，在军上衣里摸索一封信；他探身要把信递给我时，脑袋一歪瘫倒了；他下巴上有个洞，血像墨水泼在吸墨纸上那样，在他苍白的脸上洇开。

===Original===
Violently awake, I saw the ward without its phantoms. The sleepers were snoring and a nurse in grey and scarlet was coming silently along to make up the fire.

===Chinese===
猛地醒来，我看见脱去了鬼影的病房。睡客们打着鼾，一个穿灰红两色衣裳的护士正悄无声息地走过来添火。

### II / 二

===Original===
Although I have stated that after my first few days in hospital I “began to think,” I cannot claim that my thoughts were clear or consistent. I did, however, become definitely critical and inquiring about the War. While feeling that my infantry experience justified this, it did not occur to me that I was by no means fully informed on the subject. In fact I generalized intuitively, and was not unlike a young man who suddenly loses his belief in religion and stands up to tell the Universal Being that He doesn’t exist, adding that if He does, He treats the world very unjustly. I shall have more to say later on about my antagonism to the World War; in the meantime it queered my criticisms of it by continually reminding me that the Adjutant had written to tell me that my name had been “sent in for another decoration.” I could find no fault with this hopeful notion, and when I was allowed out of hospital for the first time my vanity did not forget how nice its tunic would look with one of those (still uncommon) little silver rosettes on the M.C. ribbon, which signified a Bar; or, better still, a red and blue D.S.O.

It was May 2nd and warm weather; no one appeared to be annoyed about the War, so why should I worry? Sitting on the top of a bus, I glanced at the editorial paragraphs of the *Unconservative Weekly*. The omniscience of this ably written journal had become the basis of my provocative views on world affairs. I agreed with every word in it and was thus comfortably enabled to disagree with the bellicose patriotism of the *Morning Post*. The only trouble was that an article in the *Unconservative Weekly* was for me a sort of divine revelation. It told me what I’d never known but now needed to believe, and its ratiocinations and political pronouncements passed out of my head as quickly as they entered it. While I read I concurred; but if I’d been asked to restate the arguments I should have contented myself with saying “It’s what I’ve always felt myself, though I couldn’t exactly put it into words.”

===Chinese===
虽说我说过住院头几天之后我便「开始思索」，却不能说我的想法是清晰或一贯的。不过，我确实开始明确地对这场战争抱持起批判和追问的态度。我觉得我的步兵经历使我有此资格，却没想到自己在这一题目上远未掌握全部的信息。事实上，我凭直觉大发议论，与一个突然失去宗教信仰、站起来告诉那位至高存在说祂并不存在的年轻人，并无二致——他还要补上一句：就算您存在，您对待这个世界也太不公了。关于我对这场世界大战的抵触，后文我还要再多谈几句；眼下，这种抵触却因我老想起副官来信说我名字已「呈报再授一枚勋章」，而使我对这场战争的批评打了折扣。我对这个美好的念头无话可挑剔；待我头一次获准外出时，我的虚荣心也没忘记：军功十字勋章（M.C.）的勋带上若缀上一枚（当时还稀罕的）小银玫瑰结、表示加章（Bar），那件军上衣该有多好看；再好一点，是一枚红蓝两色的优异服务勋章（D.S.O.）。

那天是五月二日，天气和暖；看起来谁也不为这场战争烦心，我又何必操心？我坐在公共汽车顶层，浏览《非保守派周刊》（*Unconservative Weekly*）的社论短评。这份文笔老到的刊物那种无所不知的口吻，已成了我对世界事务发表挑衅性看法的根据。它的每一句话我都赞同，由此便舒舒服服地得以与《晨邮报》（*Morning Post*）那好战的爱国主义唱反调。唯一的麻烦是，《非保守派周刊》上的一篇文章对我而言，等于一种天启。它告诉我一些我从前不知道、而如今正需要相信的东西，至于它那一套推理与政治论断，从我这一只耳朵进去，便从那一只耳朵出去，来得快去得也快。读的时候我是赞成的；可若要我重述那些论点，我也只能以一句「这本来一直就是我的想法，只是我说不大清楚」自满。

===Original===
The Archbishop of Canterbury was easier to deal with. Smiling sardonically, I imbibed his “Message to the Nation about the War and the Gospel.” “Occasions may arise,” he wrote, “when exceptional obligations are laid upon us. Such an emergency having now arisen, the security of the nation’s food supply may largely depend upon the labour which can be devoted to the land. This being so, we are, I think, following the guidance given in the Gospel if in such a case we make a temporary departure from our rule. I have no hesitation in saying that in the need which these weeks present, men and women may with a clear conscience do fieldwork on Sundays.” Remembering the intense bombardment in front of Arras on Easter Sunday, I wondered whether the Archbishop had given the sanction of the Gospel for that little bit of Sabbath fieldwork. Unconscious that he was, presumably, pained by the War and its barbarities, I glared morosely in the direction of Lambeth Palace and muttered, “Silly old fossil!” Soon afterwards I got off the bus at Piccadilly Circus and went into the restaurant where I had arranged to meet Julian Durley.

===Chinese===
坎特伯雷大主教比较好对付些。我冷笑着读他那篇「关于战争与福音致全国民众书」。「可能会出现某些情况，」他写道，「届时我们须承担非常的义务。如今此种紧急情况已经出现，国家粮食供应的安全，很大程度上取决于能投入土地的劳力。有鉴于此，我以为，在这种情况下我们暂且偏离我们平日的规定，是符合福音之指引的。我毫不犹豫地说，在这几周的特殊需要下，男女信徒尽可怀着无亏的良心在星期日下田劳作。」想起复活节那天在阿拉斯前方的那场猛烈炮击，我寻思大主教阁下是否也用福音的特许，认可了那一小段安息日的「下田劳作」。我并未觉察他想必也正为这场战争及其暴行所苦；只是阴沉地朝兰贝斯宫那个方向瞪了一眼，咕哝一句：「老古董！」不久，我便在皮卡迪利广场下了车，走进那家约好与朱利安·德利（Julian Durley）碰头的餐馆。

===Original===
With Durley I reverted automatically to my active service self. The war which we discussed was restricted to the doings of the Flintshire Fusiliers. Old So-and-so had been wounded; poor old Somebody had been killed in the Bullecourt show; old Somebody Else was still commanding B Company. Old jokes and grotesquely amusing trench incidents were reenacted. The Western Front was the same treacherous blundering tragicomedy which the mentality of the Army had agreed to regard as something between a crude bit of fun and an excuse for a good grumble. I suppose that the truth of the matter was that we were remaining loyal to the realities of our war experience, keeping our separate psychological secrets to ourselves, and avoiding what Durley called “his dangerous tendency to become serious.” His face, however, retained the haunted unhappy look which it had acquired since the Delville Wood attack last autumn, and his speaking voice was still a hoarse whisper.

When I was ordering a bottle of hock we laughed because the waiter told us that the price had been reduced since 1914, as it was now an unpopular wine. The hock had its happy effect, and soon we were agreeing that the front-line was the only place where one could get away from the War. Durley had been making a forlorn attempt to enter the Flying Corps, and had succeeded in being reexamined medically. The examination had started hopefully, as Durley had confined himself to nods and headshakings in reply to questions. But when conversation became inevitable the doctor had very soon asked angrily, “Why the hell don’t you stop that whispering?” The verdict had been against his fractured thyroid cartilage; though, as Durley remarked, it didn’t seem to him to make much difference whether you shouted or whispered when you were up in an aeroplane. “You’ll have to take some sort of office job,” I said. But he replied that he hated the idea, and then illogically advised me to stay in England as long as I could. I asserted that I was going out again as soon as I could get passed for General Service, and called for the bill as though I were thereby settling my destiny conclusively. I emerged from the restaurant without having uttered a single anti-war sentiment.

===Chinese===
和德利在一起，我自然而然地回到了我那个现役军人的自我。我们所谈论的那场战争，局限于弗林特郡燧发枪兵团的那些事。某老兄负伤了；可怜的某君在比勒库尔（Bullecourt）那场仗里阵亡了；另一位某君还在指挥 B 连。从前的旧笑话和那些古怪有趣的堑壕小插曲，被一一重新搬演。西线仍是那同一出背信弃义、跌跌撞撞的悲喜剧——按照军队的脾性，大家已约定俗成地把它看作一桩介于「粗线条的逗乐」与「痛痛快快发顿牢骚的借口」之间的东西。我想，事情的真相大概是：我们对战争经历的那些真实保持忠诚，把各自心底的隐秘揣着不提，并避开德利所谓「他那要变严肃起来的危险倾向」。只是他的脸，自从去年秋天德维尔树林那一仗后，便带上了一种挥之不去的悒郁之色；他说话的嗓音，仍是那般嘶哑的低语。

我们点一瓶莱茵白葡萄酒时笑了起来——侍者告诉我们，这酒价钱从一九一四年起便降下来了，因为如今没人爱喝。这酒起了它的好作用，很快我们便一致同意：前线是唯一能叫人躲开这场战争的地方。德利此前曾作过一次无望的尝试，想进飞行队，并且居然成功地说服了上面给他重新体检。体检开头倒颇有希望，因为德利对提问只用点头和摇头作答。但等到非开口不可时，医生很快就恼怒地问：「你他妈干嘛老是这么悄声悄气的？」最后的判定对他那块碎裂的甲状软骨不利；不过，照德利所说，坐在飞机上头，喊也罢、悄声也罢，似乎也没多大分别。「你只好去弄份什么办公室差事了。」我说。可他回答说他讨厌这个念头，接着又不合逻辑地劝我尽量在英国多待些日子。我断言自己一通过一般勤务的体格检查就要再赴前线，并叫人来结账，仿佛这一来便把自己的命运彻底定下了。我走出餐馆，没说出半句反战的感慨。

===Original===
When Durley had disappeared into his aimless unattached existence, I sat in Hyde Park for an hour before going back to the hospital. What with the sunshine and the effect of the hock, I felt rather drowsy, and the columns of the *Unconservative Weekly* seemed less stimulating than usual.

On the way back to Denmark Hill I diverted my mind by observing the names on shops and business premises. I was rewarded by Pledge (pawnbroker), Money (solicitor), and Stone (builder). There was also an undertaker named Bernard Shaw. But perhaps the most significant name was Fudge (printing works). What use, I thought, were printed words against a war like this? Durley represented the only reality which I could visualize with any conviction. People who told the truth were likely to be imprisoned, and lies were at a premium.﻿ ﻿… All my energy had evaporated and it was a relief to be back in bed. After all, I thought, it’s only sixteen days since I left the Second Battalion, so I’ve still got a right to feel moderately unwell. How luxurious it felt, to be lying there, after a cup of strong tea, with daylight diminishing, and a vague gratitude for being alive at the end of a fine day in late spring. Anyhow the War had taught me to be thankful for a roof over my head at night.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===
德利消失进他那无着无落、无所属的生活之后，我在海德公园坐了一个钟头，才回医院去。许是因为阳光，许是因为那瓶莱茵酒，我颇有些困倦，《非保守派周刊》的几栏文章似乎没有平日那么提神。

在回丹麦山一路，我借观察店铺和写字楼的招牌来分自己的心。果然有所斩获——普莱奇（Pledge，当铺老板，姓字意为「抵押」）、曼尼（Money，律师，姓字意为「金钱」）、斯通（Stone，营造商，姓字意为「石头」）。还有一位殡仪馆老板，名字叫萧伯纳（Bernard Shaw）。不过最有意味的，大约要算福吉（Fudge，印刷厂，姓字意为「胡诌」）。我想，对付这样一场战争，印成的文字究竟有何用处？德利是我唯一能凭任何信心想见出来的真实。说真话的人可能被关进牢里，而谎言倒吃香。……我的精力全泄尽了，回到床上倒是一桩宽慰。毕竟，我想，我离开第二营才不过十六天，所以仍大可名正言顺地觉着有些不大舒服。一杯浓茶之后，躺在那里，白昼一点点暗下去，对这样一个暮春晴日的结尾还活着，怀着一种模糊的感激——这种感觉何其奢侈。无论如何，这场战争教会了我，夜里能有片瓦遮头就该知足了。……

===Original===
Lying awake after the lights were out in the ward, it is possible that I also thought about the Second Battalion. Someone (it must have been Dunning) had sent me some details of the show they’d been in on April 23rd. The attack had been at the place where I’d left them. A little ground had been gained and lost, and then the Germans had retreated a few hundred yards. Four officers had been killed and nine wounded. About forty other ranks killed, including several of the best N.C.O.s. It had been an episode typical of uncountable others, some of which now fill their few pages in Regimental Histories. Such stories look straightforward enough in print, twelve years later; but their reality remains hidden; even in the minds of old soldiers the harsh horror mellows and recedes.

Of this particular local attack the Second Battalion Doctor afterwards wrote, “The occasion was but one of many when a Company or Battalion was sacrificed on a limited objective to a plan of attack ordered by Division or some higher Command with no more knowledge of the ground than might be got from a map of moderate scale.” But for me (as I lay awake and wondered whether I’d have been killed if I’d been there) April 23rd was a blurred picture of people bombing one another up and down ditches; of a Company stumbling across open ground and getting mown down by machine-guns; of the doctor out in the dark with his stretcher-bearers, getting in the wounded; and of an exhausted Battalion staggering back to rest-billets to be congratulated by a genial exculpatory Major-General, who explained that the attack had been ordered by the Corps Commander. I could visualize the Major-General all right, though I wasn’t aware that he was “blaming it on the Corps Commander.” And I knew for certain that Ralph Wilmot was now minus one of his arms, so my anti-war bitterness was enabled to concentrate itself on the fact that he wouldn’t be able to play the piano again. Finally, it can safely be assumed that my entire human organism felt ultra-thankful to be falling asleep in an English hospital. Altruism is an episodic and debatable quality; the instinct for self-preservation always got the last word when an infantryman was lying awake with his thoughts.

===Chinese===
病房熄灯之后，醒着躺在床上，我或许也想到了第二营。有人（一定是邓宁）寄来了他们四月二十三日那场仗的若干细节。进攻就发生在我离开他们的那个地方。一点点地盘有得有失，随后德国人退了几百码。四名军官阵亡、九名负伤。约四十名士兵阵亡，其中包括几位最好的军士。这不过是无数同类小战斗中典型的一例——其中有一些，如今在团队史里占了它们的几页篇幅。十二年之后印在纸上，这些故事看上去也颇为直截了当；可它们的真实却始终藏而不露；即便在老兵们的脑子里，那种惨烈的恐怖也已柔和了、远去了。

关于这一处局部进攻，第二营的军医后来写道：「那一回不过是许多次里的一次——一个连或一个营，被献祭在一个有限的目标上，进攻方案却是师或更高级司令部下达的，他们对地形所知，不过是中等比例尺地图上能看出来的那点东西。」但对我而言（我醒着躺在床上，寻思若我当时在场会不会被打死），四月二十三日是一幅模糊的画面：人们在壕沟里上上下下互相扔炸弹；一个连在开阔地上踉跄前行，被机枪割倒一片；军医带着担架兵摸黑在外面收伤员；一个精疲力竭的营踉踉跄跄回到休息驻地，由一位和蔼的、替人开脱的少将前来嘉勉——这位少将解释说，进攻是军长下令的。那位少将的模样我能想见，虽说我当时并不晓得他是在「把责任推给军长」。而拉尔夫·威尔莫特（Ralph Wilmot）如今确确实实少了一条胳膊，于是我的反战愤懑便得以集中到一个事实上：他再也不能弹钢琴了。最后，几乎可以断定，我整个人体都万分庆幸自己能在一家英国医院里沉沉睡去。利他主义是一种时有时无、争议纷纷的品质；一个步兵醒着躺在床上胡思乱想时，自我保全的本能总归是要说最后一句话的。

---

===Original===
With an apology for my persistent specifyings of chronology, I must relate that on May 9th I was moved on to a Railway Terminus Hotel which had been commandeered for the accommodation of convalescent officers. My longing to get away from London made me intolerant of the Great Central Hotel, which was being directed by a mind more military than therapeutic. The Commandant was a noncombatant Brigadier-General, and the convalescents grumbled a good deal about his methods, although they could usually get leave to go out in the evenings. Many of them were waiting to be invalided out of the Army, and the daily routine-orders contained incongruous elements. We were required to attend lectures on, among other things, Trench Warfare. At my first lecture I was astonished to see several officers on crutches, with legs amputated, and at least one man had lost that necessary faculty for trench warfare, his eyesight. They appeared to be accepting the absurd situation stoically; they were allowed to smoke. The Staff Officer who was drawing diagrams on a blackboard was obviously desirous of imparting information about the lesson which had been learnt from the Battle of Neuve Chapelle or some equally obsolete engagement. But I noticed several faces in the audience which showed signs of tortured nerves, and it was unlikely that their efficiency was improved by the lecturer, who concluded by reminding us of the paramount importance of obtaining offensive ascendancy in No Man’s Land.

===Chinese===
为我屡屡絮叨日期一事先致歉意，但仍须交代：五月九日，我被转送到一家被征用作疗养军官住所的铁路终点饭店。我急于离开伦敦，这使我对大中央饭店（Great Central Hotel）难以忍受——那里由一种军事气重于疗养气的心思掌管着。所长是一位非战斗部队的准将，疗养员们对他的那一套规矩啧有烦言，虽说他们通常还能获准晚上外出。他们当中许多人正等着被勒令退役，日常的例行命令里掺杂着些格格不入的内容。要求我们去听讲，讲的是堑壕战之类。头一次讲座上，我惊讶地看见几位军官拄着拐杖、截了腿，至少有一位已经丧失了堑壕战所必不可少的那种能力——视力。他们似乎在斯多葛式地接受这种荒谬处境；获准可以抽烟。那位在黑板上画图的参谋军官，显然一心想把从讷沃夏佩勒（Neuve Chapelle）之役或某场同样过时的交战中得到的教训灌输给我们。但我注意到听众席上有几张脸，露出备受煎熬的神情，这位讲师的讲解未必能增进他们的作战效率——他讲到最后，还提醒我们：夺取「无人区」内的进攻优势，是至高无上的要务。

===Original===
In the afternoon I had an interview with the doctor who was empowered to decide how soon I went to the country. One of the men with whom I shared a room had warned me that this uniformed doctor was a queer customer. “The blighter seems to take a positive pleasure in tormenting people,” he remarked, adding, “He’ll probably tell you that you’ll have to stay here till you’re passed fit for duty.” But I had contrived to obtain a letter from the Countess of Somewhere, recommending me for one of the country houses in her Organization; so I felt fairly secure. (At that period of the War people with large houses received convalescent officers as guests.)

The doctor, a youngish man dressed as a temporary Captain, began by behaving quite pleasantly. After he’d examined me and the document which outlined my insignificant medical history, he asked what I proposed to do now. I said that I was hoping to get sent to some place in the country for a few weeks. He replied that I was totally mistaken if I thought any such thing. An expression, which I can only call cruel, overspread his face. “You’ll stay here; and when you leave here, you’ll find yourself back at the front in double-quick time.
